# REVIEW ARTICLE

# Concentration Inequalities for Statistical Inference

Huiming Zhang $^{1,3,4}$ , Song Xi Chen $^{1,2,3*}$

$^{1}$  School of Mathematical Sciences,  $^{2}$  Guanghua School of Management,  $^{3}$  Center for Statistical Sciences, Peking University, Beijing, P.R. China;  
$^{4}$  Department of Mathematics, Faculty of Science and Technology, University of Macau, Macau, China.

Abstract. This paper gives a review of concentration inequalities which are widely employed in non-asymptotical analyses of mathematical statistics in a wide range of settings, from distribution-free to distribution-dependent, from sub-Gaussian to subexponential, sub-Gamma, and sub-Weibull random variables, and from the mean to the maximum concentration. This review provides results in these settings with some fresh new results. Given the increasing popularity of high-dimensional data and inference, results in the context of high-dimensional linear and Poisson regressions are also provided. We aim to illustrate the concentration inequalities with known constants and to improve existing bounds with sharper constants.

Key Words: constants-specified inequalities, sub-Weibull random variables, heavy-tailed distributions, high-dimensional estimation and testing, finite-sample theory, random matrices.

AMS Subject Classifications: 60F10, 60G50, 62E17

# 1 Introduction

In probability theory and statistical inference, researchers often need to bound the probability of a difference between a random quantity from its target, usually the error bound of estimation. Concentration inequalities (CIs) are tools for attaining such bounds, and play important roles in deriving theoretical results for various inferential situations in statistics and probability. The recent developments in high-dimensional (HD) statistical inference, and statistical and machine learning have generated renewed interests in the CIs, as reflected in Koltchinskii (2011), Vershynin (2018), Wainwright (2019) and Fan et al. (2020). As the CIs are diverse in their forms and the underlying distributional requirements, and are scattered around in references, there is an increasing need for a review which collects existing results together with some new results (sharper and constants-specified CIs) from the authors for researchers and graduate students working in statistics and probability. This motivates the writing of this review.

CIs enable us to obtain non-asymptotic results for estimating, constructing confidence intervals, and doing hypothesis testing with a high-probability guarantee. For example,

the first-order optimized condition for HD linear regressions should be held with a high probability to guarantee the well-behavior of the estimator. The concentration inequality for error distributions is to ensure the concentration from first-order optimized conditions to the estimator. Our review focuses on four types of CIs:

$$
P \left(Z _ {n} > \mathrm {E} Z _ {n} + t\right), \quad P \left(Z _ {n} <   \mathrm {E} Z _ {n} - t\right), \quad P \left(\left| Z _ {n} - \mathrm {E} Z _ {n} \right| > t\right) \text {a n d} \mathrm {E} \left(\max  _ {i = 1, \dots , n} \left| X _ {i} \right|\right)
$$

where  $Z_{n} \coloneqq f(X_{1},\dots ,X_{n})$  and  $X_{1},\dots ,X_{n}$  are random variables. We present two types of CIs: distribution-free and distribution-dependent. Distribution free CIs are free of distribution assumptions, while the distribution-dependent CIs are based on exponential moment conditions reflecting the tail property for the particular class of distributions. Concentration phenomenons for a sum of sub-Weibull random variables will lead to a mixture of two tails: sub-Gaussian for small deviations and sub-Weibull for large deviations from the mean, and it is closely related to Strong Law of Large Numbers, Central Limit Theorem, and Law of the Iterative Logarithm. We provide applications of the CIs to empirical processes and high-dimensional data settings. The latter includes the linear and Poisson regression with a diverging number of covariates. We organize the materials in the forms of Lemmas, Corollaries, Propositions, and Theorems. Lemmas and Corollaries are on existing results usually without proof except for a few fundamental ones. Propositions are also for existing results but with sharper or more precise constants and sometimes come with proofs. Theorems are for new results. This review contains 27 Lemmas, 21 Corollaries, 15 Propositions, and 4 Theorems.

The review is organized as follows. Section 2 outlines distribution-free CIs. CIs for Sub-Gaussian, Sub-exponential, sub-Gamma, and sub-Weibull random variables are given in Section 3, 4, 5, and 6 respectively. Section 7 reports concentration for the maximal of random variables and suprema of empirical processes. Applications for high dimensional linear and Poisson regression are outlined in Section 8. Section 9 discusses extensions to other settings.

# 2 Distribution-free Concentration Bounds

The purpose here is to introduce distribution-free CIs. We first review Markov's, Chebyshev's and Chernoff's tail probability bounds that constitute fundamental inequalities for deriving most of the concentration bounds; see Chap. 1 of Durrett (2019) or Appendix B in Giraud (2014) for the proofs.

Lemma 2.1 (Markov's inequality). Let  $\varphi(x): \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}^+$  be any non-decreasing positive function. For any real valued random variable (r.v.)  $X$ ,  $P(X \geq a) \leq \operatorname{E}\left[\varphi(X)\right] \frac{1}{\varphi(a)}$ ,  $\forall a \in \mathbb{R}$ .

By letting  $\varphi(x) = x^2$ , the following Chebyshev's inequality is merely an application of Markov's inequality for  $|X - EX|$ .

Lemma 2.2 (Chebyshev's Inequality). Let  $X$  be a r.v. with expectation  $EX$  and variance  $\operatorname{Var} X$ . Then, for any  $a \in \mathbb{R}^+$ :  $P(|X - EX| \geq a) \leq \frac{\operatorname{Var} X}{a^2}$ .

The Chebyshev's inequality prescribes a polynomial rate of convergence depending on the variance assumption. Another application of Markov's inequality is the Chernoff's bound which is sharper by optimizing the upper bounds.

Lemma 2.3 (Chernoff's inequality). For a r.v.  $X$  with  $Ee^{tX} < \infty$ ,  $P(X \geq a) \leq \inf_{t > 0} \{ e^{-ta}Ee^{tX} \}$ .

Proof. Lemma 2.1 with  $\varphi(x) = e^{tx}$  implies  $P(X \geq a) \leq e^{-ta} \mathrm{E}e^{tX}$  and minimize  $t$  on  $t > 0$ .

The Jensen's inequality and its truncated version [Lemma 14.6 in Buhlmann and van de Geer (2011)] are another powerful tool to derive useful inequalities by the convexity.

Lemma 2.4 (Jensen's inequality). For any convex function  $\varphi: \mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}$  and any r.v.  $X$  in  $\mathbb{R}^d$ , such that  $\varphi(X)$  is integrable, we have  $\varphi(\mathrm{EX}) \leq \mathrm{E}[\varphi(X)]$ .

Lemma 2.5 (Truncated Jensen's inequality). Let  $g(\cdot)$  be an increasing function on  $[0, \infty)$ , which is concave on  $[c, \infty)$  for some  $c \geq 0$ . Then  $\operatorname{Eg}(|Z|) \leq g[\operatorname{E}|Z| + cP(|Z| < c)]$  for r.v.  $Z$ .

The Chebyshev's, Markov's, Chernoff's and Jensen's inequalities are also valid for conditional expectations (Chapter 4 of Durrett (2019)). The Chernoff's bound typically lead to a tighter bound than Markov's inequality by optimization via an exponential  $\varphi(x)$  function. A sharper bound for the sum of independent random variables(r.vs) was attempted in Hoeffding (1963). The following is a slightly sharper bound from Theorem 1.2 in Bosq (1998).

Corollary 2.1 (Hoeffding's inequality). Let  $X_1, \dots, X_n$  be independent r.vs on  $\mathbb{R}$  satisfying bound condition  $a_i \leq X_i \leq b_i$  for  $i = 1, 2, \dots, n$ . Then for  $t, u > 0$

(a) Hoeffding's lemma:  $\mathrm{E}e^{u\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i - \mathrm{EX}_i)} \leq e^{\frac{u^2}{8}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_i - a_i)^2}$  and  $\mathrm{E}e^{u|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i - \mathrm{EX}_i)|} \leq 2e^{\frac{u^2}{8}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_i - a_i)^2}$ ;  
(b) Hoeffding's inequality:  $P\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\mathrm{EX}_{i}\right)\right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 e^{-2t^{2} / \sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(b_{i}-a_{i}\right)^{2}}$ .

With exp-decay, Corollary 2.1 has a sharper bound than the Markov's inequality or Chebyshev's inequality with the requirement of first or moment condition on  $X$ . Hoeffding's inequality has many applications in statistics as shown in the next example.

The proof of Hoeffding's lemma. Without loss of generality, we assume  $\mathrm{EX}_i = 0$ . This is from the fact that the concentration inequality is location shift-invariance. Since  $f(x) = e^{x}$  is convex, for  $u > 0$ , then  $e^{ux} \leq \frac{b_i - x}{b_i - a_i} e^{ua_i} + \frac{x - a_i}{b_i - a_i} e^{ub_i}$ ,  $a_i \leq x \leq b_i$ . Taking expectation, it gives by  $\mathrm{EX}_i = 0$

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {u X _ {i}} \leq \frac {b _ {i}}{b _ {i} - a _ {i}} e ^ {u a _ {i}} - \frac {a _ {i}}{b _ {i} - a _ {i}} e ^ {u b _ {i}} = \left[ 1 - s + s e ^ {u \left(b _ {i} - a _ {i}\right)} \right] e ^ {- s u \left(b _ {i} - a _ {i}\right) \triangleq} e ^ {f (r)}, \tag {2.1}
$$

where  $r = u(b_i - a_i), s = -a_i / (b_i - a_i)$  and  $f(r) = -sr + \log(1 - s + se^r)$ . We can show that  $f'(r) = -s + \frac{se^r}{1 - s + se^r}$ ,  $f''(r) = \frac{(1 - s)se^r}{(1 - s + se^r)^2} \leq \frac{1}{4}$  for all  $r \geq 0$ . Note that  $f(0) = f'(0) = 0$ . Consider

the Taylor's expansion of  $f$ , there exists  $\xi \in [0,1]$  such that  $f(r) = r^2 f''(\xi r) / 2 \leq r^2 / 8 = u^2 (b_i - a_i)^2 / 8$ . Substitute it to (2.1), we get the Hoeffding's lemma.

The last assertion of Lemma 2.1(a) is by letting  $Z = u\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i - \mathrm{E}X_i)$ , so that

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {| Z |} = \mathrm {E} e ^ {- Z} \cdot 1 (Z \leq 0) + \mathrm {E} e ^ {Z} \cdot 1 (Z > 0) \leq 2 e ^ {\frac {1}{8} u ^ {2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(b _ {i} - a _ {i}\right) ^ {2}}. \tag {2.2}
$$

The proof of Hoeffding's inequality. Let  $S_{n} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} X_{i}$  and  $c_{i} = a_{i} - b_{i}$ . For any  $t, u > 0$ ,

$$
P \left(S _ {n} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n} \geq t\right) = P \left(e ^ {u \left(S _ {n} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n}\right)} \geq e ^ {u t}\right) \leq \inf  _ {u > 0} e ^ {- u t} \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} e ^ {u \left(X _ {i} - \mathrm {E} X _ {i}\right)} [ \text {C h e r n o f f ' s i n e q u a l i t y} ]
$$

[Hoeffding's lemma]  $\leq \inf_{u > 0}e^{-ut}\prod_{i = 1}^{n}e^{u^2 c_i^2 /8} = \inf_{u > 0}e^{-ut + u^2\sum_{i = 1}^{n}c_i^2 /8} = e^{-2t^2 /\sum_{i = 1}^{n}c_i^2}.$  (2.3)

The smallest bounded is attained at  $u = 4t / \sum_{i=1}^{n} c_i^2$  and  $P\left(-[S_n - \mathrm{ES}_n] \geq t\right) \leq e^{-2t^2 / \sum_{i=1}^{n} c_i^2}$  similarly. Hence, the Hoeffding's inequality is verified via

$$
P \left(\left| S _ {n} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n} \right| \geq t\right) \leq P \left(S _ {n} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n} \geq t\right) + P \left(- \left[ S _ {n} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n} \right] \geq t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- 2 t ^ {2} / \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} c _ {i} ^ {2}}.
$$

Corollary 2.1 has a sharper bound than the Markov's inequality or Chebyshev's inequality with the requirement of first or moment condition on  $X$ . A second approach for proving Hoeffding's lemma is given in Lemma 1.8 of Rigollet and Hütter (2023). Hoeffding's inequality has many applications in statistics as shown in the next example.

Example 2.1 (Empirical distribution function, EDF). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n \stackrel{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim} F(x)$  for a distribution  $F$ . Let  $\mathbb{F}_n(x) := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n 1_{\{X_i \leq x\}}(x)$ ,  $x \in \mathbb{R}$  be the empirical distribution. By Hoeffding's inequality  $(a_i - b_i = 1/n)$ ,  $P(|\mathbb{F}_n(x) - F(x)| > \varepsilon) \leq 2e^{-2n\varepsilon^2}$ ,  $\forall \varepsilon > 0$ .

McDiarmid's inequality (also called bounded difference inequality, see McDiarmid (1989)) is a concentration inequality for a multivariate function of random sequence  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$ , says  $f(X_1, \ldots, X_n)$ . As a generalization of Hoeffding's inequality, it does not require any distribution assumptions about r.vs and the  $f(X_1, \ldots, X_n)$  may be dependent sum of r.vs. The only requirement is the bounded difference condition by replacing  $X_j$  by  $X_j'$  while maintaining the others fixed in  $f(X_1, \ldots, X_n)$ .

Lemma 2.6 (McDiarmid's inequality). Suppose  $X_1, \dots, X_n$  are independent r.vs all taking values in the set  $A$ , and assume  $f: A^n \to \mathbb{R}$  satisfies the bounded difference condition

$$
\sup _ {x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}, x _ {k} ^ {\prime} \in A} | f (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}) - f (x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k} ^ {\prime}, x _ {k + 1}, \dots , x _ {n}) | \leq c _ {k}.
$$

Then,  $P\left(|f(X_1,\dots ,X_n) - \operatorname {E}\{f(X_1,\dots ,X_n)\} |\geq t\right)\leq 2e^{-2t^2 /\sum_{i = 1}^n c_i^2}\forall t > 0.$

If  $f(x_{1},\dots ,x_{n}) = \sum_{i = 1}^{n}x_{i}$  then  $c_{k} = x_{k} - x_{k}^{\prime}$ . Let  $\mathcal{B}(c)$  be the set of functions  $f:\mathcal{X}^n\to \mathbb{R}$  s.t., for any  $x = (x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})$  and  $y = (y_{1},\ldots ,y_{n})$  in  $\mathcal{X}^n$ ,  $|f(x) - f(y)|\leq \sum_{i = 1}^{n}c_{i}1_{\{x_{i}\neq y_{i}\}}$ , i.e. The BDC is a Lipschitz property of  $f$  with respect to the Hamming distance.

One method of proof is by the martingale argument, which needs to check the Azuma-Hoeffding's inequality below, see Section 2.2.2 in Wainwright (2019). Theorem 3.3.14 of Giné and Nickl (2016) gives another proof based on the entropy method.

Lemma 2.7 (Azuma-Hoeffding's inequality). Let  $\{X_{n}\}_{n = 0}^{\infty}$  be a sequence of martingale (or supermartingale), adapted to an increasing filtration  $\{\mathcal{F}_n\}_{n = 0}^{\infty}$ . Suppose  $\{X_{n}\}_{n = 0}^{\infty}$  satisfies the bounded difference condition  $a_{k}\leq X_{k} - X_{k - 1}\leq b_{k}$ , a.s. for  $k = 1,\ldots ,n$ . Then,

$$
P \left(\left| X _ {n} - X _ {0} \right| > t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- 2 t ^ {2} / \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(b _ {k} - a _ {k}\right) ^ {2}}, t \geq 0.
$$

Three typical examples with bounded differences function are the concentration for U-statistics (a dependent summation) and the integral error of the kernel density estimation, and concentration of bounded empirical processes.

Example 2.2 (U-statistics). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent and identically distributed (IID) r.vs and  $g: \mathbb{R}^2 \to \mathbb{R}$  be the bounded and symmetric function. Define a U-statistic of order 2 as  $U_n = \left( \begin{array}{cc} n & \\ 2 & \end{array} \right)^{-1} \sum_{i < j} g(X_i, X_j) := f(x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ . Its bounded difference condition is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| f (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k}, x _ {k + 1}, \ldots x _ {n}) - f (x _ {1}, \ldots , x _ {k - 1}, x _ {k} ^ {\prime}, x _ {k + 1}, \ldots x _ {n}) \right| \\ = \frac {1}{\left( \begin{array}{c} n \\ 2 \end{array} \right)} | \sum_ {j = 1, j \neq k} ^ {n} [ g (x _ {k}, x _ {j}) - g (x _ {k} ^ {\prime}, x _ {j}) ] | \leq \frac {2 \cdot 2 (n - 1) \| g \| _ {\infty}}{n (n - 1)} = \frac {4 \| g \| _ {\infty}}{n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

So we have  $P(|U_n - \mathrm{EU}_n| > t) \leq 2e^{-nt^2 / 8\| g\|_\infty^2}$ .

Example 2.3 ( $L_{1}$ -error in kernel density estimation). Let  $\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n} \stackrel{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim} F(x)$  with density function  $f(x)$ . Define the kernel density estimator by  $\hat{f}_{n,h}(x) = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{h} K\left(\frac{x - X_{i}}{h}\right)$  where  $K(\cdot) > 1$  is the kernel function and  $h > 0$  is a smoothing parameter called the bandwidth. Usually, the kernel function  $K(\cdot)$  is symmetric probability density and  $h > 0$  with  $h \to 0$  and  $nh \to \infty$ . Define the  $L_{1}$ -error of  $\hat{f}_{n,h}(x)$  by  $Z_{n} = g(X_{1}, \ldots, X_{n}) = \int |\hat{f}_{n,h}(x) - f(x)| dx$ . By  $\int K(u) du = 1$ , the McDiarmid's inequality with bound difference condition

$$
\left| g \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) - g \left(x _ {1}, \dots , x _ {i} ^ {\prime}, \dots , x _ {n}\right) \right| \leq \frac {1}{n} \int \left| K \left(\frac {x - x _ {i}}{h}\right) - K \left(\frac {x - x _ {i} ^ {\prime}}{h}\right) \right| d \left(\frac {x}{h}\right) \leq \frac {2}{n}
$$

gives  $P(|Z_n - \mathrm{E}Z_n| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-2t^2 / n\left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^2} = 2e^{-nt^2 / 2}$ , which is free of the bandwidth.

The McDiarmid's inequality is particularly useful when deriving CIs for supremum of empirical processes:  $f(X) \coloneqq \sup_{f \in \mathcal{F}} \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} f(X_i)$  for independent data  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$ , see Section 7.2 for more details.

Example 2.4 (The supremum of bounded EP). Given  $f \in \mathcal{F}$ , WLOG, assume that

$$
\forall f \in \mathcal {F}, \quad \mathrm {E} [ f (X _ {i}) ] = 0, \quad \text {a n d} \quad f (X _ {i}) \in [ a _ {i}, b _ {i} ]
$$

For any  $x = (x_{1},\ldots ,x_{n})\in \mathcal{X}^{n}$  , define  $f(x) = \sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}f(x_{i})$

$$
\left| \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (x _ {i}) - \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (y _ {i}) \right| \leq \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(f (x _ {i}) - f (y _ {i})\right) \right| \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {b _ {i} - a _ {i}}{n} 1 _ {\left\{x _ {i} \neq y _ {i} \right\}}.
$$

It is clear that  $f \in \mathcal{B}(\mathbf{c})$ , with  $c_{i} = (b_{i} - a_{i}) / n$ . Therefore, the BDC implies  $\forall M \geq 0$

$$
P (\sup _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (X _ {i}) \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \mathrm {E} [ \sup _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (X _ {i}) ] + \frac {M}{\sqrt {n}}) \geq 1 - e ^ {- 2 M ^ {2} / [ \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (b _ {i} - a _ {i}) ^ {2} ]}.
$$

# 3 Sub-Gaussian Distributions

# 3.1 Motivations

In probability, there is a well-known inequality for bounding the Gaussian tail. If  $X \sim N(0,1)$ , Gordon (1941) obtained for  $x > 0$

$$
\left(\frac {1}{x} - \frac {1}{x ^ {3}}\right) \cdot \frac {e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} <   \left(\frac {x}{x ^ {2} + 1}\right) \cdot \frac {e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \leq P (X \geq x) \leq \frac {1}{x} \cdot \frac {e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}}, \tag {3.1}
$$

which is called Mills's inequality, relating to Mills's ratio (Mills, 1926). The upper bound in (3.1) is mostly used to derive law of the iterated logarithm (Durrett, 2019). However, if  $x$  tends to zero the upper bound goes to  $+\infty$  which makes it meaningless. So the Mill's inequality is useful only for larger  $x$ . We need a better inequality. In fact, the upper bound in (3.1) can be strengthened as in Lemma B.3 in Giraud (2014):  $P(|X| \geq x) \leq e^{-x^2 / 2}$ . We refer it as the sharper Mill's inequality.

Example 3.1  $(O(a^{-2})$ -decay tail inequality is not enough).  $\{X_{ij}\} \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} N(0, \sigma^2)$  for  $i = 1, \dots, n$  and  $j = 1, \dots, p_n$  with  $p_n \gg n \to \infty$ . Note that  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} X_{ij} \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} N(0, n\sigma^2)$ . By Chebyshev's inequality,

$$
T _ {n} := P \left(\max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} | \geq t \sqrt {n}\right) \leq \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} P \left(| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} | \geq t \sqrt {n}\right) \leq \frac {p n \sigma^ {2}}{n t ^ {2}} = \frac {p \sigma^ {2}}{t ^ {2}}.
$$

Sharper Mill's inequality gives

$$
T _ {n} \leq \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} \right| \geq t \sqrt {n}\right) = p e ^ {- (t \sqrt {n}) ^ {2} / (2 n \sigma^ {2})} = p e ^ {- t ^ {2} / (2 \sigma^ {2})}.
$$

Put  $t = \sqrt{p}$  and letting  $p = p_n \to \infty$ , Chebyshev's inequality derives a upper bound of  $T_n \leq p\sigma^2 / t^2 = \sigma^2 \neq 0$ . However, sharper Mill's inequality guarantees  $T_n \leq pe^{-p^2 / (2\sigma^2)} \to 0$ .

In statistics, people want to study a general class of error distributions (beyond Gaussian) whose moment generating function (MGF):  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX}$  have similar Gaussian properties with  $s$  in specific subset of  $\mathbb{R}$ . To derive sharper Mill's inequality, it is natural to define the class of sub-Gaussian r.v. as follows.

Definition 3.1 (Sub-Gaussian distribution, Kahane (1960)). A r.v.  $X \in \mathbb{R}$  with mean zero is sub-Gaussian with a variance proxy  $\sigma^2$  (denoted  $X \sim \mathrm{subG}(\sigma^2)$ ) if its MGF satisfies  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{\frac{\sigma^2s^2}{2}}$ ,  $\forall s \in \mathbb{R}$ .

The minimal  $\sigma^2$  is called the optimal variance proxy (Chow, 1966)

$$
\sigma_ {o p t} ^ {2} (X) := \inf  \left\{\sigma^ {2} > 0: \mathbb {E} \exp (t X) \leq \exp \left\{\sigma^ {2} t ^ {2} / 2 \right\}, \quad \forall t \in \mathbb {R} \right\}.
$$

With Definition 3.1 and Chernoff's inequality, we will get the exponential decay of the tail as the alternative definition of sub-Gaussian:

$$
P (X \geq t) \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} e ^ {- s t} \mathrm {E} e ^ {s X} \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} e ^ {- s t + \frac {\sigma^ {2} s ^ {2}}{2}} \stackrel {{s = t / \sigma^ {2}}} {{=}} e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 \sigma^ {2}}}.
$$

This argument is called Cramer-Chernoff method, and it is applied in proving Hoeffding's lemma for sum of independent variables. In general, let  $Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}$  be  $n$  independent centralized r.vs, and suppose there exists a convex function  $g(t)$  and a domain  $D_{0}$  containing  $\{0\}$  such that  $\mathrm{Ee}^{t\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}} \leq e^{ng(t)}$ ,  $\forall t \in D_{0} \subset \mathbb{R}$ . Denote  $g^{*}(s) = \sup_{t \in D_{0}}\{ts - g(t)\}$  as the convex conjugate function of  $g$ , therefore the Chernoff's inequality implies

$$
P \big (| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} Z _ {i} | > s \big) \leq 2 e ^ {- n g ^ {*} (s)}, \forall s > 0,
$$

which has rich applications in high-dimensional statistics, machine learning, random matrix theory, and other fields on non-asymptotic results.

Note that  $\mathrm{subG}(\sigma^2)$  denotes a class of distributions rather than a single distribution. Trivially, the Gaussian distribution is a special case of sub-Gaussian.

Example 3.2 (Normal distributions). Consider the normal r.v.  $X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2)$ . With the MGF of  $X$ :  $\operatorname{E} e^{sX} \coloneqq e^{\frac{\sigma^2 s^2}{2}}$ ,  $\forall s \in \mathbb{R}$ , it is sub-Gaussian with the variance proxy  $\sigma^2 = \operatorname{Var}(X)$ .

Example 3.3 (Bounded r.vs). By Hoeffding's lemma,  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{\frac{1}{8}s^2(b - a)^2}$  for  $s > 0$  for the centralized bounded variable  $X \in [a,b]$ . So  $X$  is essentially sub-Gaussian with variance proxy  $\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{4}(b - a)^2$ . For Bernoulli variable  $X \in \{0,1\}$ , we have  $X \sim \mathrm{subG}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)$ .

There are at least seven equivalent forms for sub-Gaussian as shown in the following.

Corollary 3.1 (Characterizations of sub-Gaussian). Let  $X$  be a r.v. in  $\mathbb{R}$  with  $\mathrm{EX} = 0$ . Then, the following are equivalent for finite positive constants  $\{K_i\}_{i=1}^7$ .

(1) The MGF of  $X$ :  $\operatorname{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{K_1^2 s^2}$  for all  $s \in \mathbb{R}$ ;  
(2) The tail of  $X$ :  $P\{|X| \geq t\} \leq 2e^{-t^2 / K_2^2}$  for all  $t \geq 0$ ;  
(3) The moments of  $X$ :  $(\operatorname{E}|X|^k)^{1/k} \leq K_3\sqrt{k}$  for all integer  $k \geq 1$ ;  
(4) The exponential moment of  $X^2: \mathrm{Ee}^{X^2 / K_4^2} \leq 2$ ;  
(5) The local MGF of  $X^2$ :  $\operatorname{Ee}^{l^2 X^2} \leq e^{K_5^2 l^2}$  for all  $l$  in a local set  $|l| \leq \frac{1}{K_5}$ .  
(6) There is a constant  $K_{6} \geq 0$  such that  $\mathrm{E}e^{\lambda X^{2} / K_{6}^{2}} \leq (1 - \lambda)^{-1/2}$  for all  $\lambda \in [0,1)$ .

Remark 3.1. The  $\mathrm{EX} = 0$  is for convenience as the zero mean is used in the proof of Corollary 3.1(1), see Vershynin (2012) for the details and the proof of the equivalences (1)-(5). The equivalences (6) is given in Theorem 2.6 of Wainwright (2019)The moment condition for integers  $k$  in (3) can be relaxed to even integers  $k$  by the symmetrization

technique. By symmetry of  $X$ , let us consider a negative independent copy  $-X'$  which is independent of  $X$  and has the same distribution as  $X$ . If (3) is true and  $\operatorname{E}(-X') = 0$ , from Jensen's inequality  $\operatorname{E}e^{\theta(-X')} \geq e^{\theta\operatorname{E}(-X')} = 1$  since  $-X'$  has zero mean. So we have by the independence of  $X'$  and  $X$ :

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\theta X} \leq \mathrm {E} e ^ {\theta X} \mathrm {E} e ^ {\theta (- X ^ {\prime})} = \mathrm {E} e ^ {\theta (X - X ^ {\prime})} = 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {\theta^ {2 k} \mathrm {E} (X - X ^ {\prime}) ^ {2 k}}{(2 k) !} \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {\theta^ {2 k} \mathrm {E} (| X | + | X ^ {\prime} |) ^ {2 k}}{(2 k) !}
$$

$$
[ \mathrm {B y} (3. 7) ] \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {\theta^ {2 k} 2 ^ {2 k} \mathrm {E} | X | ^ {2 k}}{(2 k) !} <   1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {(2 \theta K _ {3} \sqrt {2 k}) ^ {2 k}}{k ^ {k} k !} = 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {(8 \theta^ {2} K _ {3} ^ {2}) ^ {k}}{k !} = e ^ {8 \theta^ {2} K _ {3} ^ {2}} \forall \theta \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

where the last inequality is due to  $(2k)! > k^{k}\cdot k!$

# 3.2 The variance proxy and sub-Gaussian norm

We show that the  $\sigma^2$  in Definition 3.1 is indeed the upper bounds of variance of  $X$ :

$$
\operatorname {V a r} X \leq \sigma^ {2}.
$$

The  $\sigma^2$  not only characterizes the speed of decay in the sub-Gaussian tail probability, but also bounds the variance of  $n^{-1/2} \sum_{i=1}^{n} X_i$ . This is because, by the sub-Gaussian MGF

$$
\frac {\sigma^ {2} s ^ {2}}{2} + o \left(s ^ {2}\right) = e ^ {\frac {v ^ {2} s ^ {2}}{2}} - 1 \geq \operatorname {E} e ^ {s X} - 1 = s \operatorname {E} X + \frac {s ^ {2}}{2} \operatorname {E} X ^ {2} + \dots = \frac {s ^ {2}}{2} \cdot \operatorname {V a r} X + o \left(s ^ {2}\right) \tag {3.2}
$$

Dividing  $s^2$  on both sides of (3.2) and taking  $s \to 0$ .

In the theory of empirical process, sub-Gaussian definitions are characterized by Orlicz norms (see Definition 6.3 below and van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)) to derive exponential tail inequality for empirical process.

Definition 3.2 (Sub-Gaussian norm). For a sub-Gaussian r.v.  $X$ , the sub-Gaussian norm of  $X$ , denoted  $\|X\|_{\psi_2}$ , is defined by:  $\|X\|_{\psi_2} = \inf \{t > 0 : \operatorname{Ee}^{X^2 / t^2} \leq 2\}$ .

From Corollary 3.1(4),  $\| X \|_{\psi_2}$  is the smallest  $K_4$ . An alternative definition of the sub-Gaussian norm is  $\| X \|_{\psi_2} \coloneqq \sup_{p \geq 1} p^{-1/2} (E|X|^p)^{1/p}$  (Vershynin, 2012). The definition for sub-Gaussian norm makes Corollary 3.1 easily presented. In fact, if  $Ee^{X^2 / \| X \|_{\psi_2}^2} \leq 2$ ,

$$
P (| X | \geq t) = P \left(e ^ {X ^ {2} / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}} \geq e ^ {t ^ {2} / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}}\right) \leq \mathrm {E} e ^ {X ^ {2} / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}} / e ^ {t ^ {2} / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}} \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}}. \tag {3.3}
$$

Example 3.4 (The sub-Gaussian norm of bounded r.vs.). Consider a r.v.  $|X| \leq M < \infty$ . Set  $\mathrm{E}e^{X^2 / t^2} \leq e^{M^2 / t^2} \leq 2$  and  $t \geq M / \sqrt{\log 2}$ . By the definition of the sub-Gaussian norm, we have  $\| X \|_{\psi_2} = M / \sqrt{\log 2}$ . The equality holds if  $|X| = M < \infty$ .

Example 3.5 (The sub-Gaussian norm of Gaussian r.vs.). For a  $N(0,\sigma^2)$  and  $t > \sqrt{2}\sigma$ ,  $\mathrm{E}e^{X^2 /t^2} = \int e^{x^2 /t^2}\frac{e^{-x^2 / 2\sigma^2}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} dx = \frac{t}{(t^2 - 2\sigma^2)^{1 / 2}}\int e^{x^2 /t^2}\left(2\pi \frac{\sigma^2t^2}{t^2 - 2\sigma^2}\right)^{-1 / 2}\exp \left\{-\frac{x^2}{2(\frac{\sigma^2t^2}{t^2 - 2\sigma^2})}\right\} dx = \frac{t}{(t^2 - 2\sigma^2)^{1 / 2}}\leq$ $2\Rightarrow t\geq \sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sigma$  . By the definition,  $\| X\|_{\psi_2} = \sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sigma >\sqrt{2}\sigma$

However, the neat notation for defining sub-Gaussian norm sometime leads to unknown constants in the CIs as shown next.

Corollary 3.2 (Theorem 2.6.3, Vershynin (2018)). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent mean-zero sub-Gaussian,  $\forall t \geq 0$ ,  $P\{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\right| \geq t\} \leq 2e^{-C(nt)^2 / \sum_{i=1}^n \|X_i\|_{\psi_2}^2}, \forall t \geq 0$  for a constant  $C$ .

The unknown constant  $C$  makes the above CIs cannot be used in constructing confidence bands for  $\mu$ . To obtain more specific bounds (data dependent bounds as a statistics), we adopt the follow three propositions under sub-Gaussian.

Proposition 3.1 (Sub-Gaussian properties). Let  $X \sim \mathrm{subG}(\sigma^2)$ , then for any  $t > 0$ ,

(a) the tail satisfies  $P(|X| > t)\leq 2e^{-t^2 /2\sigma^2}$  
(b) (a) implies that moments  $\operatorname{E}|X|^k \leq (2\sigma^2)^{k/2} k \Gamma\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)$  and  $(\operatorname{E}(|X|^k))^{1/k} \leq \sigma e^{1/e} \sqrt{k}, k \geq 2$ ;  
(c) If (a) holds and  $\mathrm{EX} = 0$ , then  $\mathrm{Ee}^{sX} \leq e^{4\sigma^2 s^2}$  for any  $s > 0$ ;  
(d) If  $X \sim \operatorname{subG}(\sigma^2)$ , then  $\|X\|_{\psi_2} \leq \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\log 2}}\sigma$ ; conversely, if  $\|X\|_{\psi_2} = \sigma$  then  $X \sim \operatorname{subG}(4\sigma^2)$ .

Proof. The proofs of (a)-(c) are in Lemma 1.4 and 1.5 in Rigollet and Hütter (2023)). The proofs of (a, b) is similar to Proposition 3.2(a, b) below. For (d), note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E} \exp \left(s ^ {2} X ^ {2}\right) = 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {2 k} \operatorname {E} X ^ {2 k}}{k !} \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} 1 + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \frac {2 s ^ {2 k} (2 \sigma^ {2}) ^ {k} k \Gamma (k)}{k !} = 1 + 4 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {\infty} (2 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2}) ^ {k} \\ \xlongequal {\forall | 2 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} | <   1} 1 + \frac {4 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2}}{1 - 2 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2}} \stackrel {\forall | s | \leq 1 / (2 \sigma)} {\leq} 1 + 8 s ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} \leq e ^ {8 \sigma^ {2} s ^ {2}}. \tag {3.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

By (3.4), set  $\mathrm{E}e^{s_0^2 X^2} \leq e^{8s_0^2\sigma^2} \leq 2$  for some  $s_0$ . Then  $s_0^2 \leq \frac{\log 2}{8\sigma^2}$  and  $|s_0| \leq \frac{\sqrt{\log 2}}{2\sqrt{2}\sigma} \leq \frac{1}{2\sigma}$ . Put  $|s_0| = \frac{\sqrt{\log 2}}{2\sqrt{2}\sigma}$  and the sub-Gaussian norm gives  $\mathrm{E}e^{X^2 / (\frac{2\sqrt{2}\sigma}{\sqrt{\log 2}})^2} \leq 2 \Rightarrow \| X \|_{\varphi_2} \leq \frac{2\sqrt{2}\sigma}{\sqrt{\log 2}}$ .

Conversely, if  $\| X\|_{\psi_2} = \sigma$  then (3.3) gives

$$
P (| X | > t) \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / \sigma^ {2}} = 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 (\sigma / \sqrt {2}) ^ {2}}}.
$$

Then Proposition 3.1(c) concludes  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{4(\sigma/\sqrt{2})^2 s^2} = e^{4\sigma^2 s^2/2}$  for any  $s > 0$ , and we have  $X \sim \operatorname{subG}(4\sigma^2)$ .

Let  $\{Y_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  be a sequence of exponential family (EF) r.vs, its density

$$
f \left(y _ {i}; \theta_ {i}\right) = c \left(y _ {i}\right) \exp \left\{y _ {i} \theta_ {i} - b \left(\theta_ {i}\right) \right\} \tag {3.5}
$$

with  $\mathrm{E}Y_{i} = \dot{b}(\theta_{i})$  and  $\mathrm{Var}Y_{i} = \ddot{b}(\theta_{i})$ . We next introduce the sub-Gaussian CIs for the nonrandom weighted sum of EF r.vs with compact parameter space, adapted from Lemma 6.1 in Rigollet (2012) with more specific constants.

Proposition 3.2 (Concentration for weighted E-F summation). We assume (3.5) and

- (E.1): Uniformly bounded variances condition: there exist a compact set  $\Omega$  and some constant  $C_b$  such that  $\sup_{\theta_i \in \Omega} \ddot{b}(\theta_i) \leq C_b^2$  for all  $i$ .

Let  $\boldsymbol{w} := (w_1, \dots, w_n)^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$  be a non-random vector and define  $S_n^w := \sum_{i=1}^n w_i Y_i$ . Then

(a) Closed under addition:  $S_{n}^{w} - \mathrm{ES}_{n}^{w}\sim \mathrm{subG}(C_{b}^{2}\| \boldsymbol {w}\|_{2}^{2})$  and  $P\{|S_n^w -\mathrm{ES}_n^w | > t\} \leq 2e^{-t^2 /(2C_b^2\| w\|_2^2)}$  
(b) Let  $C_n \coloneqq S_n^w - \operatorname{ES}_n^w$  and  $\Gamma(t) \coloneqq \int_0^\infty x^{t-1} e^{-x} dx$  be the Gamma function. For all integer  $k \geq 1$ , we have moments bound:  $\mathbb{E}|C_n|^k \leq k(2C_b^2)^{k/2} \Gamma(k/2) \| \boldsymbol{w} \|_2^k$ .  
(c) The MGF of centralized  $|C_n|^2$ :  $\operatorname{E} e^{s[|C_n|^2 - \operatorname{E}|C_n|^2]} \leq e^{s^2 (8\sqrt{2} C_b^2 \| w \|_2^2)^2 / 2}, \forall |s| \leq (8C_b^2 \| w \|_2^2)^{-1}$ .  
(d) In this case, we do not assume (3.5) and (E.1). Suppose  $\{Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are independent distributed as  $\{\mathrm{subG}(\sigma_i^2)\}_{i=1}^n$  with  $C_b^2 \eqqcolon \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \sigma_i^2 > 0$ , then (a) - (c) also hold.

Proof. Based on the MGF and uniformly bounded variances condition, the proof of (a) can be found in Lemma 6.1 of Rigollet (2012). In the proof of (c), we update the constant, and (d) is similar for the sub-Gaussian case.

(b) The proof relies on expectation formula for positive r.v. (in terms of integral of tail probability) which transforms tail bound to moment bound. For any integer  $k \geq 1$ ,

$$
\left. \operatorname {E} \left| S _ {n} ^ {w} - \operatorname {E} S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| ^ {k} = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} P \left(\left| S _ {n} ^ {w} - \operatorname {E} S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| ^ {k} \geq s\right) d s \xlongequal {t = s ^ {1 / k}} \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} k t ^ {k - 1} P \left(\left| S _ {n} ^ {w} - \operatorname {E} S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| \geq t\right) d t. \right. \tag {3.6}
$$

Applying tail bound in (a), we have by letting  $D_{k,C} = k(2C_b^2)^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2)$

$$
\operatorname {E} \left| S _ {n} ^ {w} - \operatorname {E} S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| ^ {k} \leq 2 k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} t ^ {k - 1} e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}}} d t \xlongequal {x = t ^ {2} / 2 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}} k (2 C _ {b} ^ {2}) ^ {\frac {k}{2}} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {k} \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} x ^ {\frac {k}{2} - 1} e ^ {- x} d x = D _ {k, C} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {k}.
$$

(c) The proof will resort to  $(\operatorname{E}|Z|)^k \leq \operatorname{E}|Z|^k$  and Jensen's inequality

$$
\left(\frac {| a | + | b |}{2}\right) ^ {k} \leq \frac {1}{2} | a | ^ {k} + \frac {1}{2} | b | ^ {k}, \text {f o r i n t e g e r} k \geq 1. \tag {3.7}
$$

From Taylor's expansion, (3.7) gives

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\mathrm {s} [ | C _ {n} | ^ {2} - \mathrm {E} | C _ {n} | ^ {2} ]} = 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {k} \mathrm {E} [ | C _ {n} | ^ {2} - \mathrm {E} | C _ {n} | ^ {2} ] ^ {k}}{k !} \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {k} 2 ^ {k - 1} \mathrm {E} \left\{\left| C _ {n} \right| ^ {2 k} + \left(\mathrm {E} \left| C _ {n} \right| ^ {2}\right) ^ {k} \right\}}{k !}
$$

$$
\left[ \mathrm {B y} \left(\mathrm {E} | Z |\right) ^ {k} \leq \mathrm {E} (| Z | ^ {k}) \right] \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {k} 2 ^ {k - 1} \mathrm {E} \left\{| C _ {n} | ^ {2 k} + \mathrm {E} | C _ {n} | ^ {2 k} \right\}}{k !} \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {k} 2 ^ {k} \cdot 2 k (2 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}) ^ {k} \Gamma (k)}{k !},
$$

where the last inequality is by Proposition 3.2(b). Then, under  $|4sC_b^2\| \boldsymbol{w}\|_2^2| < 1$ , we have

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s \left[ | C _ {n} | ^ {2} - \mathrm {E} | C _ {n} | ^ {2} \right]} = 1 + 2 \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \left(4 s C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) ^ {k} = 1 + \frac {2 \left(4 s C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) ^ {2}}{1 - 4 | s | C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}
$$

$$
[ | 4 s C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} | \leq \frac {1}{2} \Leftrightarrow | s | \leq \frac {1}{8 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} ] \leq 1 + \frac {s ^ {2} (8 \sqrt {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}) ^ {2}}{2} \leq \mathrm {e} ^ {s ^ {2} (8 \sqrt {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2}) ^ {2} / 2}.
$$

(d) It follows by defining  $C_b^2 \coloneqq \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \sigma_i^2 > 0$  as the common variance proxy for  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$ . For  $i = 1, 2, \dots, n$ , we have:  $Ee^{s w_i(Y_i - \mathrm{E} Y_i)} \leq e^{\sigma^2 s^2 w_i^2 / 2}, \forall s \in \mathbb{R}$ .

Proposition 3.2(a) yields the following results (The first result is in Corollary 1.7 of Rigollet and Hütter (2023)). The second sub-Gaussian CI below specifies the unknown constant in Theorem 2.6.2 of Vershynin (2018).

Proposition 3.3. Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be  $n$  independent subG( $\sigma_i^2$ ). Define  $\sigma^2 = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \sigma_i^2$ ,

$$
P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \right| > t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / \left(2 \sigma^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right)} a n d P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \right| > t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / \left(8 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| w _ {i} X _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2}\right)}, \forall t \geq 0
$$

for any non-random vector  $\pmb{w} \coloneqq (w_1, \dots, w_n)^T$ .

Proof. To see the second CI, just use the Proposition 3.1(d) and the Proposition 3.2(d), by noticing that if  $\| X_i\|_{\psi_2} < \infty$  then  $w_{i}X_{i}\sim \mathrm{subG}(4\| w_{i}X_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2})$ .

# 3.3 Randomly weighted sum of independent sub-Gaussian variables

In this part, we outline the sub-Gaussian type CIs for the randomly weighted sum of exponential family r.vs:  $S_{n}^{W} \coloneqq \sum_{i=1}^{n} W_{i} Y_{i}$  where  $\{W_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$  are called the multipliers (or random weights) which are independent from  $\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$ . The normalized sum  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(S_{n}^{W} - E S_{n}^{W})$  is also called multiplier empirical processes, and it serves for the multiplier Bootstrap inference where the multipliers  $\{W_{i}\}$  are r.vs independent from  $\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$ , see Chapter 2.9 of van der Vaart and Wellner (1996). To get sub-Gaussian concentration, some regularity conditions for the parameter space are required.

- (E.2): Let  $\boldsymbol{W} \coloneqq (W_1, \dots, W_n)^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$  be a random vector with some bounded components, i.e.,  $|W_i| \leq w_i < \infty$  for a non-random vector  $\boldsymbol{w} \coloneqq (w_1, \dots, w_n)^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$ .

Theorem 3.1 (Concentration inequalities for randomly weighted sum). Let  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  belong to the canonical exponential family (3.5), and let  $\{W_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent of  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$ . Define the randomly weighted sum  $S_n^W =: \sum_{i=1}^n W_i Y_i$ , then under (E.1) and (E.2)

$$
P \big (| S _ {n} ^ {W} - \mathrm {E} S _ {n} ^ {W} | \geq t \big) \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / (2 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2})}.
$$

Proof. Let  $Y_{i} = \dot{b}(\theta_{i}) + Z_{i}$ , where  $\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$  are centralized and independent E-F r.vs. From  $\mathrm{E}Y_{i} = \dot{b}(\theta_{i})$  and the identity (3.8) for a dominating measure  $\mu(\cdot)$

$$
\int d F _ {Y _ {i}} (y) = 1 \Leftrightarrow \int c (y) e ^ {y \theta_ {i}} \mu (d y) = e ^ {b (\theta_ {i})}. \tag {3.8}
$$

Let  $\operatorname{E}_{\cdot |W}(\cdot) \coloneqq \operatorname{E}(\cdot | W)$  and  $s$  be in  $(-\delta, \delta)$  (a neighbourhood of zero). Then,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | \boldsymbol {W}} [ \mathrm {e} ^ {s W _ {i} Y _ {i}} ] = \int \mathrm {e} ^ {s W _ {i} Y _ {i}} d F _ {Y _ {i} | \boldsymbol {W}} (y) = \int \mathrm {e} ^ {s W _ {i} Y _ {i}} d F _ {Y _ {i}} (y) [ \text {b y} \{W _ {i} \} _ {i = 1} ^ {n} \perp \{Y _ {i} \} _ {i = 1} ^ {n} ] \\ = \int c (y) e ^ {y \theta_ {i} - b (\theta_ {i})} e ^ {s W _ {i} y} \mu (d y) \stackrel {(3. 8)} {=} e ^ {b (\theta_ {i} + s W _ {i}) - b (\theta_ {i})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

It can be easily derived from (E.2) and Taylor's expansion,

$$
\mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} \left[ \mathrm {e} ^ {s \left(W _ {i} Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} \left(W _ {i} Y _ {i}\right)\right)} \right] = e ^ {b \left(\theta_ {i} + s W _ {i}\right) - b \left(\theta_ {i}\right) - b \left(\theta_ {i}\right) W _ {i} s} \frac {\exists \tilde {\eta} _ {i} \in \left[ \theta_ {i} , \theta_ {i} + s W _ {i} \right]}{2} e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} W _ {i} ^ {2}}{2} \tilde {b} \left(\tilde {\eta} _ {i}\right)} \leq e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} W _ {i} ^ {2}}{2}}. \tag {3.9}
$$

By the conditional independence for  $\{W_iZ_i|W\}$  and (3.9), it follows that when  $s \in (-\delta, \delta)$

$$
\mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | \boldsymbol {W}} \left[ e ^ {s \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ W _ {i} Z _ {i} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | \boldsymbol {W}} \left(W _ {i} Z _ {i}\right) \right]} \right] = \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | \boldsymbol {W}} e ^ {s \left[ W _ {i} Z _ {i} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | \boldsymbol {W}} \left(W _ {i} Z _ {i}\right) \right]} \leq \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {n} e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} W _ {i} ^ {2}}{2}} \leq 2 e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{2}}, \tag {3.10}
$$

where the last inequality is from  $\{|W_i|\leq w_i\}$  for a non-random vector  $\boldsymbol {w}\coloneqq (w_1,\dots ,w_n)^T$  By the conditional Markov's inequality and symmetry of  $Z_{i}$  , we have, as  $s\in (-\delta ,\delta)$

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ W _ {i} Z _ {i} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} \left(W _ {i} Z _ {i}\right) \right] \right| \geq t | W\right) \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} \left[ e ^ {- s t} \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} e ^ {s \left(\tilde {S} _ {n} ^ {W} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} \tilde {S} _ {n} ^ {W}\right)} + e ^ {- s t} \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} e ^ {- s \left(\tilde {S} _ {n} ^ {W} - \mathrm {E} _ {\cdot | W} \tilde {S} _ {n} ^ {W}\right)} \right] \\ \leq 2 \inf  _ {s > 0} e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{2} - s t} = 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 C _ {b} ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2}}} \tag {3.11} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last equality is minimized by setting  $s = t / (C_b^2 \| \boldsymbol{w} \|_2^2)$ .

Note that Lemma 6.1 in Rigollet (2012) is about the concentration for the non-random weighted sum of exponential family r.vs. The assumption of compact parameter space for exponential family is vital for obtaining the sub-Gaussian type concentration. If we do not impose condition (E.2) and the assumption that  $\{W_i\}_{i=1}^n$  and  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are dependent, a counterexample for sub-Gaussian concentration is  $W_i = Y_i$ . Thus,  $S_n^W$  is a quadratic form, and  $S_n^W - ES_n^W$  is sub-exponential by Lemma 4.2 below. If  $\{W_i\}_{i=1}^n$  and  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are dependent but  $\{W_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are still bounded, another counterexample is  $W_i = \mathrm{sign}(Y_i)$ . Therefore,  $S_n^W = \sum_{i=1}^n |Y_i|$  is not zero-mean, and the concentration of  $\sum_{i=1}^n |Y_i|$  fails.

# 3.4 Concentration for Lipschitz functions of random vectors

In the analyses of high-dimensional statistics by empirical processes, researches often resort to the CIs of Lipschitz functions for either bounded or strongly log-concave random vectors (Wainwright, 2019).

Lemma 3.1 (Theorem 2.26, Wainwright (2019)). Let  $N \sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathrm{I}_p)$ . Let  $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  be L-Lipschitz with respect to (w.r.t.) the Euclidean norm:  $|f(\boldsymbol{a}) - f(\boldsymbol{b})| \leq L\|\boldsymbol{a} - \boldsymbol{b}\|_2$  for any  $\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . Then,  $P(|f(N) - \mathrm{E}f(N)| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-t^2/(2L^2)}, \forall t > 0$ .

A non-negative function  $f(x): \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  is log-concave if

$$
\log f (\lambda x + (1 - \lambda) y) \geq \lambda \log f (x) + (1 - \lambda) \log f (y), \forall \lambda \in [ 0, 1 ] \text {a n d} x, y \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}. \tag {3.12}
$$

A function  $\psi(x): \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  is  $\gamma$ -strongly concave if there is some  $\gamma > 0$  s.t.

$$
\lambda \psi (\boldsymbol {x}) + (1 - \lambda) \psi (\boldsymbol {y}) - \psi (\lambda \boldsymbol {x} + (1 - \lambda) \boldsymbol {y}) \leq \frac {\gamma}{2} \lambda (1 - \lambda) \| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {y} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \forall \lambda \in [ 0, 1 ] \text {a n d} \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n},
$$

A continuous probability density  $f(x)$  and the corresponding r.v. are log-concave (or strongly log-concave) if  $f(x)$  is a log-concave function (or strongly log-concave function), see Saumard and Wellner (2014) for a review of the log-concavity in statistics.

Lemma 3.2 (Theorem 3.16, Wainwright (2019)). Let  $\mathbb{P}$  be any  $\gamma$ -strongly log-concave distribution on  $\mathbb{R}^n$  with parameter  $\gamma > 0$ . Then for any function  $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  that is  $L$ -Lipschitz w.r.t. the Euclidean norm, we have

$$
P [ f (X) - \operatorname {E} f (X) \geq t ] \leq e ^ {- \frac {\gamma t ^ {2}}{4 L ^ {2}}} \text {f o r} X \sim \mathbb {P} \text {a n d} t \geq 0.
$$

The standard Gaussian random vector is 1-strongly log-concave distributed. However, Lemma 3.1 has the sharper constant  $2L^2$  than the Gaussian case of Lemma 3.2 with constant  $4L^2$ . Beyond Gaussian and strongly log-concave, it is possible to establish concentration for distributions involving bounded r.vs. A function  $f(x): \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  is said to be separately convex if, the univariate function  $y_k \mapsto f(x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_{k-1}, y_k, x_{k+1}, \ldots, x_n)$  for each index  $k \in \{1, 2, \ldots, n\}$ , is convex for each fixed vector  $(x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_{k-1}, x_{k+1}, \ldots, x_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n-1}$ .

Lemma 3.3 (Theorem 3.4, Wainwright (2019)). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent r.vs, each supported on the interval  $[a,b]$ . Let  $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  be separately convex, and  $L$ -Lipschitz w.r.t. the Euclidean norm. Then,  $P[f(X) - \operatorname{E}f(X) \geq t] \leq e^{-t^2 / 4L^2(b - a)^2}$ . for  $X \sim \mathbb{P}$  and  $t \geq 0$ .

Example 3.6 (Order Statistics). From Lemma 3.2 and Lemma 3.3, suppose that  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are independent r.vs which are  $\gamma$ -strongly log-concave distributed satisfying  $P[f(X) - \operatorname{E}f(X) \geq t] \leq e^{-\frac{\gamma t^2}{4L^2}}$  for any function  $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$  that is  $L$ -Lipschitz w.r.t. the Euclidean norm. Let  $X_{(k)}$  be the  $k$ -th order statistic of  $X_1, \ldots, X_n$ , it can be shown that

$$
P \left(\left| X _ {(k)} - \mathrm {E} X _ {(k)} \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{4 / \gamma}} \text {b y c h e c k i n g} | X _ {(k)} - Y _ {(k)} | \leq \| X - Y \| _ {2}, \text {i . e .} L = 1.
$$

Indeed, we have

$$
X _ {(k)} - Y _ {(k)} \leq | X _ {l} - Y _ {l} | \leq \| X - Y \| _ {2} \text {f o r s o m e} l \in \{1, 2, \dots , n \}.
$$

More results of the tail bounds for the order statistics of IID r.vs are reported in Boucheron (2012).

# 4 Sub-exponential Distributions

# 4.1 Characterizations

The requirement in definition of sub-Gaussian r.v.  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{\frac{\sigma^2 s^2}{2}}$ ,  $\forall s \in \mathbb{R}$  is too strong. For example, we consider the MGF of exponential distributions:

Example 4.1 (MGF of exponential distributions). Consider the exponential r.v.  $X \sim \mathrm{Exp}(\mu)$  with  $\mathrm{EX} = \mu > 0$ . The MGF of  $X - \mu$  satisfies

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s (X - \mu)} = \frac {e ^ {- s \mu}}{1 - \mathrm {s} \mu} = \left(e ^ {- s \mu / 2} \left(1 - \mathrm {s} \mu\right) ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) ^ {2} \leq e ^ {4 (s \mu / 2) ^ {2}} <   e ^ {s ^ {2} (2 \mu) ^ {2} / 2}, \forall | s | \leq \left(2 \mu\right) ^ {- 1} \tag {4.1}
$$

where the second last inequality is by  $e^{-t} / \sqrt{1 - 2t} \leq e^{2t^2}$  for  $|t| \leq 1/4$ . (This is from the property of  $f(t) \coloneqq (1 - 2t)e^{4t^2 + 2t}$  with  $f(0) = 1$ : (a).  $f'(t) > 0, 0 < t < 1/4$ ; (b).  $f(t) \geq 1, -1/4 < t < 0$ ).

In (4.1), the MGF of the exponential r.v. is divergent on  $s = 1 / \mu$  and it cannot be bounded by a Gaussian MGF of  $s$  in  $\mathbb{R}$ , and the exponential MGF is bounded by Gaussian MGF for  $|s| \leq \frac{1}{2\mu}$  via inequality (4.1). Motivated by Example 4.1, the first definition of subexponential distribution (4.2) below is exactly the locally sub-Gaussian property.

Definition 4.1 (Sub-exponential distributions, Cramér (1938)). A r.v.  $X \in \mathbb{R}$  with  $\mathrm{EX} = 0$  is sub-exponential with variance parameter  $\lambda$  (denoted  $X \sim \mathrm{subE}(\lambda)$ ) if its MGF satisfies

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\mathrm {s} X} \leq e ^ {\frac {\mathrm {s} ^ {2} \lambda^ {2}}{2}} \quad \text {f o r a l l} | s | <   1 / \lambda . \tag {4.2}
$$

In Wainwright (2019), sub-exponential distributions are generally defined by two positive parameters  $(\lambda, \alpha)$  (denoted  $X \sim \mathrm{subE}(\lambda, \alpha)$ ):  $\mathrm{E}e^{sX} \leq e^{\frac{s^2\lambda^2}{2}}$  for all  $|s| < 1/\alpha$ . The minimal variance variance

$$
\tau_ {a, o p t} ^ {2} (X) := \inf  \left\{\tau^ {2} > 0: \mathbb {E} \exp (t X) \leq \exp \left\{\tau^ {2} t ^ {2} / 2 \right\}, \forall | t | <   a ^ {- 1} \right\}
$$

is called as optimal variance parameter; see Buldygin and Kozachenko (2020).

The  $\lambda^2$  in (4.2) is treated as a variance proxy and  $\alpha$  is seen as locally sub-Gaussian factor, see Remark 4.1 later. Specifically,  $\mathrm{subE}(\lambda) = \mathrm{subE}(\lambda, \lambda)$ . Sub-Gaussian r.vs are sub-exponential by definition, but not vice versa. In Corollary 3.1, one equivalence of

sub-Gaussian r.vs is that the survival function is bounded by the Gaussian-like survival function up to a constant. Similarly, the sub-exponential r.v. has a characterization that the survival function is bounded by that of an exponential distribution. Similar to sub-Gaussian characterizations, there are at least six equivalent forms for sub-exponential distributions which are useful for checking the sub-exponential distribution.

Corollary 4.1 (Characterizations of sub-exponential). Let  $X$  be a r.v. in  $\mathbb{R}$  with  $EX = 0$ . Then the following properties are equivalent, where  $\{K_i\}_{i=1}^6$  are positive constants.

(1) The tails of  $X$  satisfy  $P\{|X| \geq t\} \leq 2e^{-t / K_1}$  for all  $t \geq 0$ ;  
(2) The MGF of  $X$  satisfies  $\operatorname{E}e^{lX} \leq e^{K_2^2 l^2}$  for all  $|l| \leq \frac{1}{K_2}$ ;  
(3) The moments of  $X$  satisfy  $(\operatorname{E}|X|^p)^{1/p} \leq K_3 p$  for integer  $p \geq 1$ ;  
(4) The MGF of  $|X|$  satisfies  $\operatorname{E}e^{l|X|} \leq e^{K_4l}$  for all  $0 \leq l \leq \frac{1}{K_4}$ ;  
(5) The MGF of  $|X|$  is bounded at some point:  $\operatorname{Ee}^{|X| / K_5} \leq 2$ ;  
(6) Bounded MGF of  $X$  in a compact set:  $\mathrm{E}e^{tX} < \infty, \forall |t| < 1 / K_6$ .

The zero mean is only used in the proof of (2) of Corollary 4.1. The equivalence among (1)-(5) is proved in Vershynin (2018) and that between (5) and (6) can be found in Lemma 5 of Petrov (1975). The (6) is the called Cramer's condition which is an essential characterization, it signifies that: All r.vs. are sub-exponential if their MGF exist in a neighborhood of zero. Pistone and Wynn (1999) names the property (6) as the exponentially integrable r.v.

Example 4.2 (Moment of exponential distributions). The  $P(X - \mu \geq t) = e^{-(t + \mu) / \mu} \leq e^{-t / \mu}$  and the symmetry of  $X - \mu$  implies  $K_{1} = \mu$  in Corollary 4.1. Continue to Example 4.1, the “ $\leq$ ” in (4.1) implies  $\mathrm{E}e^{s(X - \mu)} \leq e^{(s\mu/\sqrt{2})^2} \leq e^{(2s\mu)^2}, \forall |s| < (2\mu)^{-1}$ . So  $K_{2} = \mu/\sqrt{2}$  and  $K_{6} = 2\mu$  in Corollary 4.1. Next, we evaluate the moment of  $X$  for any  $p \geq 1$ ,  $\mathrm{E}|X|^p = \int_0^\infty x^p \cdot \mu^{-1} e^{-\mu^{-1}x} dx \stackrel{y = \mu^{-1}x}{=} \mu^p \int_0^\infty y^p e^{-y} dy = \Gamma(p+1)\mu^p$ . By  $\Gamma(p+1) \leq p^p$  for  $p \geq 1$ , it gives:  $(\mathrm{E}|X|^p)^{1/p} = (\Gamma(p+1))^{1/p} \mu \leq p\mu$ . Via (4.4) shows that  $(\mathrm{E}|X - \mu|^p)^{1/p} \leq 2p\mu$  and thus  $K_{3} = 2\mu$  in Corollary 4.1. Assume  $\mathrm{E}X = 0$ , then by Stirling's approximation  $p! \geq (p/e)^p$

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\lambda | X |} = 1 + \sum_ {p = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {\lambda^ {p} \mathrm {E} | X | ^ {p}}{p !} \leq 1 + \sum_ {p = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {(\lambda K _ {3} p) ^ {p}}{(p / e) ^ {p}} = 1 + \sum_ {p = 2} ^ {\infty} (e K _ {3} \lambda) ^ {p} = 1 + \frac {(e K _ {3} \lambda) ^ {2}}{1 - e K _ {3} \lambda}, \forall | e K _ {3} \lambda | <   1
$$

$$
\left(\operatorname {R e s t r i c t} e K _ {3} \lambda \leq 1 / 2\right) \leq 1 + 2 \left(e K _ {2} \lambda\right) ^ {2} \leq e ^ {2 \left(e K _ {3} \lambda\right) ^ {2}} \leq e ^ {e K _ {3} \lambda} \leq e ^ {2 e K _ {3} \lambda}, \forall \lambda \leq 1 / \left(2 e K _ {3}\right). \tag {4.3}
$$

Thus  $K_{4} = 2eK_{3} = 4e\mu$ . That  $\mathrm{E}e^{\lambda |X|}\leq e^{eK_3\lambda}$  for  $0 < \lambda \leq 1 / (2eK_{2})$  in (4.3) implies  $\mathrm{E}e^{|X| / (2eK_3)} < e^{1 / 2} < 2$ . Hence  $K_{5} = K_{3}$ .

Example 4.3 (Geometric distributions). The geometric distribution  $X \sim \operatorname{Geo}(q)$  for r.v.  $X$  is defined by:  $P(X = k) = (1 - q)q^{k-1}, (q \in (0,1), k = 1,2,\dots)$ . The mean and the variance of  $\operatorname{Geo}(q)$  are  $(1 - q)/q$  and  $(1 - q)/q^2$  respectively. Apply Lemma 4.3 in Hillar and Wibisono (2013), we get  $(\operatorname{E}|X|^k)^{1/k} < -2k/\log(1 - q)$ . It follows from the Minkowski's inequality and Jensen's inequality  $(\operatorname{E}|Z|)^k \leq \operatorname{E}|Z|^k$  for integer  $k \geq 1$  that

$$
\left(\mathrm {E} | X - \mathrm {E} X | ^ {k}\right) ^ {1 / k} \leq \left(\mathrm {E} | X | ^ {k}\right) ^ {1 / k} + | \mathrm {E} X | \leq 2 \left(\mathrm {E} | X | ^ {k}\right) ^ {1 / k} \leq - 4 k / \log (1 - q) \tag {4.4}
$$

and Corollary 4.1(3) implies the centralized  $\operatorname{Geo}(q)$  is sub-exponential with  $K_{3} = -4 / \log (1 - q)$ .

Example 4.4 (Discrete Laplace r.vs). A r.v.  $X \sim \mathrm{DL}(q)$  obeys the discrete Laplace distribution if  $f_{q}(k) = \mathbb{P}(X = k) = \frac{1 - q}{1 + q} q^{|k|}$ ,  $k \in \mathbb{Z} = \{0, \pm 1, \pm 2, \ldots\}$  with parameter  $q \in (0, 1)$ . The discrete Laplace r.v. is the difference of two IID  $\operatorname{Geo}(q)$ . The geometric distribution is sub-exponential, thus Corollary 4.2(a) mentioned later implies that the discrete Laplace is also sub-exponential distributed. In differential privacy of network models, the noises are assumed following the discrete Laplace distribution, see Fan et al. (2020) and references therein.

The next result shows that a sum of independent sub-exponential r.vs has two tails with difference convergence rate, which is slightly different from Hoeffding's inequality. Deviating from the mean, it tells us that the tail of the sum of sub-exponential r.vs behaves like a combination of a Gaussian tail and an exponential tail.

Corollary 4.2 (Concentration for weighted sub-exponential sums). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent  $\{\mathrm{subE}(\lambda_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  distributed with zero mean. Define  $\lambda = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \lambda_i > 0$  and the non-random vector  $w := (w_1, \dots, w_n)^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$  with  $w = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |w_i| > 0$ , we have

(a) Closed under addition:  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} w_{i} X_{i} \sim \operatorname{subE}\left(\|w\|_{2} \lambda\right)$ ;  
(b)  $P(|\sum_{i = 1}^{n}w_{i}X_{i}|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-\frac{1}{2} (\frac{t^{2}}{\|w\|_{2}^{2}\lambda^{2}}\wedge \frac{t}{w\lambda})} = \left\{ \begin{array}{cc}2e^{-t^{2} / 2\| w\|_{2}^{2}\lambda^{2}}, & 0\leq t\leq \| w\|_{2}^{2}\lambda /w\\ 2e^{-t / 2w\lambda}, & t > \| w\|_{2}^{2}\lambda /w \end{array} \right.$  
(c) Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent zero-mean  $\{\mathrm{subE}(\lambda_i, \alpha_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  distributed. Define  $\alpha := \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \alpha_i > 0$ ,  $\|\lambda\|_2 := (\sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i^2)^{1/2}$  and  $\bar{\lambda} := (\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \lambda_i^2)^{1/2}$ . Then  $\sum_{i=1}^n X_i \sim \mathrm{subE}(\|\lambda\|_2, \alpha)$  and

$$
P \left(\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i} \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {1}{2} \left(\frac {n t ^ {2}}{\bar {\lambda} ^ {2}} \wedge \frac {n t}{\alpha}\right)} = \left\{ \begin{array}{c} 2 e ^ {- \frac {n t ^ {2}}{2 \bar {\lambda} ^ {2}}}, 0 \leq t \leq \bar {\lambda} ^ {2} \\ 2 e ^ {- \frac {n t}{2 \alpha}}, t > \bar {\lambda} ^ {2} \end{array} , \forall t \geq 0. \right. \tag {4.5}
$$

Remark 4.1. The  $\left(\frac{nt^2}{\lambda^2} \wedge \frac{nt}{\alpha}\right)$  in (4.5) reveals that the smaller  $\alpha$  (locally sub-Gaussian factor) leads to sharper sub-exponential concentration. The sub-exponential concentration tends to the sub-Gaussian concentration with variance proxy  $\bar{\lambda}^2$  when  $\alpha \to 0$ , which coincides the locally sub-Gaussian definition for sub-exponential distribution in Definition 4.1.

Proof. (a) By definition of sub-exponential r.vs,  $\mathrm{E}e^{s w_i X_i} \leq e^{s^2 w_i^2 \lambda_i^2 / 2} \forall |s| \leq \frac{1}{|w_i| \lambda_i}, i = 1, 2, \dots, n$ , and it implies  $\mathrm{E}e^{s w_i X_i} \leq e^{s^2 w_i^2 \lambda_i^2 / 2}, |s| \leq \frac{1}{w \lambda}$  for all  $i$ . By the independence among  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$ ,

$$
\operatorname {E} \exp \{s \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \} = \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \operatorname {E} e ^ {s w _ {i} X _ {i}} \leq \exp \{s ^ {2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} ^ {2} \lambda_ {i} ^ {2} / 2 \} \leq e ^ {s ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \lambda^ {2} / 2}, | s | \leq \frac {1}{w \lambda}.
$$

(b) Applying Chernoff's inequality, we get for  $0 < |s| \leq \frac{1}{w\lambda}$

$$
P \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \geq t\right) \leq e ^ {- s t} E e ^ {s \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i}} \leq e ^ {\frac {s ^ {2} \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \lambda^ {2}}{2} - s t} =: e ^ {g (s, t)}. \tag {4.6}
$$

We minimise the upper bound in (4.6) on the constraint  $|s| < \frac{1}{w\lambda}$ . Note that  $\frac{\partial}{\partial s} g(s,t) = s\| w\|_2^2\lambda^2 - t$  implies the stationary point  $s_0 = \frac{t}{\|w\|_2^2\lambda^2}$ . So we have  $g(s,t)\big|_{s=s_0} = -\frac{1}{2w\lambda}$ .

- If  $s_0 \leq \frac{t}{w\lambda}$ , then the minimizer is  $s = s_0$ . Actually, the upper bound is  $e^{-\frac{t^2}{2\|w\|_2^2\Lambda^2}}$ .  
- If  $s_0 = \frac{t}{\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_2^2\lambda^2} > \frac{1}{w\lambda}$ , so the minimizer is obtained at the boundary  $s = \frac{1}{w\lambda}$  with condition  $\| \boldsymbol{w} \|_2^2 < \frac{t w}{\lambda}$ . Hence the upper bound is bounded by  $e^{-\frac{t}{w\lambda} + \frac{\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_2^2}{2w^2}} \leq e^{-\frac{t}{w\lambda} + \frac{t}{2w\lambda}} = e^{-\frac{t}{2w\lambda}}$ .

Summarizing this two cases, we can get

$$
P (\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \geq t) \leq e ^ {- \frac {1}{2} (\frac {t ^ {2}}{\| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \lambda^ {2}} \wedge \frac {t}{w \lambda})}
$$

By the same argument, we will have  $P\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} w_{i} X_{i} \leq -t\right) \leq \exp \left\{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{t^{2}}{\|w\|_{2}^{2} \lambda^{2}} \wedge \frac{t}{w \lambda}\right)\right\}$  due to the symmetric property of mean zero random variables.

(c). The proof is similar to (b), see page 29 of Wainwright (2019).

Corollary 4.2(b) is due to Petrov, and it is also called Petrov's exponential inequalities, see Lin and Bai (2011). Although Corollary 4.2(b,c) are non-asymptotically valid for any number of summands. Nevertheless, it also has asymptotical merit, which implies: Strong Law of Large Numbers (SLNN), Central Limit Theorem (CLT), and Law of the Iterated Logarithm (LIL) for sub-exponential sums, as discussed below.

(1) SLNN. Let  $w_{i} = 1 / n$ . Consider the sample mean  $\bar{X}_n = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X$  for IID  $\{\mathrm{subE}(\lambda_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  data  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  with population mean  $\mu$ , and we can use Corollary 4.2(b) to prove that  $\bar{X}_n \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \mu$ . We verify the Borel-Cantelli lemma by observing that  $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} P(|\bar{X}_n - \mu| > \varepsilon) \leq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty} 2e^{-\frac{n}{2} (\frac{\varepsilon^2}{\lambda^2} \wedge \frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda})} < \infty$ , which shows the strong convergence:  $\bar{X}_n \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \mu$ . Corollary 4.2(b)

also implies the rate of convergence for sample mean for all  $n$  with a high probability. It is easy to see that the sample mean  $\bar{X}_n$  has the non-asymptotic error bounds by

$$
| \bar {X} _ {n} - \mu | \leq \sqrt {\frac {2 \lambda^ {2} t}{n}} \vee \frac {2 \lambda t}{n} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sqrt {\frac {2 \lambda^ {2} t}{n}}, & n \geq 2 t (\text {s l o w g l o b a l r a t e}) \\ \frac {2 \lambda t}{n}, & n <   2 t (\text {f a s t l o c a l r a t e}) \end{array} \right. \tag {4.7}
$$

$\forall t > 0$  with the probability at least  $1 - 2e^{-t}$ .

(2) CLT. To study the convergence rate of CLT, we standardize the sum by letting  $w_{i} = 1 / \sqrt{n}$  and apply Corollary 4.2(b) to

$$
P (| \sqrt {n} \bar {X} _ {n} | \geq t) \leq 2 \exp \left\{- \frac {1}{2} \left(\frac {t ^ {2}}{\lambda^ {2}} \wedge \frac {t}{\lambda / \sqrt {n}}\right) \right\} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 2 e ^ {- c t ^ {2} / \lambda^ {2}}, & t \leq \lambda \sqrt {n}; \\ 2 e ^ {- t \sqrt {n} / \lambda}, & t > \lambda \sqrt {n}. \end{array} \right.
$$

The above deviation inequality is powerful as it indicates the phase transition about the tail behavior of  $\sqrt{n}\bar{X}_n$ : Small Deviation Regime. In the regime  $t \leq \lambda \sqrt{n}$ , we have a sub-Gaussian tail bound with variance proxy  $\lambda^2$  as if the sum had the normal distribution with a constant variance. Note that the domain  $t \leq \lambda \sqrt{n}$  widens as  $n$  increases and then the central limit theorem becomes more powerful. Large Deviation Regime. In the regime  $t \geq \lambda \sqrt{n}$ , the sum has a heavier tail. The sub-exponential tail bound is affected from the extreme variable among  $\{\mathrm{subE}(\lambda_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  with parameter  $\lambda / \sqrt{n}$ .

(3) LIL. Let  $w_{i} = 1 / n$  and  $t = \frac{R\sqrt{\log\log n}}{\sqrt{n}} \leq \| w\| _2^2\lambda /w = \lambda$  for some positive constant  $R$ . Corollary 4.2(b) claims

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left(\left| \bar {X} _ {n} \right| \geq \frac {R \sqrt {\log \log n}}{\sqrt {n}}\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- t ^ {2} / 2 \| w \| _ {2} ^ {2} \lambda^ {2}} = 2 \exp \left\{- \frac {n}{2 \lambda^ {2}} \cdot \frac {R ^ {2} \log \log n}{n} \right\} \\ = 2 \exp \left\{\log \left(\log n\right) ^ {- R ^ {2} / 2 \lambda^ {2}} \right\} = 2 / \left(\log n\right) ^ {R ^ {2} / 2 \lambda^ {2}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, with probability  $1 - 2 / (\log n)^{R^2 /2\lambda^2}$ ,  $|\bar{X}_n|\leq \frac{R\sqrt{\log\log n}}{\sqrt{n}}$ . Although some researchers claims that LIL is useless, we clarify that there are still some meaningful applications of LIL, see Jamieson et al. (2014) and Yang et al. (2021) for the statistical and machine learning applications of the LIL.

# 4.2 Sub-exponential norm

Recall the Corollary 4.1(5): The absolute value of sub-exponential r.v.  $|X|$  has a bound MGF at point  $K_5^{-1}$ :  $\phi_{|X|}(K_5^{-1}) \coloneqq \mathrm{Ee}^{|X| / K_5} \leq 2$ . Similar to the definition of sub-Gaussian norm, we define the sub-exponential norm.

Definition 4.2 (sub-exponential norm). The sub-exponential norm of  $X$  is defined as

$$
\left\| X \right\| _ {\psi_ {1}} = \inf  \left\{t > 0: \operatorname {E} \exp (| X | / t) \leq 2 \right\}. \tag {4.8}
$$

An alternative definition of the sub-exponential norm is  $\| X \|_{\psi_1} \coloneqq \sup_{p \geq 1} p^{-1} (E|X|^p)^{1/p}$  as in Vershynin (2012). The sub-exponential r.v.  $X$  satisfies the equivalent properties in Corollary 4.1 (Characterizations of sub-exponential). Next, we present a useful lemma below which is to determine the sub-exponential parameter in the Definition 4.1 by its MGF if we adopt Definition 4.2 of the sub-exponential norm.

Proposition 4.1 (Properties of sub-exponential norm). If  $\mathrm{Eexp}(|X| / \| X\|_{\psi_1})\leq 2$  , then

(a) Tail bounds  $P(|X| > t)\leq 2e^{-t / \| X\|_{\psi_1}}$  for all  $t\geq 0$  
(b) Moment bounds  $\operatorname{E}|X|^k \leq 2\| X\|_{\psi_1}^k k!$  for all integer  $k \geq 1$ ;  
(c) If  $\mathrm{EX} = 0$ , the MGF bounds  $\mathrm{Ee}^{sX} \leq e^{(2\| X\|_{\psi_1})^2 s^2 /2}$  for all  $|s| < 1 / (2\| X\|_{\psi_1})$ , i.e.  $X \sim \mathrm{subE}(2\| X\|_{\psi_1})$ .

Proof. (a). To verify (a), using exponential Markov's inequality, we have  $P(|X| \geq t) = P(e^{|X / \|X\|_{\psi_1}|} \geq e^{t / \|X\|_{\psi_1}}) \leq e^{-t / \|X\|_{\psi_1}} \mathbb{E}e^{|X / \|X\|_{\psi_1}|} \leq 2e^{-t / \|X\|_{\psi_1}}$  by Definition 4.2.

(b). Similar to the proof of Theorem 5.1 (b), we get from (a)

$$
\mathrm {E} | X | ^ {k} = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} P (| X | \geq t) k t ^ {k - 1} d t \leq 2 k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {- t / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}} t ^ {k - 1} d t
$$

$$
\left[ \operatorname {l e t} s = t / \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}} \right] = 2 k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {- s} \left(s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}\right) ^ {k - 1} \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}} d s = 2 \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}} ^ {k} k \Gamma (k - 1) = 2 \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}} ^ {k} k!.
$$

(c). Applying Taylor's expansion to MGF, we have

$$
\operatorname {E} \exp (s X) = 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {s ^ {k} \operatorname {E} X ^ {k}}{k !} \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} 1 + 2 \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} (s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}) ^ {k} = 1 + \frac {2 (s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}) ^ {2}}{1 - s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}}, (| s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}} | <   1)
$$

$$
\left[ \mathrm {i f} | s | <   1 / (2 \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}) \right] \leq 1 + 4 (s \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}) ^ {2} \leq e ^ {(2 \| X \| _ {\psi_ {1}}) ^ {2} s ^ {2} / 2}.
$$

Therefore,  $X \sim \mathrm{subE}(2\| X\|_{\psi_1})$

Proposition 4.1(c) implies the following user-friendly concentration inequality which contains all known constant. One should note that Theorem 2.8.1 of Vershynin (2018) includes an un-specific constant, which makes it is inefficacious when constructing non-asymptotic confident intervals for sub-exponential sample mean.

Proposition 4.2 (Concentration for r.v. with sub-exponential sum). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be zero mean independent sub-exponential distributed with  $\| X_i \|_{\psi_1} \leq \infty$ . Then for every  $t \geq 0$ ,

$$
P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i} \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 \exp \left\{- \frac {1}{4} \left(\frac {t ^ {2}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} 2 \| X _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {1}} ^ {2}} \wedge \frac {t}{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| X _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {1}}}\right) \right\}.
$$

Proof. If  $\mathrm{Eexp}(|X| / \| X\|_{\psi_1})\leq 2$  , then  $X\sim \mathrm{subE}(2\| X\|_{\psi_1})$  by using Proposition 4.1(c). The result follows by employing Corollary 4.2(b).

Götz et al. (2021) mentions an explicitly calculation the sub-exponential norm with example of Poisson distributions. Therefore, it is convenient to apply Proposition 4.2 to get the concentration of sub-exponential summation.

Lemma 4.1. If  $\| X\|_{\psi_1}$  exists, then  $\| X\|_{\psi_1} = 1 / \phi_{|X|}^{-1}(2)$  for the MGF  $\phi_X(t)\coloneqq \mathrm{Ee}^{tX}$ .

Proof. Note that  $\| \cdot \|_{\psi_1}$  is the smallest  $t$  such that  $\mathrm{Ee}^{|X| / t} = \phi_{|X|}(t^{-1}) \leq 2$ , so  $t^{-1} \leq \phi_{|X|}^{-1}(2)$  and  $t \geq 1 / \phi_{|X|}^{-1}(2)$ . By the definition of  $\| \cdot \|_{\psi_1}$  again, we have  $\| X \|_{\psi_1} = 1 / \phi_{|X|}^{-1}(2)$ .

Example 4.5 (The sub-exponential norm of bounded r.v.). Consider a r.v.  $|X| \leq M < \infty$ . Set  $\mathrm{Ee}^{|X| / t} \leq e^{M / t} \leq 2$  and  $t \geq M / \log 2$ . By the definition of  $\| X\|_{\psi_1}$ , we have  $\| X\|_{\psi_1} = M / \log 2$ .

Example 4.6 (The sub-exponential norm of Poisson r.v.). Poisson r.v.  $X$  has the probability mass function  $P(X = k) = \frac{\lambda^k}{k!} e^{-k}$ ,  $(k = 1,2,\dots ,n;\lambda >0)$ . We denote it as  $X\sim \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)$ . The MGF of the Poisson  $(\lambda)$  is  $\phi_X(t)\coloneqq e^{\lambda (e^t -1)}$ . We have  $\| X\|_{\psi_1} = [\log (\log (2)\lambda^{-1} + 1)]^{-1}$ , and the triangle inequality shows  $\| X - \mathsf{E}X\|_{\psi_1}\leq \| X\|_{\psi_1} + \| \mathsf{E}X\|_{\psi_1} = \| X\|_{\psi_1} + \frac{\lambda}{\log 2}\leq [\log (\log (2)\lambda^{-1} + 1)]^{-1} + \frac{\lambda}{\log 2}\propto \lambda$ , where we use inequality  $\| \mathsf{E}X\|_{\psi_1} = \frac{|\mathsf{E}X|}{\log 2}$  by Example 4.5.

Corollary 4.2 is useful in the next subsection for the concentration for quadratic forms.

# 4.3 Concentration for quadratic forms and norm of random vectors

All concentration results in the above sections are about the mean. The inference for the variance and covariance in high-dimensional models is an important problem, see Section 6 of Wainwright (2019). It is connected with squares of r.vs. The sample variance is a quadratic form (with shift term) of the data. The data are often postulated as sub-Gaussian. For the square of a sub-Gaussian r.v., it is natural to ask what is the behavior of the tail (or the exponential moment). The answer is sub-exponential by using (5) in Corollary 3.1.

A simple example that the quadratic form of Gaussian is  $\chi^2$  distributed, and the  $\chi^2$ -distribution of 2 degrees of freedom is exponentially distributed with mean 2. Let us look the  $\chi^2$ -concentration below:

Example 4.7 (Chi-squared r.vs). If  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n \stackrel{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim} N(0,1)$ , then we say  $Y_n := \sum_{i=1}^n X_i^2$  follows  $\chi^2$ -distribution with  $n$ -degree of freedom, denoted as  $Y_n \sim \chi^2(n)$ . The density function is  $f(y) = \Gamma^{-1}\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}y^{\frac{n}{2}-1}e^{-\frac{y}{2}} \cdot 1(y > 0)$ . As  $s < 1/2$ , the MGF of  $X_i^2 - 1$  is

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s (X _ {i} ^ {2} - 1)} = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {+ \infty} e ^ {s (x ^ {2} - 1)} e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2} d x = \frac {e ^ {- s}}{\sqrt {1 - 2 s}} \leq e ^ {2 s ^ {2}} = e ^ {(2 s) ^ {2} / 2}, \quad \mathrm {f o r a l l} | s | <   \frac {1}{4}.
$$

where the second last inequality is due to  $\frac{e^{-t}}{\sqrt{1 - 2t}}\leq e^{2t^2}$  for  $|t| < 1 / 4$ . Then  $X_{i}^{2}\sim \mathrm{subE}(2,4)$ . Applying Corollary 4.2(c), we have  $Y_{n}\sim \mathrm{subE}(2\sqrt{n},4)$ , therefore  $P(|\frac{Y_n - n}{n} |\geq t)\leq 2e^{-\frac{n}{8} (t^2\wedge t)}$ .

Similar sub-exponential results also hold for independent sum of square of sub-Gaussian r.vs. The following two lemmas in Page31 of Vershynin (2018) confirm this simple example to the general situation.

Lemma 4.2 (Square and product of sub-Gaussian are sub-exponential). (a). A r.v.  $X$  is sub-Gaussian if and only if  $X^2$  is sub-exponential. Moreover,  $\| X^2\|_{\psi_1} = \| X\|_{\psi_2}^2$ ; (b). Let  $X$  and  $Y$  be sub-Gaussian r.vs. Then  $XY$  is sub-exponential and  $\| XY\|_{\psi_1}\leq \| X\|_{\psi_2}\| Y\|_{\psi_2}$

For Lemma 4.2(a), it follows from  $\| X^2\|_{\psi_1} = \| X\|_{\psi_2}^2$  and Lemma 4.1 that Corollary 4.2 coincides Proposition 3.3 as  $\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\| X_i\|_{\psi_1}\to 0$ , i.e. the sub-exponential r.v. degenerates to the sub-Gaussian r.v. the next proposition gives the accurately sub-exponential parameter for the square of sub-Gaussian r.v. in Definition 4.1, and it improves the constant in Lemma 1.12 of Rigollet and Hütter (2023) (from  $\mathrm{subE}(16\sigma^2)$  to  $\mathrm{subE}(8\sqrt{2}\sigma^2)$ ).

Proposition 4.3. Let  $X \sim \mathrm{subG}(\sigma^2)$ , then  $Z := X^2 - \mathrm{EX}^2 \sim \mathrm{subE}(8\sqrt{2}\sigma^2)$  or  $\sim \mathrm{subE}(8\sqrt{2}\sigma^2, 8\sigma^2)$ .

Proof. The proof is immediately from Proposition 3.2(c) by letting  $\boldsymbol{w} := (1,0,\dots,0)^T$ .

In below, we deal with a sharper Hanson-Wright inequality in Bellec (2019). The Hanson-Wright (HW) inequality is a general concentration result for quadratic forms of sub-Gaussian r.vs, which was first studied in Hanson and Wright (1971). Let  $\mathbf{A} = (a_{ij})\in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$  be a real matrix and the  $\xi = (\xi_1,\dots,\xi_n)^T$  be a centered random vector with independent components. Define the Frobenius norm (Hilbert-Schmidt norm)  $\| \mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\coloneqq \sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A})} = \sqrt{\sum_{i,j}A_{i,j}^2}$  and the spectral norm (operator norm)  $\| \mathbf{A}\| _2\coloneqq \sup_{\| u\| _2\leq 1}\| \mathbf{A}u\| _2$  As an extension of  $\chi^2$  r.vs, it is of interest to study the concentration behavior of  $\xi^T\mathbf{A}\xi -$  E[  $\pmb {\xi}^T\mathbf{A}\pmb {\xi}]$  . Under the setting above, Example 2.12 in Boucheron et al. (2013) gives the Gaussian chaos concentration.

Corollary 4.3 (Gaussian chaos of order 2). Let  $\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n$  be zero-mean Gaussian with  $\mathrm{E}\xi_i^2 = \sigma_i^2$ , Define  $\mathbf{D}_{\sigma} = \mathrm{diag}(\sigma_1, \ldots, \sigma_n)$ , then for any  $x > 0$

$$
P \left(\boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} - \mathrm {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} \right] \geq 2 \| \mathbf {D} _ {\sigma} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {D} _ {\sigma} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \sqrt {x} + 2 \| \mathbf {D} _ {\sigma} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {D} _ {\sigma} \| _ {2} x\right) \leq e ^ {- x}. \tag {4.9}
$$

The similar concentration phenomenon is also available for sub-Gaussian r.vs. which is named as the HW inequality. Rudelson and Vershynin (2013) gives a modern proof by the so-called decoupling argument attributed to Bourgain (1996).

Corollary 4.4 (R-V's HW inequality). Let  $n \geq 1$  and  $\xi := (\xi_1, \dots, \xi_n)^T$  be an independent zero-mean sub-Gaussian r.vs with  $\max_{i=1,\dots,n} \|\xi_i\|_{\psi_2} \leq K$  for  $K > 0$ . Let  $A$  be any  $n \times n$  real matrix. Then there exists a constant  $c > 0$  such that

$$
P \left(\boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} - \mathrm {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} \right] > t\right) \leq e ^ {- c \left(\frac {t ^ {2}}{K ^ {4} \| \mathbf {A} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}} \wedge \frac {t}{K ^ {2} \| \mathbf {A} _ {2}}\right)}, t \geq 0 \tag {4.10}
$$

Furthermore, for any  $x > 0$ ,  $P(\pmb{\xi}^T \mathbf{A} \pmb{\xi} - \mathrm{E}[\pmb{\xi}^T \mathbf{A} \pmb{\xi}] \leq c K^2 (\| \mathbf{A} \|_2 x + \| \mathbf{A} \|_{\mathrm{F}} \sqrt{x})) \geq 1 - e^{-x}$ .

Intuitively, the term  $K^2\| \mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}$  is seen as the "variance term". When  $\mathbf{A}$  is diagonal-free (i.e. the  $\mathbf{A}$  matrix has zeros down its diagonal:  $a_{ii} = 0$ ), the r.v.  $\boldsymbol{\xi}^{T}\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\xi}$  is zero-mean. Pollard (2015) shortens the proof without unknown constant.

Corollary 4.5 (Diagonal-free Hanson-Wright inequality). Let  $\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n$  be independent, centered sub-Gaussian r.vs with  $\max_{i=1,\ldots,n} \|\xi_i\|_{\psi_2} \leq K < \infty$ . Let  $\mathbf{A}$  be an  $n \times n$  matrix of real numbers with  $a_{ii} = 0$  for each  $i$ . Then  $P(\boldsymbol{\xi}^T \mathbf{A} \boldsymbol{\xi} \geq t) \leq e^{-(\frac{t^2}{64K^4 \| \mathbf{A} \|_{\mathrm{F}}} \wedge \frac{t}{8\sqrt{2K^2 \| \mathbf{A} \|_2}})}$  for  $t \geq 0$ .

Under assumptions on the moments of  $\xi_1,\ldots ,\xi_n$  (do not need sub-Gaussian assumption), the next corollary provides a concentration inequality for quadratic forms of independent r.vs satisfying Bernstein's moment condition (discussed in the next subsection).

Corollary 4.6 (Quadratic forms concentration with moment conditions). Assume that the r.v.  $\pmb{\xi} = (\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})^{T}$  satisfies the condition on independent variables  $\xi_1^2,\ldots ,\xi_n^2$  :  $\operatorname {E}|\xi_i|^{2p}\leq$ $\frac{1}{2} p!\sigma_i^2\kappa^{2p - 2}\forall p\geq 1$  for some  $\kappa >0$  . Let  $A$  be any  $n\times n$  real matrix. Then for all  $t\geq 0$

$$
P \left(\boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} - \mathrm {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} \right] > t\right) \leq e ^ {- \left(\frac {t ^ {2}}{1 9 2 \kappa^ {2} \| \mathbf {A D} _ {\sigma} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}} \wedge \frac {t}{2 5 6 \kappa^ {2} \| \mathbf {A} \| _ {2}}\right)}, \tag {4.11}
$$

where  $\mathbf{D}_{\sigma} \coloneqq \mathrm{diag}(\sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_n)$ . Furthermore, with probability greater than  $1 - e^{-x}$ ,

$$
\boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} - \mathrm {E} [ \boldsymbol {\xi} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} ] \leq 2 5 6 \kappa^ {2} \| \mathbf {A} \| _ {2} x + 8 \sqrt {3} \kappa \| \mathbf {A D} _ {\sigma} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \sqrt {x}, \forall x \geq 0. \tag {4.12}
$$

The bound in (4.11) is exactly  $\exp \left(-\frac{t^2}{192\kappa^2\|\mathbf{AD}_c\|_{\mathrm{F}}^2}\right)$  if  $t$  is small, and while the  $\exp \left(-c\frac{t^2}{K^4\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^2}\right)$  in right hand side of the R-V's HW inequality (4.10) has an unspecific constant  $c > 0$ .

We finish this subsection with an exponential inequality for quadratic forms of a sub-Gaussian random vector. Consider the  $n$ -dimensional unit sphere  $S^{n-1} \coloneqq \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \| x \|_2 = 1\}$ . Early in Fukuda (1990), a random vector  $X$  in  $\mathbb{R}^n$  is called sub-Gaussian (sub-exponential) if the one-dimensional marginals  $\langle X, x \rangle$  are sub-Gaussian (sub-exponential) r.vs for all  $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . Naturally, the sub-Gaussian (sub-exponential) norm of  $X$  is defined as  $\| X \|_{\psi_2} \coloneqq \sup_{x \in S^{n-1}} \| \langle X, x \rangle \|_{\psi_2} (\| X \|_{\psi_1} \coloneqq \sup_{x \in S^{n-1}} \| \langle X, x \rangle \|_{\psi_1})$ . For the sub-Gaussian, Fukuda (1990)'s definition is equivalent to Chapter 6.3 of Wainwright (2019), a random vector  $X \in \mathbb{R}^d$  with parameter  $\sigma \in \mathbb{R}$  is sub-Gaussian (denote  $\operatorname{subGV}(\sigma^2)$ ) so that:

$$
\operatorname {E} e ^ {\lambda (v, X - \operatorname {E} X)} \leq e ^ {\lambda \sigma^ {2} / 2}, \forall \lambda \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n} \text {a n d} v \in S ^ {n - 1} \Leftrightarrow \operatorname {E} e ^ {\boldsymbol {\alpha} ^ {T} (X - \operatorname {E} X)} \leq e ^ {\| \boldsymbol {\alpha} \| ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} / 2}, \forall \boldsymbol {\alpha} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n} \tag {4.13}
$$

In Pan and Zhou (2020), the subG random vector with parameter  $v_0 \geq 1$  is defined by  $P(|\langle u, X \rangle| \geq v_0 \| u \|_{\Sigma} \cdot t) \leq 2e^{-t^2 / 2}$  for all  $u \in \mathbb{R}^n$  and  $t \geq 0$ , where  $\Sigma = \operatorname{E}\left(XX^T\right)$  and  $\| u \|_{\mathbf{A}} = \left\| \mathbf{A}^{1/2} u \right\|_2$  is the norm indexed by  $\mathbf{A}$ . For Definition (4.13), Hsu et al. (2012) obtains a tail bound for subG random vectors:

Corollary 4.7 (Tail inequality for quadratic forms of sub-Gaussian vectors). Let  $\boldsymbol{\Sigma} = \mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A}$  for  $p\times n$  matrix  $\mathbf{A}$ . Consider a sub-Gaussian random vector  $\xi = (\xi_1,\dots,\xi_n)^T\sim \mathrm{subGV}(\sigma^2)$  with independent components for  $\mu = \operatorname {E}\xi$ . Then, for any  $t\geq 0$

$$
P \{\| \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\xi} \| ^ {2} > \sigma^ {2} [ \mathrm {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) + 2 \mathrm {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {2} t) ^ {1 / 2} + 2 \| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \| _ {2} t ] + \mathrm {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} \boldsymbol {\mu} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {T}) (1 + 2 \sqrt {\frac {\| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\mathrm {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {2})} t}) \} \leq e ^ {- t}.
$$
