Conditioning on a divergence number of non-random covariates, an application of Corollary 4.7 for the prediction error (8.6) in regressions with sub-Gaussian noise is given in Section 8.1. The concentration bounds of sub-Gaussian random vectors depend on the parameter  $\sigma$ : the smaller  $\sigma$ , the tighter concentration bounds. Equation (4.13) requires the distribution of subG random vectors to be isotropic, and the random vectors have an exponential tail, but the sub-Gaussian parameter  $\sigma$  may be large, which leads to loose bounds for constructing confidence bands. To establish tighter bounds, Jin et al. (2019) define a different and general class of sub-Gaussian distributions in  $\mathbb{R}^n$ , called norm-subGaussian random vectors as follows.

Definition 4.3 (Norm-subGaussian). A random vector  $\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  is norm-subG (denoted nsubG(σ²)), if  $\exists \sigma$  so that:  $P(\| \mathbf{X} - \mathbf{E}\mathbf{X}\| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-\frac{t^2}{2\sigma^2}}$ ,  $\forall t \in \mathbb{R}^{+}$ .

The Definition 4.3 only requires the tail probability estimate has sub-Gaussian tail under  $l_{2}$ -norm, avoiding the uniform condition in (4.13). If  $\mathbf{E}X = \mathbf{0}$  and  $2e^{-t^2 /2\sigma^2}\geq P(\| X\| \geq t)$  from nsubG  $(\sigma^2)$ , we get  $P(|\langle u,X\rangle |\geq t)\leq P(\| X\| \geq t)\leq 2e^{-t^2 /2\sigma^2}$ ,  $u\in S^{n - 1}$  by Cauchy's inequality. Thus, nsubG  $(\sigma^2)$  implies (4.13), and this verifies that the norm-subG is more general. Jin et al. (2019) show that if  $X\in \mathbb{R}^n$  is subGV  $(\sigma^2 /n)$ , then  $X\sim \mathrm{nsubG}(8\sigma^2)$ .

# 5 Sub-Gamma Distributions and Bernstein's Inequality

# 5.1 Sub-Gamma distributions

Comparing to the classical Chebyshev's inequality, Bernstein-type inequalities have more precise concentration, it originally is an extension of the Hoeffding's inequality with bounded assumption [see Bernstein (1924), Bennett (1962)]. As mentioned by Pollard (2015), the proof of Hoeffding's inequality with endpoints of the interval  $[a,b]$  in Lemma 2.1 (with  $n = 1$ ) crudely depends on the variance bound:

$$
\operatorname {V a r} X = \mathrm {E} (X - \mathrm {E} X) ^ {2} \leq \mathrm {E} [ X - (b - a) / 2 ] ^ {2} \leq [ (b - a) / 2 ] ^ {2} \text {i f} a \leq X \leq b \tag {5.1}
$$

without any other variance information.

If  $X$  takes values near the endpoints of the interval  $[a, b]$  with a small probability, it is guessed that the sharper concentration could be improved by adding variance condition. The following tail bound for the sum  $S_{n} := \sum_{i=1}^{n} X_{i}$  needs extra variance information.

Corollary 5.1 (Bernstein's inequality with the bounded condition). Let  $X_1, \ldots, X_n$  be centralized independent variables such that  $|X_i| \leq M$  a.s. for all  $i$ . Then,  $\forall t > 0$

$$
P \big (| S _ {n} | \geq t \big) \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i} + M t / 3}}, P \big \{| S _ {n} | \geq (2 t \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i}) ^ {1 / 2} + \frac {M t}{3} \big \} \leq 2 e ^ {- t}.
$$

The next example illustrates a sharp confidence interval for sample mean if we know that the variance is sufficient small.

Example 5.1 (Non-asymptotic confidence intervals). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n \stackrel{\text{IID}}{\sim} X$  with the support  $[-c, c]$  and the mean  $\mu$ . Hoeffding's and Bernstein's inequalities show for  $\bar{X} := n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^n X_i$

$$
\begin{array}{l} P (| \bar {X} - \mu | \leq \sqrt {\frac {2 c ^ {2} \log (2 / \delta)}{n}}) \geq 1 - \delta \quad \mathrm {H o e f f d i n g}; \\ P \left(\left| \bar {X} - \mu \right| \leq \frac {c}{3 n} \log (2 / \delta) + \sqrt {\frac {2 (\operatorname {V a r} X) \log (2 / \delta)}{n}}\right) \geq 1 - \delta \quad \text {B e r n s t e i n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

For large  $n$ , the Bernstein's confidence interval is substantially shorter if  $X_{i}$  has relatively small variance, i.e.  $\operatorname{Var}X \ll c^{2}$  (the factor  $\sqrt{\frac{\log(2 / \delta)}{n}}$  is a dominated term). The Hoeffding's confidence is shorter as  $\operatorname{Var}X = c^{2}$  (This extreme case attains the upper bound  $\operatorname{Var}X \leq c^{2}$  in (5.1) due to  $b - a = 2c$ ). But, for the case  $\operatorname{Var}X < c^{2}$ , if  $n$  is sufficient small s.t.  $\frac{c}{3n}\log (2 / \delta) \geq (c - \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}X})\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2 / \delta)}{n}}$ , i.e. we need restrictions  $n \leq \frac{1}{18}\left(\frac{c}{c - \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}X}}\right)^{2}\log (\frac{2}{\delta}) \geq 1$  to ensure Hoeffding's confidence interval is more accurate when  $\delta \leq 2\exp \left\{-\frac{1}{18}\left(\frac{c - \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}X}}{c}\right)^{2}\right\}$ .

To prove Corollary 5.1, we need get the sharp bounds of the MGF of the single variable and then do aggregation for the summation. By the Taylor expansion, we have

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s X _ {i}} = 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} s ^ {k} \frac {\mathrm {E} X _ {i} ^ {k}}{k !} \leq 1 + \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} s ^ {k} \frac {M ^ {k - 2} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i}}{k !} \leq 1 + s ^ {2} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {(| s | M) ^ {k - 2}}{k !}, 1 \leq i \leq n.
$$

Applying the inequality  $k! / 2 \geq 3^{k - 2}$  for any  $k \geq 2$ , it implies

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s X _ {i}} \leq 1 + \frac {s ^ {2} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i}}{2} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \left(\frac {| s | M}{3}\right) ^ {k - 2} = 1 + \frac {s ^ {2} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i} / 2}{1 - | s | M / 3} \leq \exp \left(\frac {s ^ {2} \mathrm {V a r} X _ {i} / 2}{1 - | s | M / 3}\right). \tag {5.2}
$$

The upper bounds of MGF essentially have the same form in comparison with Gamma distribution below whose MGF is bounded by (5.3) in following example.

Example 5.2 (Gamma r.vs). The Gamma distribution with density  $f(x) = \frac{x^{a - 1}e^{-x / b}}{\Gamma(a)b^a}$ ,  $x \geq 0$  is denote as  $\Gamma(a,b)$ . We have  $\mathrm{EX} = ab$  and  $\mathrm{Var}X = ab^2$  for  $X \sim \Gamma(a,b)$ . The Page28 of Boucheron et al. (2013) illustrates that the log-MGF of a centered  $\Gamma(a,b)$  is bounded by

$$
\log \left(\mathrm {E} e ^ {s (X - \mathrm {E} X)}\right) = a (- \log (1 - s b) - s b) \leq s ^ {2} a b ^ {2} / [ 2 (1 - b s) ], \quad \forall 0 <   s <   b ^ {- 1}. \tag {5.3}
$$

Motivated by the MGF bounds in (5.3), Boucheron et al. (2013) defines the sub-Gamma r.v. based on the right tail and left tail with variance factor  $v$  and scale factor  $b$ .

Definition 5.1 (Sub-Gamma r.v.). A centralized r.v.  $X$  is sub-Gamma with the variance factor  $v > 0$  and the scale parameter  $c > 0$  (denoted by  $X \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v, c)$ ) if

$$
\log \left(\mathrm {E} e ^ {s X}\right) \leq s ^ {2} v / \left[ 2 (1 - c | s |) \right], \quad \forall 0 <   | s | <   c ^ {- 1}. \tag {5.4}
$$

If the restriction  $0 < |s| < b^{-1}$  is replaced by one side conditions  $0 < s < b^{-1}$  (or  $0 < -s < b^{-1}$ ), we call it sub-Gamma on the right tail (or sub-Gamma on the left tail), denoted as  $\mathrm{sub}\Gamma_{+}(v,c)$  (or  $\mathrm{sub}\Gamma_{-}(v,c)$ ). In Example 5.2, the Gamma r.v.  $X \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma_{+}(ab^{2},b)$ . The (5.4) is called two-sided Bernstein's condition.

Example 5.3 (Sub-exponential r.vs). The sub-exponential distribution implies the sub-Gamma condition:  $\log (\mathrm{E}e^{sX})\leq \frac{s^2\lambda^2}{2}\leq \frac{s^2\lambda^2}{2(1 - \lambda|s|)},\forall |s| < \frac{1}{\lambda}$ . This shows that  $X\sim \mathrm{subE}(\lambda)$  implies  $X\sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma (\lambda^2,\lambda)$ .

The sub-Gamma condition (5.4) leads to the useful tail bounds and moment bounds.

Lemma 5.1 (Sub-gamma properties, Boucheron et al. (2013)). If  $X \sim \operatorname{sub}\Gamma(v, c)$ , then

$$
P (| X | > t) \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {v}{c ^ {2}} h \left(\frac {c t}{v}\right)} \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + c t}}, \tag {5.5}
$$

where  $h(u) = 1 + u - \sqrt{1 + 2|u|}$ . Moreover, we have  $P\{|X| > \sqrt{2vt} + ct\} \leq e^{-t}$ .

The tail bound in Lemma 5.1 verifies that, the sub-Gamma variable has sub-Gaussian tail behavior with parameter  $\upsilon$  for suitably small  $t$ , and it has exponential tail behavior for larger  $t$ . The proof is originated from Bennett (1962).

Proof. By Chernoff's inequality,  $P(X - \mathrm{EX} \geq t) \leq \inf_{s > 0} e^{-st} \mathrm{E}e^{s(X - \mathrm{EX})}$ . It remains to bound  $\log(e^{-st} \mathrm{E}e^{s(X - \mathrm{EX})})$  by definition of sub-Gamma variable for all  $0 < |s| < c^{-1}$

$$
\inf  _ {c ^ {- 1} \geq s > 0} \log (e ^ {- s t} E e ^ {s (X - E X)}) \leq \inf  _ {c ^ {- 1} \geq u > 0} \left(\frac {u ^ {2}}{2} \frac {v}{1 - c u} - u t\right) = - \frac {v}{c ^ {2}} h (\frac {c t}{v}) \leq - \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{v + c t},
$$

where the last inequality is from  $h(u) = 1 + u - \sqrt{1 + 2|u|} \geq \frac{u^2 / 2}{1 + u}$ . So we conclude (5.5).

Proposition 5.1 (Concentration for sub-Gamma sum). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent  $\{\mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v_i, c_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  distributed with zero mean. Define  $c = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} c_i$ , we have

(a) Closed under addition:  $S_{n}\coloneqq \sum_{i = 1}^{n}X_{i}\sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma (\sum_{i = 1}^{n}v_{i},c)$  
(b) For every  $t \geq 0$ :  $P(|S_n| \geq t) \leq 2 \exp \left(-\frac{t^2 / 2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n} v_i + ct}\right)$  and  $P\{|S_n| \geq (2t \sum_{i=1}^{n} v_i)^{1/2} + ct\} \leq 2e^{-t}$ ;  
(c) If  $X \sim \operatorname{sub}\Gamma(v, c)$ , the moments bounds satisfy for any integer  $k \geq 1$ :

$$
\operatorname {E} X ^ {k} \leq k 2 ^ {k - 2} \left[ 2 \left(\sqrt {2 v}\right) ^ {k} \Gamma \left(\frac {k}{2}\right) + c \left(\sqrt {2 v}\right) ^ {k - 1} \Gamma \left(\frac {k + 1}{2}\right) + 3 c ^ {k} \Gamma (k) \right].
$$

(d) If  $X \sim \operatorname{sub}\Gamma(v, c)$ , the even moments bounds satisfy  $\operatorname{EX}^{2k} \leq k!(8v)^k + (2k)! (4c)^{2k}$ ,  $k \geq 1$ .  
(e) If  $P\{|X| > (2tv)^{1/2} + ct\} \leq 2e^{-t}$ , then  $X \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma(32(v + 2c^2), 8c)$ .

Proof. (a) By definition of  $\{\mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v_i, c_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ , we have  $\log(\mathrm{Ee}^{sX_i}) \leq \frac{s^2}{2} \frac{v_i}{1 - c_i|s|}, \forall 0 < |s| < c^{-1}$ , from which and the independence among  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$ , thus

$$
\log \left(\operatorname {E} e ^ {s S _ {n}}\right) \leq \frac {s ^ {2}}{2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {v _ {i}}{1 - c _ {i} | s |} \leq \frac {s ^ {2}}{2} \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} v _ {i}}{1 - c | s |}, \quad \text {f o r a l l} 0 <   | s | <   c ^ {- 1}.
$$

(b) Employing Lemma 5.1, we immediately obtain (b) due to (a).  
(c) Applying the integration form of the expectation formula, it yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E X} ^ {k} \leq \operatorname {E} | X | ^ {k} = k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} x ^ {k - 1} P \{| X | > x \} d x = k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} x ^ {k - 1} P \{| X | > \sqrt {2 v t} + c t \} (\frac {\sqrt {2 v}}{2 \sqrt {t}} + c) d t \\ \leq 2 k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} (\sqrt {2 v t} + c t) ^ {k - 1} \left(\frac {\sqrt {2 v t} + 2 c t}{2 t}\right) e ^ {- t} d t = k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \left[ \left(\sqrt {2 v t} + c t\right) ^ {k} + c t \left(\sqrt {2 v t} + c t\right) ^ {k - 1} \right] \frac {e ^ {- t}}{t} d t. \\ \end{array}
$$

From (b) and inequality (3.7),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E X} ^ {k} \leq k \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \left\{2 ^ {k - 1} \left[ \left(\sqrt {2 v t}\right) ^ {k} + (c t) ^ {k} \right] + c t 2 ^ {k - 2} \left[ \left(\sqrt {2 v t}\right) ^ {k - 1} + (c t) ^ {k - 1} \right] \right\} \frac {e ^ {- t}}{t} d t \\ = k 2 ^ {k - 2} \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \left[ 2 \left(\sqrt {2 v}\right) ^ {k} t ^ {(k / 2) - 1} + c \left(\sqrt {2 v}\right) ^ {k - 1} t ^ {(k + 1) / 2 - 1} + 3 c ^ {k} t ^ {k - 1} \right] e ^ {- t} d t \\ = k 2 ^ {k - 2} \left[ 2 (\sqrt {2 v}) ^ {k} \Gamma \left(\frac {k}{2}\right) + c (\sqrt {2 v}) ^ {k - 1} \Gamma \left(\frac {k - 1}{2}\right) + 3 c ^ {k} (k - 1)! \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

(d,e) The proofs are in Theorem 2.3 of Boucheron et al. (2013).

![](images/fccc1adbf670c7f015ac5a0c589fed73d2c2b09dadaa1968255f5b33ebc67476.jpg)

Having obtained Proposition 5.1(b), from the upper bound in (5.2), we finish the proof of Proposition 5.1 by treating  $X_{i} \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma(\mathrm{Var}X_{i}/2, M/3)$  for  $i = 1, 2, \dots, n$ .

# 5.2 Bernstein's growth of moments condition

In some settings, one can not assume the r.vs being bounded. Bernstein's inequality for the sum of independent r.vs allows us to estimate the tail probability by a weaker version of an exponential condition on the growth of the  $k$ -moment without the boundedness.

Corollary 5.2 (Bernstein's inequality with the growth of moment condition). If the centred independent r.vs  $X_{1},\ldots ,X_{n}$  satisfy the growth of moments condition

$$
\left. \right. \operatorname {E} \left| X _ {i} \right| ^ {k} \leq 2 ^ {- 1} v _ {i} ^ {2} \kappa_ {i} ^ {k - 2} k!, (i = 1, 2, \dots , n), \text {f o r a l l} k \geq 2 \tag {5.6}
$$

where  $\{\kappa_i\}_{i=1}^n, \{v_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are constants independent of  $k$ . Let  $\nu_n^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n v_i^2$  (the fluctuation of sums) and  $\kappa = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \kappa_i$ . Then, we have  $X_i \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v_i, \kappa_i)$  and for  $t > 0$

$$
P \left(\left| S _ {n} \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 \nu_ {n} ^ {2} + 2 \kappa t}}, P \left(\left| S _ {n} \right| \geq \sqrt {2 \nu_ {n} ^ {2} t} + \kappa t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- t}. \tag {5.7}
$$

Proof. Given that  $\kappa_{i}|s| < 1$  for all  $i$ , (5.6) implies that  $X_{i} \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v_{i}, \kappa_{i})$  for  $1 \leq i \leq n$ .

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {s X _ {i}} \leq 1 + \frac {v _ {i} ^ {2}}{2} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} | s | ^ {k} \kappa_ {i} ^ {k - 2} = 1 + \frac {s ^ {2} v _ {i} ^ {2}}{2 (1 - | s | \kappa_ {i})} \leq e ^ {s ^ {2} v _ {i} ^ {2} / (2 - 2 \kappa_ {i} | s |)}.
$$

The independence among  $\{X_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  and Proposition 5.1(a,b) implies (5.7).

![](images/c065df1ca07c317b067e4f0c7eb002950cf6a108153defb26290c3897f5df992.jpg)

The (5.6) is also called Bernstein's moment condition. Corollary 5.2 slightly extends Lemma 2.2.11 in van der Vaart and Wellner (1996) for the case  $\kappa_i \equiv \kappa$  (a fixed number). It should be noted that (5.6) can be replaced by  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{E}|X_i|^k \leq \frac{1}{2}v^2\kappa^{k-2}k!, k=3,4,\ldots,\forall i$ , where the  $v^2$  is a variance-dependent constant such that  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{E}|X_i|^2 \leq v^2$ . Then (5.7) still holds with  $\nu_n^2 = n\nu$ , see Theorem 2.10 in Boucheron et al. (2013).

Example 5.4 (Normal r.v.). Applying the relation between MGF and moment, the  $k$ -th moment of  $X \sim N(0, \sigma^2)$  is  $\mathrm{EX}^{2k-1} = 0$ ;  $\mathrm{E}|X|^{2k} = \sigma^{2k}(2k-1)(2k-3) \cdots 3 \cdot 1 \leq 2^{-1}(2\sigma^2)\sigma^{2k-2}(2k)!$ , which satisfies (5.6) with  $v^2 = 2\sigma^2, \kappa = \sigma^2$ .

If (5.6) is changed to  $\mathrm{E}|X_i|^k \leq \tilde{v}_i^2 (2\tilde{\kappa})^{k-2}$ ,  $\tilde{v}_n^2 = \sum_{i=1}^n \tilde{v}_i^2$  ( $1 \leq i \leq n; \forall k \geq 2$ ), in this case, then we have a looser moment bound  $\mathrm{E}|X_i|^k \leq \tilde{v}_i^2 (2\tilde{\kappa})^{k-2} \leq \frac{1}{2} \cdot 2\tilde{v}_i^2 (2\tilde{\kappa})^{k-2} k!$ , which gives  $P(|S_n| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-\frac{t^2}{4\tilde{v}_n^2 + 4\tilde{\kappa}t}}$  by (5.7) in Corollary 5.2 ( $v_i^2 = 2\tilde{v}^2$ ,  $\kappa = 2\tilde{\kappa}$ ). Without using the accurate sub-Gamma conditions, Lemma 19.32 in van der Vaart (1998) shows a looser CI  $P(|S_n| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-\frac{t^2}{4\tilde{v}_n^2 + 4\tilde{\kappa}t}}$  by a different proof.

# 5.3 Concentration of exponential family without compact space

Theory and statistical applications of natural exponential family (3.5) have attracted renewed attention in the past years (Lehmann and Romano, 2006). In Lasso penalized generalized linear models (GLMs), the results of oracle inequalities lie on CIs of a quantity that can be represented as Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions (see (8.24)) related to the centralized exponential family empirical process:  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} w_i(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)$  for no-random weights  $\{w_i\}_{i=1}^n$  depending on the fixed design. Kakade et al. (2010) has studied the sub-exponential growth of the cumulants of an exponential family distribution and studied oracle inequalities of Lasso regularized GLMs, but the constant in their result is not specific.

In this part, we obtain central moments bounds with a specific constant, which gives the Bernstein's inequality for the general exponential family, and the proof is based on the Cauchy formula of higher-order derivatives for complex functions [Corollary 4.3 in Stein and Shakarchi (2010)].

Lemma 5.2 (Cauchy's derivative inequalities). If  $f$  is analytic in an open set that contains the closure of a disk  $D$  centered at  $z_0$  of radius  $0 < r < \infty$ , then  $|f^{(n)}(z_0)| \leq \frac{n!}{r^n} \sup_{z:|z - z_0| = r} |f(z)|$ .

Zhang et al. (2014) adopts a similar approach for recovering the probability mass function (p.m.f.) from the characteristic function.

It is well-known that exponential families on the natural parameter space,  $\Theta \coloneqq \{\theta \in \mathbb{R}^k : e^{b(\theta)} \coloneqq \int c(y) e^{y\theta} \mu(dy) < \infty\}$  have finite analytic (standardized) moments and cumulants, see Lemma 3.3 in Kakade et al. (2010). The natural parameter space of an exponential family is convex, see Lehmann and Romano (2006). A nice property in Lehmann's measure-theoretical statistical inference book is that:

Lemma 5.3 (Analytic property of MGF in the exponential family). The MGF  $m_{\theta_i}(s) \coloneqq \mathrm{E}_{\theta_i} e^{sX_i}$  on  $s \in \mathbb{C}$  of exponential family r.vs indexed by  $\theta_i$ , is analytic on  $\Theta$  [see Theorem 2.7.1 in Lehmann and Romano (2006) or Theorem 2 in Pistone and Wynn (1999)].

First, let us check the following lemma which is deduced by Cauchy's inequalities for the Taylor's series coefficients of a complex analytic function.

Proposition 5.2. The  $s \mapsto \bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s) \coloneqq \mathrm{E}_{\theta_i} e^{s|Y_i - \dot{b}(\theta_i)|}$  is analytic on the natural parameter space  $\Theta$  with radius  $\mathbf{r}(\Theta)$ , and the  $k$ -th absolute central moment of  $\{w_i Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  is bounded by

$$
\mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} \left| w _ {i} \left(Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}\right) \right| ^ {k} \leq \frac {k !}{2} \left(w \sqrt {2 C _ {\theta_ {i}}}\right) ^ {2} \left(w C _ {\theta_ {i}}\right) ^ {k - 2}, k = 2, 3, \dots
$$

where  $\{w_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are non-random with  $w := \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |w_i| > 0$ , and  $C_{\theta_i} := \inf_{0 < r \leq \mathbf{r}(\Theta)} r^{-1} \mathrm{E}_{\theta_i} e^{r|X_i - \dot{b}(\theta_i)|}$ .

Proof. Let  $s \in \mathbb{R}\mathrm{i} \coloneqq \{b\mathrm{i} : b \in \mathbb{R}\}$  be a given complex number on imaginary axis.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bar {m} _ {\theta_ {i}} (s) = \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} (e ^ {s [ Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) ]} 1 \{Y _ {i} \geq \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \}) + \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} (e ^ {s [ \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) - Y _ {i} ]} 1 \{Y _ {i} <   \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \}) \\ = \int_ {x \geq \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} c (x) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} + s)} e ^ {- \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} \mu (d x) + \int_ {x <   \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} c (y) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} - s)} e ^ {- \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} \mu (d x) \\ = e ^ {- \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} \left[ \int_ {x \geq \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} c (x) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} + s)} \mu (d x) + \int_ {x <   \dot {b} (\theta_ {i})} c (x) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} - s)} \mu (d x) \right]. \tag {5.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

The natural parameter space implies  $\int c(x)e^{x\theta_i}\mu(dx)$  is finite and analytic for  $\theta_i \in \Theta$ , so

$$
\int 1 \{x \geq \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \} c (x) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} + s)} \mu (d x) \text {a n d} \int 1 \{x <   \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \} c (y) e ^ {x (\theta_ {i} - s)} \mu (d x)
$$

are finite and analytic for  $s \in \mathfrak{i}, \theta_i \in \Theta$ . By Lemma 5.3,  $\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)$  in (5.8) is analytic on

$$
D _ {\theta_ {i}} := \left\{s \in \mathbf {C}: \operatorname {R e} \left(\theta_ {i} + s\right) \in \operatorname {I n t} (\Theta) \text {a n d} \operatorname {R e} \left(\theta_ {i} - s\right) \in \operatorname {I n t} (\Theta) \right\}.
$$

by using analytic continuation [i.e. the  $\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)$  has an analytic continuation from  $\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)$  on  $s \in D_{\theta_i}$  to  $\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)$  on  $s \in \mathbb{C}$ , see Corollary 4.9 in Stein and Shakarchi (2010)].

Since  $0 + \theta_{i} = \theta_{i}\in D_{\theta_{i}}\subset \mathrm{Int}(\Theta)$ ,  $\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)$  is analytic at the point 0 and hence the function is also analytic in a neighborhood of 0. By the analyticity of the functions  $\{\bar{m}_{\theta_i}(s)\}_{\theta_i\in \Theta}$  on  $s\in \operatorname {Int}(\Theta)$ , and Cauchy's derivative inequality with  $z_0 = 0$ , we have

$$
\left. \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} \mid Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \right| ^ {k} = \bar {m} _ {\theta_ {i}} ^ {(k)} (0) \leq k! r ^ {- k} \sup  _ {| s | = r} \left| \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {s \left| Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \right|} \right|, 0 <   r \leq \mathrm {r} (\Theta). \tag {5.9}
$$

Let  $s = r(\cos \omega + \mathrm{i}\sin \omega), \omega \in [0,2\pi]$ . Then, we get  $\mathrm{E}_{\theta_i}e^{s|Y_i - \dot{b}(\theta_i)|} = \mathrm{E}_{\theta_i}e^{r(\cos \omega + \mathrm{i}\sin \omega)|Y_i - \dot{b}(\theta_i)|}$

\[
= \mathrm{E}_{\theta_i}\big[e^{r\cos \omega |Y_i - \dot{b} (\theta_i)|}e^{\mathrm{i}rs\sin \omega |Y_i - \dot{b} (\theta_i)|}\big].
\]

$$
k! \frac {1}{r ^ {k}} \sup  _ {| s | = r} | \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {s | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} | \leq k! \frac {1}{r ^ {k}} \sup  _ {\omega \in [ 0, 2 \pi ]} \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r \cos \omega | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} \leq k! \frac {\mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |}}{r ^ {k}},
$$

$$
\left(\text {D u e t o} \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} \geq 1\right) = k! \left\{\frac {\left[ \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} \right] ^ {1 / k}}{r} \right\} ^ {k} \leq k! \left\{\frac {\left[ \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} \right]}{r} \right\} ^ {k}.
$$

From (5.9), it shows that by take infimum over  $0 < r \leq \mathbf{r}(\Theta)$

$$
\mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} \left| Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) \right| ^ {k} \leq k! \left\{\inf  _ {0 <   r \leq \mathrm {r} (\Theta)} r ^ {- 1} \left[ \mathrm {E} _ {\theta_ {i}} e ^ {r | Y _ {i} - \dot {b} (\theta_ {i}) |} \right] \right\} ^ {k} \leq k! C _ {\theta_ {i}} ^ {k} = \frac {k !}{2} \left(\sqrt {2 C _ {\theta_ {i}}}\right) ^ {2} C _ {\theta_ {i}} ^ {k - 2}
$$

where  $C_{\theta_i} \coloneqq \inf_{0 < r \leq \mathbf{r}(\Theta)} r^{-1}[\mathrm{E}_{\theta_i} e^{r|Y_i - \hat{b}(\theta_i)|}]$ . Then for  $\{w_i(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ , we have  $\mathrm{E}_{\theta_i}|w_i(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)|^k \leq$

$$
\frac {1}{2} k! (\sqrt {2 C _ {\theta_ {i}}}) ^ {2} C _ {\theta_ {i}} ^ {k - 2} w ^ {k} = \frac {1}{2} k! (w \sqrt {2 C _ {\theta_ {i}}}) ^ {2} (w C _ {\theta_ {i}}) ^ {k - 2}, k = 2, 3, \dots .
$$

Therefore,  $w_{i}X_{i}\sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma (w\sqrt{2C_{\theta_{i}}},wC_{\theta_{i}})$  by Proposition 5.2 and we can apply the Bernstein's inequality with the growth of moments condition to get the following concentration of exponential family on a natural parameter space.

Theorem 5.1 (Concentration of exponential family). Let  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be a sequence of independent r.vs with their densities  $\{f(y_i; \theta_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  belong to canonical exponential family (3.5) on the natural parameter space  $\theta_i \in \Theta$ . Given non-random weights  $\{w_i\}_{i=1}^n$  with  $w = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |w_i| > 0$ , then

$$
P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} \left(Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}\right) \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2}}{4 w ^ {2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} C _ {\theta_ {i}} + 2 w \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} C _ {\theta_ {i}} t}\right). \tag {5.10}
$$

Theorem 5.1 has no compact space assumption. If we impose the compact space assumption (E.1) in Proposition 3.2, it leads to the sub-Gaussian concentration as presented in Proposition 5.1. The constant  $C_{\theta_i}$  in Theorem 5.1 is hard to determine in general exponential family with infinite support. However, if the exponential family is Poisson, the  $C_{\theta_i}$  can be obtained as an explicit form.

Theorem 5.2 (Concentration for weighted Poisson summation). Let  $\{Y_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent  $\{\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  distributed. For non-random weights  $\{w_i\}_{i=1}^n$  with  $w = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |w_i| > 0$ , put  $S_n^w := \sum_{i=1}^n w_i(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)$ , then for all  $t \geq 0$

$$
P \left(\left| S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2} / 2}{w ^ {2} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {n} \lambda_ {i} + w t / 3}\right), P \left\{\left| S _ {n} ^ {w} \right| > w \left[ \left(2 t \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \lambda_ {i}\right) ^ {1 / 2} + \frac {t}{3} \right] \right\} \leq e ^ {- t}. \tag {5.11}
$$

Proof. We evaluate the log-MGF of centered Poisson r.vs  $\{Y_{i} - \mathrm{E}Y_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$

$$
\log \mathrm {E} e ^ {s w _ {i} \left(Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}\right)} = - s w _ {i} \mathrm {E} Y _ {i} + \log \mathrm {E} e ^ {s w _ {i} Y _ {i}} = - \lambda_ {i} s w _ {i} + \log e ^ {\lambda_ {i} \left(e ^ {s w _ {i}} - 1\right)} = \lambda_ {i} \left(e ^ {s w _ {i}} - s w _ {i} - 1\right).
$$

Note that, for  $s$  in a small neighbourhood of zero,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {i} \left(e ^ {s w _ {i}} - s w _ {i} - 1\right) = \lambda_ {i} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {\left(s w _ {i}\right) ^ {k}}{k !} \leq \lambda_ {i} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {\left(\left| s w \right|\right) ^ {k}}{k !} = \frac {\lambda_ {i} s ^ {2} w ^ {2}}{2} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {w ^ {k - 2}}{k (k - 1) \cdots 3} \\ \leq \frac {\lambda_ {i} s ^ {2} w ^ {2}}{2} \sum_ {k = 2} ^ {\infty} \left(\frac {| s w |}{3}\right) ^ {k - 2} = \frac {s ^ {2}}{2} \frac {w ^ {2} \lambda_ {i}}{1 - w | s | / 3}, \tag {5.12} \\ \end{array}
$$

for  $|s| \leq 3 / w$ , which implies  $w_{i}(Y_{i} - \mathrm{E}Y_{i}) \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma (w^{2}\lambda_{i},w / 3)$ . By Proposition 5.1(a), we have  $S_n^w \sim \mathrm{sub}\Gamma (w^2\sum_{k = i}^n\lambda_i,w / 3)$ . Then applying Proposition 5.1(b), we get (5.11).

Before ending this section, we show a result for checking Bernstein's moment condition by the moment recurrence condition of log-concave distributions.

Definition 5.2 (Moment recurrence condition). A r.v.  $Z$  is called moment bounded with parameter  $L > 0$  if it has recurrence condition  $\operatorname{E}|Z|^p \leq pL \cdot \operatorname{E}|Z|^{p-1}$  for any integer  $p \geq 1$ .

By the recursion relation, Definition 5.2 implies that any moment bounded r.v.  $Z$  satisfies  $\mathrm{E}|Z|^p \leq p!L^p$ . Hence, the tails of its moment bounded r.vs decay as the Bernstein's growth of moment condition. So the constant  $C_{\theta_i}$  in Theorem 5.1 is relatively easy to find. Lemmas 7.2, 7.3, 7.6 and 7.7 in Schudy and Sviridenko (2012) showed that any log-concave continuous distribution (see Section 3.4) and log-concave discrete distribution  $X$  with density  $f$  is moment bounded with parameter  $L \propto \mathrm{E}|X|$ .

Example 5.5 (Log-concave continuous distributions, Bagnoli and Bergstrom (2005)). Many continuous distributions, such as normal distribution, exponential distribution, uniform distribution over any convex set, logistic distribution, extreme value distribution, chi-square distribution, chi distribution, hyperbolic secant distribution, Laplace distribution, Weibull distribution (the shape parameter  $\theta \geq 1$ ), Gamma distribution (the shape parameter  $a \geq 1$ ) and Beta distribution (both shape parameters are  $\geq 1$ ) have log-concave continuous densities.

Analogous to the log-concave continuous function in (3.12), we can define log-concave sequence for the p.m.f. of discrete r.v., which also has Bernstein-type concentrations.

Definition 5.3 (Log-concave discrete distributions). A sequence  $\{p_i\}_{i\in \mathbb{Z}}$  (or  $\{p_i\}_{i\in \mathbb{N}}$ ) is said to be log-concave if  $p_{i + 1}^2\geq p_ip_{i + 2}$  for all  $i\in \mathbb{Z}$  (or  $i\in \mathbb{N}$ ). An integer-valued r.v.  $X$  is log-concave if its probability mass function (p.m.f.)  $p_i\coloneqq P(X = i)$  is log-concave sequence.

Example 5.6 (Log-concave discrete distributions). Bernoulli and binomial distributions, Poisson distribution, geometric distribution, and negative binomial distribution (with number of success  $>1$ ) and hypergeometric distribution have log-concave integer-valued p.m.f., see Johnson et al. (2005).

# 6 Sub-Weibull distributions

# 6.1 Sub-Weibull r.vs and  $\psi_{\theta}$ -norm

A r.v. is heavy-tailed if its distribution function  $F(\cdot)$  fails to be bounded by a decreasing exponential function (Foss et al., 2011),

$$
\int e ^ {\lambda x} d F (x) = \infty , \forall \lambda > 0 (\text {t h e t a i l d e c a y s s l o w e r t h a n s o m e e x p o n e n t i a l r . v . s}).
$$

We first give a simple example of the heavy-tailed distributions arisen by multiplying sub-Gaussian r.vs. The proof is motivated by Lemmas 2.7.7 of Vershynin (2018).

Lemma 6.1 (The product of sub-Gaussians). Suppose  $\{X^{(m)}\}_{m = 1}^{d}$  are sub-Gaussian (may be dependent). Then  $\prod_{m = 1}^{d}|X^{(m)}|^{2 / d}$  is sub-exponential and  $\| \prod_{m = 1}^{d}[X^{(m)}]^{2 / d}\|_{\psi_1}\leq \prod_{m = 1}^{d}\| X^{(m)}\|_{\psi_2}^{2 / d}$

Proof. By the definition of sub-Gaussian norm,  $\mathrm{Ee}^{|X^{(m)}| / \| X^{(m)}\|_{\psi_2}|^2} \leq 2$ ,  $m = 1,2,\dots ,d$ . Applying the elementary inequality  $\prod_{m = 1}^{d}a_{m} \leq \frac{1}{d}\sum_{m = 1}^{d}a_{m}^{d}$ , we get by Jensen's inequality

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\prod_ {m = 1} ^ {d} \left[ | X ^ {(m)} | ^ {2 / d} / \| X ^ {(m)} \| _ {\psi_ {2}} ^ {2 / d} \right]} \leq \mathrm {E} e ^ {\frac {1}{d} \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {d} \left[ X ^ {(m)} / \| X ^ {(m)} \| _ {\psi_ {2}} \right] ^ {2}} \leq \frac {1}{d} \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {d} \mathrm {E} e ^ {\left[ X ^ {(m)} / \| X ^ {(m)} \| _ {\psi_ {2}} \right] ^ {2}} \leq 2. \tag {6.1}
$$

The proof is finished by the definition of the sub-exponential norm.

In probability, Weibull r.vs are generated from the power of the exponential r.vs.

Example 6.1 (Weibull r.vs). The Weibull r.v.  $X \in \mathbb{R}^{+}$  is defined by its survival function

$P(X \geq x) = e^{-bx^{\theta}} (x \geq 0)$  for the scale parameter  $b > 0$  and the shape parameter  $\theta > 0$ .

Sub-Weibull distribution is characterized by the right tail of the Weibull distribution and is a generalization of both sub-Gaussian and sub-exponential distributions.

Definition 6.1 (Sub-Weibull distributions). A r.v.  $X$  satisfying  $P(|X| \geq x) \leq ae^{-bx^{\theta}}$  for given  $a, b, \theta > 0$ , is called a sub-Weibull r.v. with tail parameter  $\theta$  (denoted by  $X \sim \mathrm{subW}(\theta)$ ).

A subW( $\theta$ )'s tail is no heavier than that of a Weibull r.v. with tail parameter  $\theta$ . It is emphasized that  $X \sim \mathrm{subW}(\theta)$  r.vs with  $\theta < 1$  belongs to heavy-tailed r.vs. Recently, the Weibull-like tail condition is also studied in high-dimensional statistics and random matrix theory [see Tao and Vu (2013), Kuchibhotla and Chakraborty (2022) and Wong et al. (2020)]. Götze et al. (2021) names subW( $\theta$ ) as  $\theta$ -sub-exponential r.v. There are 4 equivalent conditions to reveal the sub-Weibull tail condition which is useful in applications.

Corollary 6.1 (Characterizations of sub-Weibull condition). Let  $X$  be a r.v.. Then the following properties are equivalent.

(1) The tails of  $X$  satisfy  $P(|X| \geq x) \leq e^{-(x / K_1)^\theta}$ , for all  $x \geq 0$ .  
(2) The moments of  $X$  satisfy  $\| X\| _k\coloneqq (\operatorname {E}|X|^k)^{1 / k}\leq K_2k^{1 / \theta}$  for all  $k\geq 1\wedge \theta$  
(3) The MGF of  $|X|^{1 / \theta}$  satisfies  $\mathrm{E}e^{\lambda^{1 / \theta}|X|^{1 / \theta}} \leq e^{\lambda^{1 / \theta}K_3^{1 / \theta}}$  for  $|\lambda| \leq \frac{1}{K_3}$ ;  
(4) The MGF of  $|X|^{1 / \theta}$  is bounded at some point:  $\mathrm{Ee}^{|X / K_4|^{1 / \theta}} \leq 2$ .

The proof can be founded in Wong et al. (2020), Vladimirova et al. (2020) by mimicking the proof of Proposition 2.5.2 in Vershynin (2018). It follows from Corollary 6.1(4) that  $X$  is sub-Weibull with tail parameter  $\theta$  if and only if  $|X|^{1 / \theta}$  is sub-exponential.

Let  $\theta_{1}$  and  $\theta_{2}$  be two sub-Weibull parameters. Corollary 6.1 implies  $\mathrm{subW}(\theta_1)\subset$ $\mathrm{subW}(\theta_2)$ . for  $0 < \theta_{2}\leq \theta_{1}$ . The following Orlicz-type norms play crucial roles in deriving tail and maximal inequality for sub-Weibull r.vs without the zero-mean assumption.

Definition 6.2 (Sub-Weibull norm or  $\psi_{\theta}$ -norm). Let  $\psi_{\theta}(x) = e^{x^{\theta}} - 1$ . The sub-Weibull norm of  $X$  for any  $\theta > 0$  is defined as  $\| X\|_{\psi_{\theta}} := \inf \{C \in (0, \infty) : \operatorname{Ee}^{|X|^{\theta} / C^{\theta}} \leq 2\}$ .

From Corollary 6.1(4), a second useful definition of sub-Weibull r.vs is the r.vs with finite  $\psi_{\theta}$ -norm. Sub-Weibull norm is a special case of the Orlicz norm (Wellner, 2017).

Definition 6.3 (Orlicz norms). Let  $g \colon [0,\infty) \to [0,\infty)$  be a non-decreasing convex function with  $g(0) = 0$ . The "g-Orlicz norm" of a r.v.  $X$  is  $\| X\|_g \coloneqq \inf \{\eta > 0 : \operatorname{E}[g(|X| / \eta)] \leq 1\}$ .

Let  $g(x) = e^{x^{\theta}} - 1$  and  $\operatorname{E}[g(|X| / \eta)] \leq 1$  implies  $\operatorname{E}[\exp (|X|^{\theta} / \eta^{\theta})] \leq 2$ , which is the definition of sub-Weibull norm. Similar to sub-exponential, Zajkowski (2019), Wong et al. (2020), Vladimirova et al. (2020) attained the following.

Corollary 6.2 (Properties of sub-Weibull norm). If  $\mathrm{Ee}^{|X| / \| X\|_{\psi_\theta}|^\theta} \leq 2$ , then (a).  $P(|X| > t) \leq 2e^{-(t / \| X\|_{\psi_\theta})^\theta}$  for all  $t \geq 0$ ; (b). Moment bounds:  $\mathrm{E}|X|^k \leq 2\| X\|_{\psi_\theta}^k\Gamma (\frac{k}{\theta} +1)$ .

A sharpen version of Corollary 6.2 is presented as follows.

Corollary 6.3 (Sharper moment bounds, Corollary 3 in Zhang and Wei (2022)). Let  $C_{\theta} \coloneqq \max_{k \geq 1} \left( \frac{2\sqrt{2\pi}}{\theta} \right)^{1/k} \left( \frac{k}{\theta} \right)^{1/(2k)}$ , we have  $(\operatorname{E}|X|^k)^{1/k} \leq C_{\theta} (\theta e^{11/12})^{-1/\theta} \| X \|_{\psi_\theta} k^{1/\theta}$  for all  $k \geq 1$ .

Via a power transform of sub-Weibull r.v., the next corollary explains the relation of sub-Weibull norm with parameter  $\theta$  and  $r\theta$ , which is similar to Lemma 6.1 for subexponential norm.

Corollary 6.4 (Corollary 4 in Zhang and Wei (2022)). For any  $\theta, r \in (0,\infty)$ , if  $X \sim \mathrm{subW}(\theta)$  then  $|X|^r \sim \mathrm{subW}(\theta / r)$ . Moreover,

$$
\left\| \left\| X \right\| ^ {r} \right\| _ {\psi_ {\theta / r}} = \left\| X \right\| _ {\psi_ {\theta}} ^ {r}. \tag {6.2}
$$

Conversely, if  $X \sim \operatorname{subW}(r\theta)$ , then  $X^r \sim \operatorname{subW}(\theta)$  with  $\| X^r \|_{\psi_\theta} = \| X \|_{\psi_{r\theta}}^r$ .

Lemma 6.1 and Corollary 6.4 can be extended to product of r.vs, and we state it as the following corollary.

Corollary 6.5 (Proposition D.2 in Kuchibhotla and Chakraborty (2022)). If  $\{W_i\}_{i=1}^d$  are (possibly dependent) r.vs satisfying  $\| W_i \|_{\psi_{\alpha_i}} < \infty$  for some  $\alpha_i > 0$ , then

$$
\| \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {d} W _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {\beta}} \leq \prod_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \| W _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {\alpha_ {i}}} \text {w h e r e} \frac {1}{\beta} := \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {1}{\alpha_ {i}}.
$$

# 6.2 Concentrations for sub-Weibull summation

The Chernoff inequality tricks in the derivation of Corollary 4.2 for sub-exponential concentration is not valid for sub-Weibull distributions, since the exponential moment equivalent conditions of sub-Weibull are on the absolute value  $|X|$ . However, Bernstein's moment condition is the exponential moment of the absolute value. An alternative method is given by Kuchibhotla and Chakraborty (2022), who defines the so-called Generalized Bernstein-Orlicz (GBO) norm. Fixed  $\alpha > 0$  and  $L \geq 0$ , define a function  $\Psi_{\theta,L}(\cdot)$  with its inverse function  $\Psi_{\theta,L}^{-1}(t) \coloneqq \sqrt{\log(t + 1)} + L[\log(t + 1)]^{1/\theta} \forall t \geq 0$ . A promising development is that the following GBO norm helps us derive tail behaviors for sub-Weibull r.vs.

Definition 6.4 (Generalized Bernstein-Orlicz Norm). The generalized Bernstein-Orlicz (GBO) norm of a r.v.  $X$  is then given by:  $\| X\|_{\Psi_{\theta ,L}}\coloneqq \inf \{\eta >0:\operatorname {E}[\Psi_{\theta ,L}(|X| / \eta)]\leq 1\}$

The monotone function  $\Psi_{\theta ,L}(\cdot)$  is motivated by the classical Bernstein's inequality for sub-exponential r.vs. Like the sub-Weibull norm properties Corollary 6.2(a), the following proposition in Kuchibhotla and Chakraborty (2022) allows us to get the concentration inequality for r.vs with finite GBO norms.

Corollary 6.6 (GBO norm concentration). For any r.v.  $X$  with  $\| X\|_{\Psi_{\theta ,L}} < \infty$ , we have  $P(|X|\geq \| X\|_{\Psi_{\theta ,L}}\{\sqrt{t} +Lt^{1 / \theta}\})\leq 2e^{-t}$ , for all  $t\geq 0$ .

From Corollary 6.6, it is easy to derive the concentration inequality for a single sub-Weibull r.v. or even the sum of independent sub-Weibull r.vs. Theorem 3.1 in Kuchibhotla and Chakraborty (2022) obtains an upper bound for the GBO norm of the summation.

Corollary 6.7 (Concentration for sub-Weibull summation). If  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  are independent centralized r.vs such that  $\| X_i\|_{\psi_\theta} < \infty$  for all  $1 \leq i \leq n$  and some  $\theta > 0$ , then for any weight vector  $\boldsymbol{w} = (w_1, \ldots, w_n) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we have  $\|\sum_{i=1}^n w_i X_i\|_{\Psi_{\theta, L_n(\theta)}} \leq 2eC(\theta)\| \boldsymbol{b}\|_2$  and

$$
P \left(\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} w _ {i} X _ {i} \right| \geq 2 e C (\theta) \| \boldsymbol {b} \| _ {2} \left\{\sqrt {t} + L _ {n} (\theta) t ^ {1 / \theta} \right\}\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- t} \tag {6.3}
$$

where  $\pmb{b} = (w_{1}\| X_{1}\|_{\psi_{\theta}},\dots ,w_{n}\| X_{i}\|_{\psi_{\theta}})^{T}\in \mathbb{R}^{n},L_{n}(\theta)\coloneqq \frac{\pmb{4}^{1 / \theta}}{\sqrt{2}\|\pmb{b}\|_{2}}\times \left\{ \begin{array}{ll}\| \pmb {b}\|_{\infty}, & \text{if}\theta < 1,\\ 4e\| \pmb {b}\|_{\frac{\theta}{1 - \theta}} / C(\theta), & \text{if}\theta \geq 1 \end{array} \right.$

$$
\text {a n d} C (\theta) := \max  \{\sqrt {2}, 2 ^ {1 / \theta} \} \times \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \sqrt {8} e ^ {3} (2 \pi) ^ {1 / 4} e ^ {1 / 2 4} (e ^ {2 / e} / \theta) ^ {1 / \theta}, & \text {i f} \theta <   1, \\ 4 e + 2 (\log 2) ^ {1 / \theta}, & \text {i f} \theta \geq 1. \end{array} \right.
$$

The upper bound of sub-Weibull norm for summation provided by Corollary 6.7 depends on  $\| X_i\|_{\psi_\theta}$  and the  $w$ . Zhang and Wei (2022) gives a sharper version of Corollary 6.7. The  $\theta = 1$  is the phrase transition point, and reflect the fact that Weibull r.vs are log-convex for  $\theta \leq 1$  and log-concave for  $\theta \geq 1$ . At last, we mention a generalized Hanson-Wright inequality for sub-Weibull r.vs in Proposition 1.5 of Götze et al. (2021). Let  $\max_{i=1,\dots,n} \| (a_{ij})_j \|_2 := \| A \|_{2 \to \infty}$  where  $\| A \|_{p \to q} := \sup \{ \| Ax \|_q : \| x \|_p \leq 1 \}$ .

Corollary 6.8 (Concentration for the quadratic form of sub-Weibull r.vs.). Let  $q \in \mathbb{N}, A = (a_{ij})$  be a symmetric  $n \times n$  matrix and let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent and centered r.vs with  $\| X_i \|_{\Psi_{2/q}} \leq M$  and  $\mathrm{EX}_i^2 = \sigma_i^2$ . We have  $P(|\sum_{i,j} a_{ij} X_i X_j - \sum_{i=1}^n \sigma_i^2 a_{ii}| \geq t) \leq 2e^{-\eta(A, q, t / M^2) / C}, \forall t \geq 0$ , where  $\eta(A, q, t) := \min(\frac{t^2}{\|A\|_F^2}, \frac{t}{\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}}, (\frac{t}{\max_{i=1,\dots,n} (a_{ij})_j \|_2})^{\frac{2}{q+1}}, (\frac{t}{\|A\|_\infty)^{\frac{1}{q}})$  and  $C$  is a constant.

# 7 Concentration for Extremes

The CIs presented so far only concern with linear combinations of independent r.vs or Lipschitz function of random vectors. In many statistics applications, we have to control the maximum of the  $n$  r.vs when deriving the error bounds, while these r.vs may be arbitrarily dependent. This section is developed on advanced proof skills. So we present the proofs even for existing results, which are applications of CIs in a probability aspect.

# 7.1 Maximal inequalities

This section presents the maximal inequalities for r.vs.  $\{X_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  which may not be independent. In the theory of empirical process, it is of interest to bound  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_i|$  [Section 2.2, van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)]. If  $\{X_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  are arbitrary sequences of real-valued r.vs and have finite  $r$ -th moments  $(r\geq 1)$ , Aven (1985) gives a crude upper bounds for  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1\leq i\leq n}X_{i}$  by Jensen's inequality

$$
\mathrm {E} \left\{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \left| X _ {i} \right| ^ {r} \right\} ^ {1 / r} \leq \left\{\operatorname {E} \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \left| X _ {i} \right| ^ {r} \right\} ^ {1 / r} \leq \left\{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} \left| X _ {i} \right| ^ {r} \right\} ^ {1 / r} \leq n ^ {1 / r} \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \left(\mathrm {E} \left| X _ {i} \right| ^ {r}\right) ^ {1 / r} \tag {7.1}
$$

Page314 of van der Vaart (1998) mentions a sharper version of (7.1) without the proof. In below, we introduce the proof by the truncation technique.

Corollary 7.1 (Sharper maximal inequality). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be identically distributed but not necessarily independent and assume  $\operatorname{E}(|X_1|^p) < \infty, (p \geq 1)$ . Then,  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_i| = o(n^{1/p})$ .

Proof. Let  $M_{n} \coloneqq \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_{i}|$ . For any  $\epsilon > 0$ , we truncate  $M_{n}$  by  $\epsilon n^{1/p}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {E M} _ {n} = \int_ {0} ^ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} P (M _ {n} > t) d t + \int_ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} ^ {\infty} P (M _ {n} > t) d t \leq \int_ {0} ^ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} 1 d t + \int_ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} ^ {\infty} n P (| X _ {1} | > t) d t \\ = \epsilon n ^ {1 / p} + n ^ {1 / p} \int_ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} ^ {\infty} n ^ {(p - 1) / p} P (| X _ {1} | > t) d t \leq \epsilon n ^ {1 / p} + \frac {n ^ {1 / p}}{\epsilon^ {p - 1}} \int_ {\epsilon n ^ {1 / p}} ^ {\infty} t ^ {p - 1} P (| X _ {1} | > t) d t. \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus, by dividing  $n^{1/p}$  we have  $\frac{\mathrm{EM}_n}{n^{1/p}} \leq \epsilon + \frac{1}{\epsilon^{p-1}} \int_{\epsilon n^{1/p}}^{\infty} t^{p-1} P(|X_1| > t) dt = \epsilon + o(1)$ , where we adopt the fact  $\int_{\epsilon n^{1/p}}^{\infty} t^{p-1} P(|X_1| > t) dt = o(1)$  from moment condition:  $\mathrm{E}|X_1|^p < \infty$ . Finally, it implies that  $\limsup_{n \to \infty} \frac{\mathrm{EM}_n}{n^{1/p}} \leq \epsilon$ , which gives  $\mathrm{EM}_n = o(n^{1/p})$  by letting  $\epsilon \to 0$ .

Example 7.1 (Pareto distribution). For instance, let  $\{Z_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be IID Pareto r.v.s with the density function  $f(x) = \nu a^\nu x^{-\nu - 1} \cdot 1_{\{x \geq k\}}$ ,  $(k > 0, \nu > 0)$ , and  $E Z_i^{\nu - \epsilon} < \infty$  for small  $\epsilon \in (0, \nu)$ . Corollary 7.1 gives  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq n} Z_i = o(n^{1 / (\nu - \epsilon)})$ . Malik (1966) showed that  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq n} Z_i = a^r \frac{\Gamma(n + 1) \Gamma(1 - \frac{r}{v})}{\Gamma(n + 1 - \frac{r}{v})} = O(n^{1 / \nu})$  by using Stirling's approximation of gamma function.

Corollary 7.1 reveals that  $\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_i|$  diverges at rate slower than  $n^{1 / r}$  under the  $r$ -th moment condition. As  $r$  increases, it will slow down the divergence rate of the maxima. If we have arbitrary finite  $r$ -th moment conditions (such as Gaussian distribution), it means that the divergence rate of maxima is slower than any polynomial rate  $n^{1 / r}$ . This suggests that the rate may be logarithmic. With the sub-Gaussian assumptions, the logarithmic divergence rate is possible and the proof is based on controlling the expectation of the supremum of variables, from the argument in Pisier (1983).

Corollary 7.2 (Sub-Gaussian maximal inequality, Rigollet and Hütter (2023)). Let  $\{X_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  be r.vs (without independence assumption) such that  $X_{i}\sim \mathrm{subG}(\sigma^{2})$ . Then,

(a)  $\operatorname{E}\left[\max_{1 < i < n} X_i\right] \leq \sigma \sqrt{2 \log n}$  and  $\operatorname{E}\left[\max_{1 < i < n} |X_i|\right] \leq \sigma \sqrt{2 \log(2n)}$ .  
(b)  $P\left(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} X_i > t\right) \leq n e^{-\frac{t^2}{2\sigma^2}}$  and  $P\left(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_i| > t\right) \leq 2n e^{-\frac{t^2}{2\sigma^2}}$ .

Proof. (a) By the property of maximum, sub-Gaussian MGF and Jensen's inequality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname * {E} _ {1 \leq i \leq n} X _ {i} = \inf  _ {s > 0} s ^ {- 1} \operatorname {E} \log e ^ {s \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} X _ {i}} \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} s ^ {- 1} \log E e ^ {s \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} X _ {i}} \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} s ^ {- 1} \log \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} E e ^ {s X _ {i}} \leq \inf  _ {s > 0} s ^ {- 1} \log \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} e ^ {\frac {\sigma^ {2} s ^ {2}}{2}} \\ = \inf  _ {s > 0} \left(\frac {\log n}{s} + \frac {\sigma^ {2} s}{2}\right) = \sigma \sqrt {2 \log n} [ S e t t i n g s = \sqrt {\frac {2 \log n}{\sigma^ {2}}} a s t h e o p t i m a l b o u n d. ] \\ \end{array}
$$

Let  $Y_{2i-1} = X_i$  and  $Y_{2i} = -X_i (1 \leq i \leq n)$ . It gives  $\operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_i| = \operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq n} \max \{X_i, -X_i\} = \operatorname{Emax}_{1 \leq i \leq 2n} Y_i$ . The previous result for sample size  $2n$  finishes the proof of the second part.

(b) By Chernoff inequality and the sub-Gaussian MGF, we have  $P(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} X_i > t) \leq \inf_{s > 0} e^{-st} \operatorname{E} e^{s \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} X_i} \leq \inf_{s > 0} e^{-st} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \operatorname{E} e^{sX_i} \leq \inf_{s > 0} ne^{-st + \frac{\sigma^2 s^2}{2}} = \frac{s = t / \sigma^2}{2\sigma^2} = ne^{-\frac{t^2}{2\sigma^2}}$ . For the second part, note that  $P(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_i| > t) = P(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} X_i > t, \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} -X_i \geq t) \leq 2P(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} X_i > t)$ .

The Corollary 7.2 (b) implies a tail bound for maximum of sub-Gaussian random variables for  $t > 0$

$$
P \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} X _ {i} \leq \sigma \sqrt {2 (\log n + t)}\right) \geq 1 - \exp \{- t \}.
$$

By a similar proof, Corollary 7.2 can be extended to other r.vs, such as sub-Gamma r.vs and r.vs characterized by sub-Weibull norm (or Orlicz norm) as presented before.

Corollary 7.3 (Concentration for maximum of sub-Gamma r.vs). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent zero-mean  $\{\mathrm{sub}\Gamma(v_i, c_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ . Then, for  $\max_{i=1,\dots,n} v_i := v$  and  $\max_{i=1,\dots,n} c_i := c$ ,

$$
\operatorname {E} \left(\max  _ {i = 1, \dots , n} | X _ {i} |\right) \leq \left[ 2 v \log (2 n) \right] ^ {1 / 2} + c \log (2 n).
$$

(See Theorem 3.1.10 in Giné and Nickl (2016) for the proof of Corollary 7.3.)

In bellow, based on the sub-Weibull norm condition, a fundamental result due to Pisier (1983) is given for obtaining the divergence rate of the maxima of sub-Weibull r.vs.

Corollary 7.4 (Maximal inequality for sub-Weibull r.vs). For  $\theta \geq 1$ , consider the sub-Weibull norm  $\| X\|_{\psi_\theta} \coloneqq \inf_{C \in (0,\infty)} \{Ee^{|X|^{\theta} / C^\theta} \leq 2\}$  for  $\psi_\theta(x) = e^{x^\theta} - 1$ . For any r.vs  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$ ,

$$
\operatorname {E} \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} | X _ {i} |\right) \leq \psi_ {\theta} ^ {- 1} (n) \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| X _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {\theta}} = (\log (1 + n)) ^ {1 / \theta} \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| X _ {i} \| _ {\psi_ {\theta}}. \tag {7.2}
$$

If the function  $\psi_{\theta}(x)$  is replaced by any non-decreasing convex function  $g(x)$  with  $g(0) = 0$  in the definition of Orlicz norm:  $\| X\| _g\coloneqq \inf \{\eta >0:\operatorname {E}[g(|X| / \eta)]\leq 1\}$ , then

$$
\operatorname {E} \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} | X _ {i} |\right) \leq g ^ {- 1} (n) \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| X _ {i} \| _ {g} \quad \text {f o r f i n i t e} \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| X _ {i} \| _ {g}.
$$

Proof. From Jensen's inequality, for  $C \in (0,\infty)$  and  $\psi_{\theta}(x) = e^{x^{\theta}} - 1$  we get

$$
\psi_ {\theta} \left[ \mathrm {E} \left(\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} | X _ {i} | / C\right) \right] \leq \mathrm {E} \left[ \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \psi_ {\theta} (| X _ {i} | / C) \right] \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} \psi_ {\theta} (| X _ {i} | / C) \leq n \tag {7.3}
$$

where the last inequality is by the definition of sub-Weibull norm:  $\mathrm{E}\psi_{\theta}(|X_i| / C)\leq 1$

Let  $C = \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} \| X_i \|_{\psi_\theta}$ . Applying the non-decreasing property of  $\psi_\theta(x)$  (so does its inverse  $\psi_\theta^{-1}(x)$ ), the (7.3) implies  $\operatorname{E}(\max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |X_i| / C) \leq \psi_\theta^{-1}(n)$  by operating the map  $\psi_\theta^{-1}$ , and so we have (7.2). The derivation of Orlicz norm case is the same.

By Hoeffding's lemma, the following results on the maxima of the sum of independent r.vs, is useful for bounding empirical processes.

Corollary 7.5 (Maximal inequality for bounded r.vs, Lemma 14.14 in Buhlmann and van de Geer (2011)). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent r.vs on  $\mathcal{X}$  and  $\{f_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be real-valued functions on  $\mathcal{X}$  which satisfy  $\operatorname{E}f_j(X_i) = 0$ ,  $|f_j(X_i)| \leq a_{ij}$  for all  $j = 1, \dots, p$  and all  $i = 1, \dots, n$ . Then

$$
\operatorname {E} \left(\max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \mid \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f _ {j} (X _ {i}) \mid\right) \leq \left[ 2 \log (2 p) \right] ^ {1 / 2} \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

Proof. Let  $V_{j} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} f_{j}(X_{i})$ . By Jensen's inequality and Hoeffding's lemma

$$
\operatorname * {E} _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | V _ {j} | = \frac {1}{\lambda} \operatorname {E} \log e ^ {\lambda \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | V _ {j} |} \leq \frac {1}{\lambda} \log E e ^ {\lambda \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | V _ {j} |} \leq \frac {1}{\lambda} \log \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} E e ^ {\lambda | V _ {j} |}
$$

$$
\left[ \text {C o r o l l a r y} 2. 1 \right] \leq \frac {1}{\lambda} \log \left[ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} 2 e ^ {\frac {1}{2} \lambda^ {2} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} ^ {2}} \right] \leq \frac {1}{\lambda} \log \left[ 2 p e ^ {\frac {1}{2} \lambda^ {2} \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} a _ {i j} ^ {2}} \right] = \frac {1}{\lambda} \log (2 p) + \frac {1}{2} \lambda \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} ^ {2}.
$$

$$
\mathrm {T h e} \operatorname * {E} _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | V _ {j} | \leq \inf _ {\lambda > 0} \{\frac {1}{\lambda} \log (2 p) + \frac {1}{2} \lambda \max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} ^ {2} \} = \sqrt {2 \log (2 p)} \max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} (\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} ^ {2}) ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

If Hoeffding's lemma for moment is replaced by Bernstein's moment conditions, then the maximal inequality for the sum of independent bounded r.v.s in Corollary 7.5 can be extended to Bernstein's moment conditions. We give a modified version of Corollary 14.1 in Buhlmann and van de Geer (2011) based on truncated Jensen's inequality.

Proposition 7.1 (Maximal inequality with Bernstein's moment conditions). If  $\{X_{ij}\}, j = 1, \ldots, p$  are read-valued independent variables across  $i = 1, \ldots, n$ . Assume  $\mathrm{EX}_{ij} = 0$  and Bernstein's moment conditions:  $\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \mathrm{E}|X_{ij}|^k \leq \frac{1}{2} v^2 \kappa^{k-2} k!$ ,  $k = 2, 3, \ldots, \forall j$ . Then,

$$
\operatorname {E} \big (\max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} | ^ {m} \big) \leq [ \frac {\kappa \log (2 p)}{n} + (v ^ {2} + 1) \sqrt {\frac {\log (2 p)}{n}} ] ^ {m}, \forall 1 \leq m \leq 1 + \log p a n d p \geq 2.
$$

Proof. Let  $M_{n,m} = \max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}X_{ij}\right|^{m}$ . First, we show for any r.v.  $X$  and all  $m\geq 1$ ,

$$
\mathrm {E} | X | ^ {m} \leq \log^ {m} \left(\mathrm {E} e ^ {| X |} - 1 + e ^ {m - 1}\right). \tag {7.4}
$$

The function  $g(x) = \log^m (x + 1), x \geq 0$  is concave for all  $x \geq e^{m - 1} - 1$ . By the truncated Jensen's inequality in Lemma 2.5 with  $Z \coloneqq e^{|X|} - 1$ ,  $c = e^{m - 1} - 1$ , we have

$$
\operatorname {E} | X | ^ {m} = \operatorname {E} \log^ {m} (e ^ {| X |} - 1 + 1) \leq \log^ {m} [ \operatorname {E} (e ^ {| X |} - 1) + 1 + (e ^ {m - 1} - 1) ] = \log^ {m} [ \operatorname {E} (e ^ {| X |} - 1) + e ^ {m - 1} ].
$$

Then for all  $L, m > 0$ ,

$$
\left(\frac {L}{n}\right) ^ {- m} \mathrm {E} M _ {n, m} \leq \log^ {m} \left[ \mathrm {E} e ^ {\max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} / L \right|} - 1 + e ^ {m - 1} \right] \leq \log^ {m} \left[ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} \mathrm {E} \left(e ^ {\left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} / L \right|} - 1\right) + e ^ {m - 1} \right]. \tag {7.5}
$$

Therefore, it is sufficient to bound  $\mathrm{E}e^{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n} X_{ij}\right| / L}$  uniformly in  $j$ .

Second. To bound the MGF in (7.5), then we show that for any real-valued r.v.  $X$ ,

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {X} \leq e ^ {\mathrm {E} e ^ {| X |} - 1 - \mathrm {E} | X |}, \text {w i t h} \mathrm {E} X = 0. \tag {7.6}
$$

Indeed, for any  $c > 0$ , we have  $e^{X - c} - 1 \leq \frac{e^{|X|}}{1 + c} - 1 = \frac{e^{X} - 1 - X + X - c}{1 + c} \leq \frac{e^{|X|} - 1 - |X| + X - c}{1 + c}$ . Let  $c = \operatorname{Ee}^{|X|} - 1 - \operatorname{E}|X|$ . Note that  $\operatorname{E}X = 0$ , so  $\operatorname{Ee}^{X - c} - 1 \leq \frac{\operatorname{Ee}^{|X|} - 1 - \operatorname{E}|X| - c}{1 + c} = 0$ . Hence  $\log (\operatorname{Ee}^X) \leq c$ .

Using Taylor's expansion, the (7.6) and  $e^{|x|} \leq e^x + e^{-x}$  give

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} | / L} - 1 \leq \mathrm {E} e ^ {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} / L} + \mathrm {E} e ^ {- \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} / L} - 1 \leq 2 e ^ {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} (e ^ {| X _ {i j} | / L} - 1 - | X _ {i j} | / L)} - 1 = 2 e ^ {\sum_ {m = 2} ^ {\infty} \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} | X _ {i j} | ^ {m}}{L ^ {m} m !}} - 1
$$

$$
[ \text {B y} ] \leq 2 e ^ {\frac {n v ^ {2}}{2 L ^ {2}} \sum_ {m = 2} ^ {\infty} (\kappa / L) ^ {m - 2}} - 1 = 2 e ^ {\frac {n v ^ {2}}{2 L ^ {2} (1 - \kappa / L)}} - 1. \tag {7.7}
$$

Combining (7.7) and (7.5), we obtain for  $L > \kappa \eqqcolon L - \sqrt{n / 2\log(p + e^{m - 1})}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {E M} _ {n, m} \leq (\frac {L}{n}) ^ {m} \log^ {m} [ p (2 e ^ {\frac {n v ^ {2}}{2 L ^ {2} (1 - \kappa / L)}} - 1) + e ^ {m - 1} ] \leq (\frac {L}{n}) ^ {m} \log^ {m} [ (p + e ^ {m - 1}) e ^ {\frac {n v ^ {2}}{2 (L ^ {2} - L \kappa)}} ] \\ = (\frac {L \log (p + e ^ {m - 1})}{n} + \frac {v ^ {2} L}{2 (L ^ {2} - L \kappa)}) ^ {m} = (\frac {\kappa \log (p + e ^ {m - 1})}{n} + \sqrt {\frac {\log (p + e ^ {m - 1})}{2 n}} + \frac {v ^ {2}}{2 (L - \kappa)}) ^ {m} \\ \leq [ \frac {\kappa \log (p + e ^ {m - 1})}{n} + (v ^ {2} + 1) \sqrt {\frac {\log (p + e ^ {m - 1})}{2 n}} ] ^ {m} \leq [ \frac {\kappa \log (2 p)}{n} + (v ^ {2} + 1) \sqrt {\frac {\log (2 p)}{2 n}} ] ^ {m}. \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second and last inequality is by  $\frac{v^2}{L - \kappa} = [\sqrt{\frac{n}{2\log(p + e^{m - 1})}}]^{-1}v^2$  and  $e^{m - 1}\leq p$

In order to derive some error bounds, one has to control the maximum of the norm of  $n$  sub-Gaussian random vectors as an application of the tail inequality for quadratic forms of sub-Gaussian vectors in Corollary 4.7. The  $n$  random vectors may be arbitrarily dependent.

Proposition 7.2 (Maximal inequality for quadratic forms of sub-Gaussian vectors). Let  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A}$  for a  $p\times m$  matrix  $\mathbf{A}$ . Consider a sequence of sub-Gaussian random vectors  $\{\zeta_i\coloneqq (\zeta_{i1},\dots,\zeta_{im})^T\}_{i = 1}^n$  with independent components for  $\pmb{\mu}_i = \mathrm{E}\pmb{\zeta}_i$  and  $\sigma^2$  s.t.

$$
\mathrm {E} e ^ {\boldsymbol {\alpha} ^ {T} \left(\zeta_ {i} - \mu_ {i}\right)} \leq e ^ {\| \boldsymbol {\alpha} \| ^ {2} \sigma^ {2} / 2}, \forall \boldsymbol {\alpha} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m} f o r a l l i ^ {\prime} s.
$$

where  $\{\zeta_i\}_{i = 1}^n$  may be arbitrary dependence. Then,

$$
P \left\{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| \zeta_ {i} \| ^ {2} \leq \sigma^ {2} \left[ m + 2 \sqrt {2 m \log n} + 4 \log n \right] \right\} \geq 1 - \frac {1}{n}.
$$

Moreover, we have  $\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\| \boldsymbol {\zeta}_i\| ^2 = O_p(m\vee \log n)$

Proof. By Corollary 4.7 with  $\mathbf{A} \coloneqq \mathbf{I}_m$  and  $\boldsymbol{\Sigma} \coloneqq \mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{I}_m$ , we get

$$
\operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = \operatorname {t r} \left(\mathbf {I} _ {m}\right) = m, \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {2}\right) = m, \| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \| = 1.
$$

Therefore, we have  $P\{\| \mathbf{I}_m\boldsymbol {\zeta}_i\|^2 >\sigma^2 (m + 2\sqrt{mt} +2t)\} \leq e^{-t}$  for  $i = 1,2,\dots ,n$  . It gives

$$
P \left\{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| \zeta_ {i} \| ^ {2} > \sigma^ {2} [ m + 2 \sqrt {m t} + 2 t ] \right\} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} P \left\{\| \zeta_ {i} \| ^ {2} > \sigma^ {2} [ m + 2 \sqrt {m t} + 2 t ] \right\} \leq n e ^ {- t}. \tag {7.8}
$$

Choose  $t$  such that  $p_n \coloneqq n e^{-t} \to 0$ . For example, let  $t \coloneqq \log n - \log p_n = 2 \log n$  with  $p_n = \frac{1}{n}$ . Inequality (7.8) shows

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left\{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| \zeta_ {i} \| ^ {2} \leq \sigma^ {2} [ m + 2 \sqrt {2 m \log n} + 4 \log n ] \right\} = P \left\{\max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| \zeta_ {i} \| ^ {2} \leq \sigma^ {2} [ m + 2 \sqrt {m t} + 2 t ] \right\} \\ \geq 1 - \frac {1}{n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that  $m + 2\sqrt{2m\log n} + 4\log n \lesssim m \vee \log n$ . Let  $p_n = \frac{1}{rn}$  for  $r > 0$ , and  $t := \log n - \log p_n = 2\log n + \log r$ . Check the definition of the stochastic order,

$$
P \{\max _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \| \boldsymbol {\zeta} _ {i} \| ^ {2} \geq \sigma^ {2} [ m + 2 \sqrt {m (2 \log n + \log r)} + 2 (2 \log n + \log r) ] \} \leq \frac {1}{r n}, \forall r > 0.
$$

Then we get  $\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\| \zeta_i\| ^2 = O_p(m\vee \log n)$

□

# 7.2 Concentration for suprema of empirical processes

Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be a random sample from a measure  $P$  on a measurable space  $(\mathcal{X}, \mathcal{A})$ . The empirical distribution  $\mathbb{P}_n := n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^n \delta_{X_i}$ , where  $\delta_x$  is the probability mass of 1 at  $x$ . Given a measurable function  $f: \mathcal{X} \mapsto \mathbb{R}$ , let  $\mathbb{P}_n f := \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n f(X_i)$  be the expectation of  $f$  under the empirical measure  $\mathbb{P}_n$ , and  $Pf := \int f dP$  be the expectation under  $P$ . The  $\mathbb{P}_n f$  is called the empirical process index by  $n$ .

The study of the empirical processes begins with the uniform limit law of EDF in Example 2.1. The Glivenko-Cantelli theorem extends the LLN for EDF and gives uniform convergence:  $\| \mathbb{F}_n - F\|_{\infty} = \sup_{t\in \mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{F}_n(t) - F(t)|\stackrel {\mathrm{as}}{\to}0$ . Moreover, a stronger result than Example 2.1 is the Dvoretzky-Kiefer-Wolffowitz (DKW) inequality (Dvoretzky et al., 1956)

$$
P \left(\sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {R}} \left| \mathrm {F} _ {n} (x) - F (x) \right| > \varepsilon\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- 2 n \varepsilon^ {2}} \quad \forall \varepsilon > 0. \tag {7.9}
$$

The DKW inequality is a uniform version of Hoeffding's inequality, which also strengthens the Glivenko-Cantelli theorem since (7.9) implies Glivenko-Cantelli:  $\| \mathbb{F}_n - F\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{a.s.}0$  by the first Borel-Cantelli lemma:  $\sum_{n = 1}^{\infty}P(|Y_n|\geq \epsilon) < \infty$  for any  $\varepsilon >0\Rightarrow Y_n\xrightarrow{a.s.}0$  for any sequence of r.v.s  $\{X_i\}_{i = 1}^n$ . Dvoretzky et al. (1956) proves  $P(\sup_{x\in \mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{F}_n(x) - F(x)| > \varepsilon)\le Ce^{-2n\varepsilon^2}$  with an unspecified constant  $C$ . Massart (1996) attains the sharper constant  $C = 2$ .

In some statistical applications, given an estimator  $\hat{\theta}$ , and  $f_{\hat{\theta}}(X_i)$  is a function of  $X_i$  and  $\hat{\theta}$ . We want to study its asymptotic properties for sums of  $f_{\hat{\theta}}(X_i)$  that changes with both  $n$  and  $\hat{\theta}$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ f _ {\hat {\theta}} (X _ {i}) - \operatorname {E} f _ {\hat {\theta}} (X _ {i}) \right], (\mathrm {a d e p e n d e n t s u m}).
$$

A possible route to attain results is via the suprema of the empirical process for all possible the "true" parameter  $\theta_0$  on a set  $K$ :

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ f _ {\hat {\theta}} (X _ {i}) - \operatorname {E} f _ {\hat {\theta}} (X _ {i}) \right] \leq \sup _ {\theta_ {0} \in K} \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ f _ {\theta_ {0}} (X _ {i}) - \operatorname {E} f _ {\theta_ {0}} (X _ {i}) \right] \right| =: \sup _ {\theta_ {0} \in K} \left| (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P) f _ {\theta_ {0}} \right|.
$$

Fortunately, the summation in the sup enjoys independence. So, the study of convergence rate suprema of empirical processes is important if we consider a functional class  $\mathcal{F}$  instead of the set  $K$  such that:  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}|(\mathbb{P}_n - P)f| = \sup_{\theta_0\in K}|\left(\mathbb{P}_n - P\right)f_{\theta_0}|$ .

Let  $(\mathcal{F},\| \cdot \|)$  be a normed space of real functions  $f\colon \mathcal{X}\to \mathbb{R}$ . For a probability measure  $Q$ , define the  $L_{r}(Q)$ -space with  $L_{r}(Q)$ -norm by  $\| f\|_{L_r(Q)} = \left(\int |f|^r dQ\right)^{1 / r}$ . Given two functions  $l(\cdot)$  and  $u(\cdot)$ , the bracket  $[l,u]$  is the set of all functions  $f\in \mathcal{F}$  with  $l(x)\leq f(x)\leq u(x)$  for all  $x\in \mathcal{X}$ . An  $\varepsilon$ -bracket is a bracket  $[l,u]$  with  $\| l - u\|_{L_r(Q)} < \varepsilon$ . The bracketing number  $N_{[]}(\varepsilon ,\mathcal{F},L_r(Q))$  is minimum number of  $\varepsilon$ -brackets needed to cover  $\mathcal{F}$ , i.e.

$$
N _ {[ ]} (\varepsilon , \mathcal {F}, L _ {r} (Q)) = \inf \{n: \exists l _ {1}, u _ {1}, \ldots , l _ {n}, u _ {n} \mathrm {s . t .} \cup_ {i = 1} ^ {n} [ l _ {i}, u _ {i} ] = \mathcal {F} \mathrm {a n d} \| l _ {n} - u _ {n} \| _ {L _ {r} (Q)} <   \varepsilon \}.
$$

The covering number  $N(\varepsilon, \mathcal{F}, L_r(Q))$  is the minimal number of  $L_r(Q)$ -balls of radius  $\varepsilon$  needed to cover the set  $\mathcal{F}$ . The uniform covering numbers is  $\sup_Q N(\varepsilon \| F \|_{L_r(Q)}, \mathcal{F}, L_r(Q))$ , where the supremum is taken over all probability measures  $Q$  for which  $\| F \|_{L_r(Q)} > 0$ . Two

conditions to get the convergence of  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}|\bigl (\mathbb{P}_n - P\bigr)f_\theta |$  are the finite bracketing number condition with  $L_{1}(P)$  -norm in Theorem 19.4 of van der Vaart (1998) (or finite uniform covering numbers in Theorem 19.13 of van der Vaart (1998)).

Lemma 7.1 (Glivenko-Cantell class). For every class  $\mathcal{F}$  of measurable functions, if

$$
N _ {[ ]} (\varepsilon , \mathcal {F}, L _ {1} (P)) <   \infty \left(\text {o r} \sup  _ {Q} N (\varepsilon \| F \| _ {L _ {1} (Q)}, \mathcal {F}, L _ {1} (Q)) <   \infty \text {w i t h} P ^ {*} F <   \infty\right) \text {f o r e v e r y} \varepsilon > 0.
$$

Then,  $\mathcal{F}$  is  $P$ -Glivenko-Cantelli, i.e.  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}|\left(\mathbb{P}_n - P\right)f|\stackrel {\mathrm{as}}{\to}0$

Proof. Choose the  $\varepsilon$ -brackets  $\{[l_i, u_i]\}$  containing the minimal number  $N := N_{[]}(\varepsilon, \mathcal{F}, L_1(P)) < \infty$  of the intervals, in which the items have  $P|u_i - l_i| = \| u_i - l_i\|_{L_1(P)} < \varepsilon$ , then for every  $f \in \mathcal{F}$ , there are some  $i$  rendering the following inequalities held:

$$
\mathbb {P} _ {n} f - P f \leq \left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P\right) u _ {i} + P \left(u _ {i} - f\right) <   \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq N} \left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P\right) u _ {i} + \varepsilon
$$

$$
P f - \mathbb {P} _ {n} f \leq P (f - l _ {i}) - (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P) l _ {i} <   \varepsilon - \min  _ {1 \leq i \leq N} (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P) l _ {i}
$$

By SLLN,  $\max_{1\leq i\leq N}(\mathbb{P}_n - P)u_i,\min_{1\leq i\leq N}(\mathbb{P}_n - P)l_i\xrightarrow{a.s.}\geq 0$  , that complementing with any  $\varepsilon >0$  leads the ultimate result  $\| \mathbb{P}_n f - P f\|_{\mathcal{F}} = \sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}|\mathbb{P}_n f - P f|\xrightarrow{a.s.}\varepsilon ,\quad \varepsilon \downarrow 0.$  So the  $\mathcal{F}$  is P-Glivenko-Cantelli.

Example 7.2 (Empirical process with indicator functions, Example 19.4 of van der Vaart (1998)). Let  $\mathcal{F}$  be the collection of all indicator functions of the form  $f_{t}(x) = 1_{(-\infty ,t]}(x)$ , with  $t$  ranging over  $\mathbb{R}$ . Then,  $\mathcal{F}$  is P-Glivenko-Cantelli. Indeed, Consider brackets of the form  $[1_{(-\infty ,t_{i - 1}]},1_{(-\infty ,t_i)}]$  for a grid of points  $-\infty = t_0 < t_1 < \dots < t_k = \infty$  with the property  $F(t_{i} - ) - F(t_{i - 1}) < \varepsilon$  for each  $i$ . These brackets have  $L_{1}(P)$ -size  $\varepsilon$ , namely

$$
P \left| 1 _ {(- \infty , t _ {i})} - 1 _ {(- \infty , t _ {i - 1} ]} \right| \leq \operatorname {E} \left[ 1 _ {(- \infty , t _ {i})} (X) - 1 _ {(- \infty , t _ {i - 1} ]} (X) \right] <   \varepsilon .
$$

Their total number  $k$  can be chosen smaller than  $\lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil \leq 2 / \varepsilon$ , where  $\lceil r \rceil = \min \{m \in \mathbb{Z}; m \leq r + 1\}$  is the upper integer part of  $r \in \mathbb{R}$  (i.e. is the smallest integer that is greater than or equal to  $x$ ). So

$$
N _ {[ ]} (\varepsilon , \mathcal {F}, L _ {1} (P)) \leq k <   2 / \varepsilon <   \infty \text {f o r e v e r y} \varepsilon > 0.
$$

We conclude that the class  $\mathcal{F}$  is P-Glivenko-Cantelli, and it shows the Glivenko-Cantelli theorem.

Example 7.3 (Weighted empirical process with dependent weights). Suppose we observe a sequence of IID observations  $\{(X_i,Y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  drawn from a random pair  $(X,Y)$ . Given some weighted functions  $W(\cdot)$  and a bounded estimator  $\hat{t} \in (0,\tau]$ , we want to study the stochastic convergence of dependent weighted empirical processes

$$
\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} 1 \left(Y _ {i} \geq \hat {t}\right) W (X _ {i}) - \mu (\hat {t}; W) \right| \left(\leq \sup  _ {0 \leq t \leq \tau} \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} 1 \left(Y _ {i} \geq t\right) W (X _ {i}) - \mu (t; W) \right|\right)
$$

where  $\mu (t;W) = \mathrm{E}_{X,Y}\{1(Y\geq t)W(X_i)\} <  \infty$  and  $W(X_{i})\leq U_{f} <   \infty$  and  $\tau <  \infty$

Consider the class of functions indexed by  $t$ ,

$$
\mathcal {F} = \left\{1 (y \geq t) W (x) / U _ {f} \colon t \in [ 0, \tau ], y \in \mathbb {R}, W (x) \leq U _ {f} \right\}.
$$

It is crucial to evaluate  $N_{[]}(\varepsilon, \mathcal{F}, L_1(Q))$ . Given  $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ , let  $t_i$  be the  $i$ -th  $\lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil$  quantile of  $Y$ , thus  $P(Y \leq t_i) = i\varepsilon$ ,  $i = 1, \ldots, \left\lceil \frac{1}{\varepsilon} \right\rceil - 1$ . Furthermore, take  $t_0 = 0$  and  $t_{\lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil} = +\infty$ . For  $i = 1, 2, \dots, \lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil$ , define brackets  $[L_i, U_i]$  with

$$
L _ {i} (x, y) = 1 (y \geq t _ {i}) \frac {W (x)}{U _ {f}}, U _ {i} (x, y) = 1 (y > t _ {i - 1}) \frac {W (x)}{U _ {f}}
$$

such that  $L_{i}(x,y)\leq 1(y\geq t)e^{f_{\theta}(x)} / U_{f}\leq U_{i}(x,y)$  as  $t_{i - 1} < t\leq t_i$  . The Jensen's inequality gives  $\operatorname {E}|U_i - L_i|\leq |E[\frac{W(X_i)}{U_f}\{1(Y\geq t_i) - 1(Y > t_{i - 1})\} ]|\leq |P(t_{i - 1} < Y\leq t_i)| = \varepsilon$  . Therefore,  $N_{[]}(\varepsilon ,\mathcal{F},L_1(P))\leq$ $\lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil <  \infty$  for every  $\varepsilon >0$  . So the class  $\mathcal{F}$  is P-Glivenko-Cantelli.

If the upper bounds of  $N_{[]}(\varepsilon, \mathcal{F}, L_2(P))$  and  $\sup_Q N(\varepsilon, \mathcal{F}, L_2(Q))$  have polynomial rates w.r.t.  $O(1 / \varepsilon)$ , the following tail bound estimate gives the convergence rate of suprema of empirical processes in Lemma 7.1 obtained by Talagrand (1994). It extends DKW inequality to general empirical processes with the bounded function classes.

Lemma 7.2 (Sharper bounds for suprema of empirical processes). Consider a probability space  $(\Omega, \Sigma, P)$ , and consider  $n$  IID r.vs  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  valued in  $\Omega$ , of law  $P$ . Let  $\mathcal{F}$  be a class of measurable functions  $f: \mathcal{X} \mapsto [0,1]$  that satisfies bracketing number conditions:

$$
N _ {[ ]} (\varepsilon , \mathcal {F}, L _ {2} (P)) \leq (K / \varepsilon) ^ {V} \left(\text {o r} \sup  _ {Q} N (\varepsilon , \mathcal {F}, L _ {2} (Q)) \leq (K / \varepsilon) ^ {V}\right), \text {f o r e v e r y} 0 <   \varepsilon <   K.
$$

Then, for every  $t > 0$

$$
P(\sqrt{n}\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}|(\mathbb{P}_{n} - \mathbb{P})f|\geq t)\leq \left(\frac{D(K)t}{\sqrt{V}}\right)^{V}e^{-2t^{2}}\text{with a constant} D(K)  \text{depending on} K\text{only}.
$$

The explicit constant  $D(K)$  can be found in Zhang (2006), who studied the tail bounds for the suprema of the unbounded and non-IID empirical process. Kong and Nan (2014) derived the rate of convergence for the Lasso regularized Cox models by using sharper concentration inequality for the suprema of empirical processes in Example 7.2 related to the negative log-partial likelihood function. In Example 7.3, we have

$$
\left\{\operatorname {E} | U _ {i} - L _ {i} | ^ {2} \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \leq \left\{E \left[ \frac {W (X _ {i})}{U _ {m}} \left\{1 (Y \geq t _ {i}) - 1 (Y > t _ {i - 1}) \right\} \right] ^ {2} \right\} ^ {1 / 2} \leq \left| P (t _ {i - 1} <   Y \leq t _ {i}) \right| ^ {1 / 2} = \sqrt {\varepsilon},
$$

which implies  $N_{[]}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},\mathcal{F},L_2(P))\leq \lceil 1 / \varepsilon \rceil \leq 2 / \varepsilon$  for every  $\varepsilon >0$ . Hence,  $N_{[]}(\varepsilon ,\mathcal{F},L_2(P))\leq 2 / \varepsilon^2$ . By applying Lemma 7.2 with  $V = 2$ ,  $K = \sqrt{2}$ , we have

$$
P (\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq \tau} | \frac {1}{U _ {f} \sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} [ 1 (Y _ {i} \geq t) W (X _ {i}) - \mu (t; W) ] | \geq t) \leq \frac {D ^ {2} (\sqrt {2})}{2} t ^ {2} e ^ {- 2 t ^ {2}}.
$$

In the following, we present an alternative method to derive constant-specified concentration inequalities for suprema of the empirical process of independent samples over an unbounded class of functions. If the high-probability upper bound of

$$
\left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \sqrt {n} | (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}) f | \right\} - \mathrm {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \sqrt {n} | (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}) f | \right\} a v a i l a b l e.
$$

Then, next two results are the symmetrization theorem and the contraction theorem, which are fundamental tools to get sharper bounds for suprema of empirical processes.

Lemma 7.3 (Symmetrization theorem, Lemma 2.3.1 in van der Vaart (1998)). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent r.vs with values in some space  $\mathcal{X}$  and  $\mathcal{F}$  be a class of measurable real-valued functions on  $\mathcal{X}$ . Let  $\{\epsilon_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be a Rademacher sequence with uniform distribution on  $\{-1,1\}$ , independent of  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  and  $f \in \mathcal{F}$ . If  $\operatorname{E}|f(X_i)| < \infty$  for all  $i$ , then

$$
\operatorname {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \Phi \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left[ f \left(\mathbf {X} _ {i}\right) - \operatorname {E} f \left(\mathbf {X} _ {i}\right) \right]\right) \right\} \leq \operatorname {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \Phi \left[ 2 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} f \left(\mathbf {X} _ {i}\right) \right] \right\}
$$

for every nondecreasing, convex  $\Phi (\cdot):\mathbb{R}\mapsto \mathbb{R}$  and class of measurable functions  $\mathcal{F}$ .

Lemma 7.4 (Contraction theorem). Let  $x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}$  be the non-random elements of  $\mathcal{X}$  and  $\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}$  be Rademacher sequence. Consider  $c$ -Lipschitz functions  $g_{i}$ , i.e.  $|g_{i}(s) - g_{i}(t)| \leq c|s - t|, \forall s,t \in \mathbb{R}$ . Then for any function  $f$  and  $h$  in  $\mathcal{F}$ , we have

$$
\mathrm {E} _ {\epsilon} [ \sup _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \varepsilon_ {i} [ g _ {i} \{f (x _ {i}) \} - g _ {i} \{h (x _ {i}) \} ] | ] \leq 2 c \mathrm {E} _ {\epsilon} [ \sup _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \varepsilon_ {i} \{f (x _ {i}) - h (x _ {i}) \} | ].
$$

A improved symmetrization theorem is studied in Theorem 3.4 of Kashlak (2018), which replaces the constant 2 to a tighter constant  $1 + O(1 / \sqrt{n})$ . By refine the constant  $2c$  to  $c$ , a sharper contraction theorem is given in Proposition 4.3 and (4.9) in Bach (2024). A gentle introduction to suprema of empirical processes and its statistical applications are nicely presented in Sen (2018).

Example 7.4 (Expected bound for supremum of bounded EP).  $\operatorname{E}[\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}f(X_i)]$  can easily be evaluated when  $\mathcal{F}$  is a set linear functionals. Let  $r > 0$  and  $\| \cdot \|$  denote the Euclidean norm on  $\mathbb{R}^p$ . Put

$$
r \mathbf {B} = \left\{\mathbf {a} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}: \| \mathbf {a} \| \leq r \right\}, \mathcal {F} = \left\{f: \mathbb {R} ^ {p} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}: \exists \mathbf {a} \in r \mathbf {B}, f (\mathbf {x}) = \mathbf {a} ^ {T} \mathbf {x} \right\}.
$$

Using Symmetrization Theorem,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E} \left[ \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (X _ {i}) \right] \leq \operatorname {E} \left[ \sup  _ {\mathbf {a} \in r \mathbf {B}} \frac {2}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} \mathbf {a} ^ {T} X _ {i} \right] = \operatorname {E} \left[ \sup  _ {\mathbf {a} \in r \mathbf {B}} \mathbf {a} ^ {T} \left(\frac {2}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i}\right) \right] \\ \mathrm {(C a u c h y ^ {\prime} s i n e q u a l i t y)} \leq r \mathrm {E} \| \frac {2}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i} \| \leq r \left(\mathrm {E} \| \frac {2}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i} \| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \\ = \frac {2 r}{\sqrt {n}} (\sum_ {1 \leqslant i, j \leqslant n} \operatorname {E} \left\{\operatorname {E} \left[ \epsilon_ {i} \epsilon_ {j} X _ {i} ^ {T} X _ {j} \right] | \mathbf {X} \right\}) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\big (\{\varepsilon_ {i} \} _ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {a r e c o n d i t i o n a l l y I I D} \big) = \frac {2 r}{\sqrt {n}} \big (\sum_ {1 \leqslant i = j \leqslant n} \mathrm {E} \{\epsilon_ {i} \epsilon_ {j} \mathrm {E} [ X _ {i} ^ {T} X _ {j} ] | X \} \big) ^ {1 / 2} = 2 r \big (\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} [ X _ {i} ^ {T} X _ {i} ] \big) ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

Finally, McDiarmid's inequality shows with prob. at least  $1 - e^{-2M^2 / \left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_i - a_i)^2\right]}$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {f \in F} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (X _ {i}) \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \mathrm {E} [ \sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (X _ {i}) ] + \frac {M}{\sqrt {n}} \leq \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} [ 2 r (\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \mathrm {E} [ X _ {i} ^ {T} X _ {i} ]) ^ {1 / 2} + M ].
$$

Remark: In general,  $\operatorname{E}[\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}f(X_i)]$  is bounded by the uniform entropy integral evaluated by VC dimension of the general  $\mathcal{F}$ , see Theorem 3.5.4 in Giné and Nickl(2015).

To further bound  $\operatorname{E}\{\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\sqrt{n} |(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})f|\}$  in Lemma 7.3 with  $\Phi (t) = |t|$ , Theorem 3.5.4 in Gine and Nickl (2016) gave a constants-specified upper bound for the expectation of suprema of unbounded empirical processes.

Lemma 7.5 (Moment bound for suprema of unbounded empirical processes). Let  $\mathcal{F}$  be a countable class of measurable functions with  $0\in \mathcal{F}$ , and let  $F$  be a strictly positive envelope for  $\mathcal{F}$ . Assume that  $J(\mathcal{F},F,t)\coloneqq \int_0^t\sup_Q\sqrt{\log[2N(\mathcal{F},L_2(Q),\tau\|F\|_{L_2(Q)})]} d\tau < \infty$  for some (for all)  $t > 0$ . Given  $\mathcal{X}$ -valued IID r.vs  $\{X_i\}_{i = 1}^n$  with law  $P$  s.t.  $PF^2 < \infty$ . Set  $U = \max_{1\leq i\leq n}F(X_i)$  and  $\delta = \sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\sqrt{Pf^2} /\| F\|_{L^2 (P)}$ . Then, for  $A_{1} = 8\sqrt{6}$  and  $A_{2} = 2^{15}3^{5 / 2}$ ,

$$
\operatorname {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \sqrt {n} | (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P) f | \right\} \leq A _ {1} \| F \| _ {L ^ {2} (P)} J (\mathcal {F}, F, \delta) \vee \left[ A _ {2} \| U \| _ {L ^ {2} (P)} J ^ {2} (\mathcal {F}, F, \delta) / (\sqrt {n} \delta^ {2}) \right]. \tag {7.10}
$$

The bound in (7.10) matches the non-asymptotically sub-exponential CLT in (4.7), and it reveals that  $\sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}\sqrt{n} |(\mathbb{P}_n - P)f|$  has the sub-exponential behaviour, although with a huge parameter (the constant  $A_{2} = 2^{15}3^{5 / 2}$  is so large). Recently, Theorem 2 in Baraud and Chen (2024) sharpened bound (7.10) when  $\mathcal{F}$  takes values in  $[-1,1]$ . The uniform entropy integral  $J(\mathcal{F},F,t)$  can be evaluated by VC dimension of  $\mathcal{F}$ , see Theorem 7.11 in Sen (2018). Applying Adamczak (2008)'s tail inequalities for the summation  $Z_{n}\coloneqq \sup_{f\in \mathcal{F}}n|(\mathbb{P}_n - P)f|$  with unbounded  $\mathcal{F}$ , they obtained following result.

Lemma 7.6 (Tail estimates for suprema of empirical processes under sub-Weibull norms). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent  $\mathcal{X}$ -valued r.vs and let  $\mathcal{F}$  be a countable class of measurable functions  $f: \mathcal{X} \to \mathbb{R}$ . For some  $\alpha \in (0,1]$ , assume  $\| \sup_{f \in \mathcal{F}} |f(X_i) - \mathrm{E}f(X_i)| \|_{\psi_\alpha} < \infty$  for every  $i$ . Define  $\sigma_n^2 = \sup_{f \in \mathcal{F}} \sum_{i=1}^n \operatorname{Var} f(X_i)$ . For all  $\eta \in (0,1)$  and  $\delta > 0$ , then there exists a constant  $C_{\alpha, \eta, \delta} > 0$  s.t. both  $P(Z_n \geq (1 + \eta) \mathrm{E}Z_n + t)$  and  $P(Z_n \leq (1 - \eta) \mathrm{E}Z_n - t)$  are bounded by

$$
