\delta_ {n, t, \eta , \delta} (\sigma_ {n} ^ {2}, \alpha) := \exp \left(- \frac {t ^ {2}}{2 (1 + \delta) \sigma_ {n} ^ {2}}\right) + 3 \exp \left(- \left(\frac {t}{C _ {\alpha , \eta , \delta} \| \operatorname* {m a x s u p} _ {i} | f (X _ {i}) - \operatorname* {E} f (X _ {i}) | \| _ {\psi_ {\alpha}}}\right) ^ {\alpha}\right) \text {f o r a l l} t \geq 0.
$$

So, Lemmas 7.5 and 7.6 give  $P(n^{-1}Z_{n} \geq (1 + \eta)n^{-1/2}[\text{Right hand side of (7.10)}] + t) \leq P(Z_{n} \geq (1 + \eta)n^{1/2}\mathrm{E}[n^{-1/2}Z_{n}] + nt) \leq \delta_{n,nt,\eta,\delta}(\sigma_{n}^{2},\alpha)$ . We have

$$
P (\sup _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} | (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - P) f | \leq (1 + \eta) n ^ {- 1 / 2} [ \mathrm {R i g h t h a n d s i d e o f (7 . 1 0)} ] + t) \geq 1 - \delta_ {n, n t, \eta , \delta} (\sigma_ {n} ^ {2}, \alpha),
$$

which extends Example 2.4 from bound to unbound empirical processes.

The constant-unspecific version of Lemma 7.5 (Lemma 19.36-19.38 in van der Vaart (1998) or other versions) has wide applications in deriving the rate of convergence for kernel density estimations, M-estimators in high-dimensional and increasingly-dimensional regressions; see Giné and Nickl (2016), Buhlmann and van de Geer (2011), Pan and Zhou (2020) and references therein.

We illustrate a proof for a weak version of DKW inequality based on the Lévy inequality and symmetrization theorem.

Example 7.5 (A proof of DKW inequality, Oliveira (2016)). A weak version of DKW inequality is

$$
P \left(\sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {R}} \left| \mathbb {F} _ {n} (x) - F (x) \right| > \varepsilon\right) \leq 4 e ^ {- n \varepsilon^ {2} / 8} \quad \forall \varepsilon > 0.
$$

The proof is divided into 3 steps.

Lemma 7.7 (Lévy inequality, p72 in Chow and Teicher (2003)). Let  $\{X_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be independent r.vs, let  $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k X_i$  and be  $\operatorname{Med}(X)$  as the median of  $X$ , then  $P\{\max_{1 \leq k \leq n}[S_k - \operatorname{Med}(S_k - S_n)] \geq x\} \leq 2P\{S_n \geq x\}$ .

Remark 7.1. It is worth noting that the inequalities of Hoeffding, sub-exponential, Bernstein and Sub-weibull also hold for the maximum of the partial-sums  $\max_{1\leq k\leq n}S_k$  by virtue of Doob's sub-martingale inequality (Section 7.2.c, 7.3, 7.5 in Lin and Bai (2011)).

Step1. We denote functional class  $\mathcal{F} = \{\frac{1}{n} 1_{(-\infty, x]}(t) : x \in \mathbb{R}\} \cup \{\frac{-1}{n} 1_{(-\infty, x]}(t) : x \in \mathbb{R}\}$ . By applying symmetrization theorem for the increasing convex function  $\Psi(x) = e^{\theta x}$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E} \left[ e ^ {\theta \sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {R}} | \mathbb {F} _ {n} (x) - F (x) |} \right] = \operatorname {E} \left\{\Psi \left(\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} [ f (X _ {i}) - \operatorname {E} f (X _ {i}) ]\right) \right\} = \operatorname {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \Psi \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} [ f (X _ {i}) - \operatorname {E} f (X _ {i}) ]\right) \right\} \\ \leq \operatorname {E} \left\{\sup  _ {f \in \mathcal {F}} \Psi \left(2 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} f (X _ {i})\right) \right\} = \operatorname {E} \left[ \sup  _ {b \in \{\pm 1 \}, x \in \mathbb {R}} e ^ {\frac {2 \theta b}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} 1 _ {(- \infty , x ]} (X _ {i})} \right] = \operatorname {E} \left[ \operatorname {E} \left(\sup  _ {b \in \{\pm 1 \}, x \in \mathbb {R}} e ^ {\frac {2 \theta b}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} 1 _ {(- \infty , x ]} (X _ {i})} | X\right) \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathbf{X} \coloneqq (X_1, \dots, X_n)^T$  and  $b$  is a binary r.v. with value  $\pm 1$ .

Step2. Next, conditioning on  $X \coloneqq (X_1, \dots, X_n)^T = (x_1, \dots, x_n)^T \eqqcolon x$ , we analyze the last term in Step1. We permute the indices  $1, 2, \dots, n$ , so that  $x_1 \leq x_2 \leq \dots \leq x_n$ . No matter how to permute, the permutation doesn't change the law of  $\sum_{j=1}^{n} \epsilon_j 1_{(-\infty, x]}(x_j)$ . Now,

$$
\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {j} 1 _ {(- \infty , x ]} \left(x _ {j}\right) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 0, & \text {i f} x <   x _ {1}, (i = 0) \\ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \epsilon_ {j} & \text {i f} x _ {i} \leq x <   x _ {i + 1}, (1 \leq i <   n) \\ \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {j} & \text {i f} x \geq x _ {n}, (i = n) \end{array} \right.
$$

and thus we have  $\sup_{b\in \{\pm 1\} ,x\in \mathbb{R}}e^{\frac{2\theta b}{n}\sum_{j = 1}^{n}\epsilon_{j}1_{(-\infty ,x]}\left(x_{j}\right)} = \sup_{b\in \{\pm 1\} ,0\leq i\leq n}e^{\frac{2\theta b}{n}\sum_{j = 1}^{i}\epsilon_{j}}\leq \max_{0\leq i\leq n}\left\{e^{\frac{2\theta}{n}\sum_{j = 1}^{i}\epsilon_{j}} + e^{\frac{-2\theta}{n}\sum_{j = 1}^{i}\epsilon_{j}}\right\} .$  Note that  $\{\epsilon_i\}_{i = 1}^n$  are symmetric about O, we get:

$$
\operatorname {E} \left[ \sup  _ {b \in \{\pm 1 \}, x \in \mathbb {R}} e ^ {\frac {2 \theta b}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} 1 _ {(- \infty , x ]} (X _ {i})} | X = x \right] \leq 2 \operatorname {E} \left[ \max  _ {0 \leq i \leq n} e ^ {\frac {2 \theta}{n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \epsilon_ {i}} \right] = 2 \operatorname {E} \left[ e ^ {\frac {2 \theta}{n} \max  _ {0 \leq i \leq n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \epsilon_ {i}} \right] \leq 4 e ^ {\frac {2 \theta^ {2}}{n}} \tag {7.11}
$$

where the last inequality will be proved in Step3.

Step3. Let  $\{\varepsilon_i\}_{i=1}^n$  be i.i.d sequence of Rademacher rvs taking values on  $\{1,-1\}$ , its MGF is  $Ee^{\theta \epsilon_j} \leq \exp\left(\frac{\theta^2}{2}\right)$  by Hoeffding's lemma. Setting  $S_k = \sum_{j=1}^k \epsilon_j$ , we note that the distribution of  $\{\varepsilon_i\}_{i=1}^n$  is symmetric and so is  $S_k - S_n$ , which implies that  $\text{Median}(S_k - S_n) = 0$ . By Lévy inequality (Lemma 7.7), we have  $P(\max_{0 \leq k \leq n} S_k \geq t) \leq 2P(S_n \geq t)$ . Then applying identity for differentiable function  $f$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E} [ f (X) 1 _ {\{X \geq 0 \}} ] = \operatorname {E} [ (f (0) + \int_ {0} ^ {X} f ^ {\prime} (t) d t) 1 _ {\{X \geq 0 \}} ] = f (0) \operatorname {E} [ 1 _ {\{X \geq 0 \}} ] + \operatorname {E} [ \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} f ^ {\prime} (t) 1 _ {\{X \geq t \}} d t 1 _ {\{X \geq 0 \}} ] \\ = f (0) \mathrm {E} \left[ 1 _ {\{X \geq 0 \}} \right] + \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} f ^ {\prime} (t) \mathbf {P} [ X \geq t ] d t \tag {7.12} \\ \end{array}
$$

and Lévy inequality, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {E} \left[ \exp \left(\theta \max  _ {0 \leq i \leq n} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \epsilon_ {j}\right) \right] = 1 + \theta \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {\theta t} P \left(\max  _ {0 \leq i \leq n} S _ {i} \geq t\right) d t \leq 1 + 2 \theta \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {\theta t} P \left(S _ {n} \geq t\right) d t \\ = 1 + 2 \mathrm {E} \left[ \left(e ^ {\theta S _ {n}} - 1\right) 1 _ {\{S _ {n} \geq 0 \}} \right] \leq 2 \mathrm {E} e ^ {\theta S _ {n}} \leq 2 e ^ {\frac {\theta^ {2} n}{2}} [ \text {H o e f f d i n g ' s l e m m a} ]. \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last equality is by (7.12):  $\int_0^\infty f'(t)\mathbf{P}[X\geq t]dt = \operatorname {E}[f(X)1_{\{X\geq 0\}}] - f(0)\operatorname {E}[1_{\{X\geq 0\}}]$  and the second last inequality is by Lévy inequality (for  $\max S_i\geq 0^{\dagger}$  and  $S_{n}$ ):  $P(S_{n}\geq 0)\geq 1 / 2$

Therefore by (7.11), we have sub-Gaussian MGF of suprema of empirical processes

$$
\operatorname {E} \left[ e ^ {\theta \sup  _ {x \in \mathbb {R}} \left| \mathbb {F} _ {n} (x) - F (x) \right|} \right] \leq \operatorname {E} \left[ \operatorname {E} \left(\sup  _ {b \in \{\pm 1 \}, x \in \mathbb {R}} e ^ {\frac {2 \theta b}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} 1 _ {(- \infty , x ]} (X _ {i})} | X\right) \right] \leq 4 e ^ {\frac {2 \theta^ {2}}{n}}.
$$

Finally, by Chernoff's inequality, we obtain the DKW inequality (7.5):  $P(\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}} |\mathbb{F}_n(x) - F(x)| > \varepsilon) \leq \inf_{\theta > 0} 4e^{\frac{2\theta^2}{n} - \theta \epsilon} = 4e^{-n\varepsilon^2 / 8}$  for every  $\varepsilon > 0$ .

# 8 Concentration for High-dimensional Statistics

With the emergence of high-dimensional (HD) data such as the gene expression data, there are renewed interests on the CIs. One aspect of the HD data is such that the number of variables  $p$  can be comparable to or even greater than the sample size  $n$ . This section provides results in three commonly encountered settings: increasing-dimensional ( $p_n = o(n) < n$ ), large-dimensional ( $p_n = O(n)$ ) and high-dimensional setting ( $p_n \gg n$ ,  $p_n = e^{o(n)}$ ).

# 8.1 Linear models with diverging number of covariates

Suppose that we have an  $n$ -dimensional random vector  $\mathbf{Y}$  which contains  $n$  responses  $\{Y_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  to  $p$  covariates  $\mathbf{X}_i = (x_{i1},\dots ,x_{ip})^T$ , respectively. The  $n$  copies of  $\mathbf{X}_i$  as row vectors

make a  $n \times p$  design matrix  $\mathbf{X} = (\mathbf{X}_1^T, \dots, \mathbf{X}_n^T)^T$ . The conditional expectation  $\operatorname{E}[Y_i | \mathbf{X}_i]$  is linearly related to a coefficient vector  $\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*} = (\beta_{1}^{*}, \dots, \beta_{p}^{*})^{T}$  such that

$$
Y = X \beta^ {*} + \varepsilon \tag {8.1}
$$

where  $\{\varepsilon_i\}_{i=1}^n$  in the error vector  $\varepsilon = (\varepsilon_1, \dots, \varepsilon_n)^T$  are IID with zero mean and finite variance  $\sigma^2$ . The  $\beta^*$  needs to be estimated.

This subsection only considers the case that  $p$  is increasing but  $p < n$ . The ordinary least square (OLS) estimator is

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L S} = \operatorname {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \| \boldsymbol {Y} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {8.2}
$$

Assume  $\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{X}) = p$ , which is not hard to meet since  $p < n$ ,  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{LS} = (\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{Y}$  is the unique solution of the (8.2). The following result for the OLS estimator is well known.

Lemma 8.1. Under the assumptions on the linear models and the rank of  $\mathbf{X}$  is  $p$ , then (i) Let  $\mathbf{A}$  be a  $p\times n$  matrix, then  $\mathrm{E}\| \mathbf{A}\varepsilon \|_2^2 = \mathrm{E}(\varepsilon^T\mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A}\varepsilon) = \sigma^2\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A})$ .

(ii)(The curse of dimensionality) The mean square error and the average in-sample  $\ell_2$  risk of the OLS estimator are  $\mathrm{E}\| \hat{\pmb{\beta}}_{LS} - \pmb {\beta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2} = \mathrm{tr}((\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1})\sigma^{2}$  and  $\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{E}\| \mathbf{X}(\hat{\pmb{\beta}}_{LS} - \pmb {\beta}^{*})\| _2^2 = \frac{p\sigma^2}{n}$

Proof. (i) As  $\| \mathbf{A}\pmb {\varepsilon}\| _2 = \pmb{\varepsilon}^T\mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A}\pmb {\varepsilon}$  is scalar, we have  $\pmb{\varepsilon}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\pmb {\varepsilon} = \mathrm{tr}(\pmb{\varepsilon}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\pmb {\varepsilon}) = \mathrm{tr}(\pmb{\varepsilon}\pmb{\varepsilon}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A})$  Therefore, by linearity of the trace operator, we have

$$
\left. \right. \operatorname {E} \| \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \operatorname {E} \left(\boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon}\right) = \operatorname {E} \left[ \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} ^ {T} \mathbf {A}\right)\right] = \operatorname {t r} \left[ \operatorname {E} \left(\boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} ^ {T}\right) \mathbf {A} ^ {T} \mathbf {A} \right] = \sigma^ {2} \operatorname {t r} \left(\mathbf {A} ^ {T} \mathbf {A}\right).
$$

(ii) Substituting  $Y = X\beta^{*} + \varepsilon$  into the formula of  $\hat{\beta}$ , we have

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} = \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {Y} = \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \left(\mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} + \varepsilon\right) = \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} + \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon}. \tag {8.3}
$$

Therefore,  $\hat{\beta} -\pmb{\beta}^{*} = (\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{T}\pmb {\varepsilon},$  which readily derives the first results of (ii) by letting  $\mathbf{A} = (\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T$  in (i). From (i), according to (8.3), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \operatorname {E} \left\| \mathbf {X} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \operatorname {E} \left\| \mathbf {X} \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \operatorname {t r} \left[ \mathbf {X} \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \right] \sigma^ {2} \right. \\ = \operatorname {t r} \left[ \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} \right] \sigma^ {2} = \operatorname {t r} \left(\mathbf {I} _ {p}\right) \sigma^ {2} = p \sigma^ {2}. \tag {8.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

Remark 8.1. As  $p, n \to \infty$  with  $p < n$ , part (ii) implies that the OLS estimator may had poor performance if  $p / n \to c > 0$ . The average in-sample  $\ell_2$ -risk tends to zero if  $p_n = o(n)$ .

Put  $\hat{\pmb{\beta}}\coloneqq \hat{\pmb{\beta}}_{LS}$ . Let  $\{\lambda_i(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})\}_{i = 1}^k$  be the eigenvalue values of  $\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}$ . Markov's inequality and Lemma 8.1 with  $\mathbf{A} = (\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T$  implies

$$
P \{\| \hat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} > t \} \leq \frac {\sigma^ {2} \mathrm {t r} [ (\pmb {X} ^ {T} \pmb {X}) ^ {- 1} ]}{t ^ {2}} = \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{t ^ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p} \frac {1}{\lambda_ {i} (\pmb {X} ^ {T} \pmb {X})} \leq \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{t ^ {2}} \frac {p}{\lambda_ {\min} (\pmb {X} ^ {T} \pmb {X})} =: \delta_ {n},
$$

which implies that, with probability greater than  $1 - \delta_{n}$

$$
\left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {2} \leq \sigma \sqrt {p / n} \cdot \left[ \delta_ {n} \lambda_ {\min } \left(\frac {1}{n} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) \right] ^ {- 1 / 2}. \tag {8.5}
$$

Assume that  $p \coloneqq p_n = o(n^r)$  as  $n \to \infty$ ,  $p_n < n$ . We specific two groups of regularity conditions and the value of  $r$  such that the  $l_2$  consistency  $(\| \hat{\beta} - \beta^* \|_2 \xrightarrow{p} 0)$  is true.

(1) By Lemma 8.1, if  $\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}$  is uniformly positive definite ( $\exists c > 0$  s.t.  $\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X} \succ c\mathbf{I}_p$ ) then

$$
\frac {p \sigma^ {2}}{n} = \frac {1}{n} \mathrm {E} \| \mathbf {X} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \mathrm {E} [ (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ^ {T} \frac {1}{n} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ] \geq c \mathrm {E} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

If  $p = o(n)$ , then  $\mathrm{E} \| \hat{\beta} - \beta^* \|_2^2 = o(1)$  which implies  $\| \hat{\beta} - \beta^* \|_2 = o_p(1)$ .

(2) From (8.5), if  $p = o(\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}))$ , we have  $\| \hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta} \|_2 = o_p(1)$ . In this case, if we consider: "  $\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}$  is positive definite" in (1), it also leads to  $p = o(\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})) = o(n)$ .

In (8.1) with fixed design, suppose that the  $\varepsilon_1,\ldots ,\varepsilon_n$  are sub-Gaussian zero-mean noise for which there exists a  $\sigma >0$  such that  $\mathrm{Ee}^{\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}\leq e^{\sigma^{2}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{2}},\forall \alpha_{1},\ldots ,\alpha_{n}\in \mathbb{R}$ . Suppose that the Gram matrix  $S_{n}\coloneqq \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}$  is invertible. The excess in-sample prediction error  $R(\hat{\pmb{\beta}})$  is the difference between the expected squared error for  $X_{i}^{T}\hat{\pmb{\beta}}$  and for  $X_{i}^{T}\pmb{\beta}^{*}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} R (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) := \frac {1}{n} \left\{\operatorname {E} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} - Y _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right] - \operatorname {E} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - Y _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right] \right\} | _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} \\ = \frac {1}{n} \left\{\operatorname {E} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - Y _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right] \mid_ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} - \operatorname {E} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - Y _ {i}\right) ^ {2} \right] \right\} \\ = \frac {1}{n} \| \mathbf {X} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{n} \mathrm {E} \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \cdot \varepsilon_ {i} \right] | _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} = \frac {1}{n} \| \mathbf {X} \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\varepsilon} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {8.6} \\ \end{array}
$$

which is a quadratic form of sub-Gaussian vector.

By Corollary 4.7 with  $\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T/\sqrt{n}, \boldsymbol{\xi}:=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}, \boldsymbol{\mu}=\mathbf{0}$  and  $\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=\mathbf{A}^T\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T/n$

$$
\operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = \operatorname {t r} \left(\left(\mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X}\right) / n = p / n, \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {2}\right) = p / n ^ {2}, \| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \| _ {2} = 1 / n.
$$

where last identity is due to  $\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^T$  being a projection matrix. Thus  $P[R(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) > \frac{\sigma^2(p + 2\sqrt{pt} + 2t)}{n} ]\leq e^{-t}$ , i.e. with probability  $1 - e^{-t}$ ,

$$
R (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) \leq \frac {\sigma^ {2} (p + 2 \sqrt {p t} + 2 t)}{n}.
$$

For Gaussian noise,  $\mathrm{ER}(\hat{\beta}) = \frac{\sigma^2p}{n}$  in Lemma 8.1, so  $P\{R(\hat{\beta}) - \mathrm{ER}(\hat{\beta}) \leq \frac{\sigma^2(2\sqrt{pt} + 2t)}{n}\} \geq 1 - e^{-t}$ .

# 8.2 Non-asymptotic Bai-Yin theorem for random matrix

Let  $\mathbf{A}$  be a  $p\times p$  Hermitian matrix with real eigenvalues:  $\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}\coloneqq \lambda_1\geq \dots \geq \lambda_p\eqqcolon \lambda_{\mathrm{min}}$ . The empirical spectral distribution (ESD) of  $\mathbf{A}$  is

$$
F _ {\mathbf {A}} (x) = \frac {1}{p} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} 1 (\lambda_ {j} \leq x),
$$

which resembles the EDF of IID samples. Let  $\{\mathbf{A}_n\}_{n\geq 1}$  be a sequence of  $p\times p$  Hermitian random matrices indexed by the sample size  $n$ , and  $F_{\mathbf{A}_n}$  be the ESD of  $\mathbf{A}_n$ .

A major interest in random matrix theory is to investigate the convergence of  $F_{\mathbf{A}_n}$  as a sequence of distributions to a limit  $F$ . Recall the  $F$  is said to be the limit of  $\{F_{\mathbf{A}_n}\}_{n\geq 1}$  if  $\lim_{n\to \infty}F_{\mathbf{A}_n}(x) = F(x)$  for all  $x\in C(F)$ , where  $C(F)$  is the set of continuous point set of  $F(x)$ . In multivariate statistics, it is of interest to study the sample covariance matrix  $\mathbf{S}_n\coloneqq \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^T$  where the double array  $\mathbf{X} = \{X_{ij},i = 1,\dots,p;j = 1,\dots,n\}$  contains zero-mean IID r.vs  $\{X_{ij}\}$  with variance  $\sigma^2$ . Suppose that the dimensions  $n$  and  $p$  grow to infinity while  $p / n$  converges to a constant in [0,1]. Marčenko and Pastur (1967) gives the limit behavior of the ESD of  $\mathbf{S}_n$ . Bai and Yin (1993) obtained a strong version of the Marčenko-Pastur law.

Corollary 8.1 (Bai-Yin theorem). Let  $\mathbf{X}$  be an  $n \times p$  random matrix whose entries are independent copies of a r.v. with zero mean, unit variance, and finite fourth moment  $(\mathrm{E}|X_{11}|^4 < \infty)$ . As  $n \to \infty, p \to \infty, p / n \to y \in (0,1)$ , then

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \lambda_ {\min} (\mathbf {S} _ {n}) = \sigma^ {2} (1 - \sqrt {y}) ^ {2}, \quad \lim _ {n \to \infty} \lambda_ {\max} (\mathbf {S} _ {n}) = \sigma^ {2} (1 + \sqrt {y}) ^ {2} a. s..
$$

Note that  $\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{S}_{n}) = \lambda_{i}(\mathbf{X} / \sqrt{n})$  for all  $i$ , Bai-Yin's law asserts that if  $\sigma^2 = 1$ :  $\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X} / \sqrt{n}) = 1 - \sqrt{p / n} + o(\sqrt{p / n})$ ,  $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{X} / \sqrt{n}) = 1 + \sqrt{p / n} + o(\sqrt{p / n})$  a.s..

Theorem 4.6.1 in Vershynin (2018) studies the non-asymptotic upper and lower bounds of the extreme eigenvalues of  $\mathbf{S}_n$  with independent sub-exponential entries, but the bounds contained un-specific constants. We give a constant-specified version:

Proposition 8.1 (Constants-specified non-asymptotic Bai-Yin theorem). Let  $\mathbf{X}$  be an  $n\times p$  matrix whose rows  $\mathbf{X}_i$  are independent sub-Gaussian random vectors in  $\mathbb{R}^p$  with  $\operatorname {Var}(X_i) = \mathbf{I}_p$ . Define  $Z_{i}\coloneqq |\langle X_{i},x\rangle |,\forall x\in S^{n - 1}$ . Further assume that  $\{Z_i^2 -1\}_{i = 1}^n$  are  $\mathrm{subE}(\theta)$ , then

$$
P \left\{\left\| n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \right\| \leq 2 c \theta \max  \left(\delta , \delta^ {2}\right) \right\} \geq 1 - 2 e ^ {- c t ^ {2}}, t \geq 0 \tag {8.7}
$$

where  $\delta = 2c\left(\sqrt{p / n} + t / \sqrt{n}\right)$  with  $t = c\theta \max \left(\delta, \delta^2\right)$  and  $c \geq 2n\log 9 / p$ . Moreover,

$$
P \left\{1 - t ^ {2} \leq \lambda_ {\min } \left(\mathbf {S} _ {n}\right) \leq \lambda_ {\max } \left(\mathbf {S} _ {n}\right) \leq 1 + t ^ {2} \right\} \geq 1 - 2 e ^ {- c t ^ {2}}. \tag {8.8}
$$

Proposition 8.1 does not require  $p / n \to y \in (0,1)$  as in Corollary 8.1.

Proof. Step1. We introduce a counting measure for measuring the complexity of a set in some space. The covering number  $\mathcal{N}(K,\varepsilon)$  is the smallest number of closed balls centered at  $K$  with radii  $\varepsilon$  whose union covers  $K$ . For some  $\varepsilon \in [0,1)$ , a subset  $\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon} \subset \mathbb{R}$  is an  $\varepsilon$ -net for  $S^{n-1}$  if for all  $x \in S^{n-1}$ , there is an  $y \in \mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}$ , such that  $\|x - y\| < \varepsilon$ . We use the following results in Lemma 5.2 and 5.4 of Vershynin (2012).

Lemma 8.2 (Covering numbers of the sphere).  $\mathcal{N}(S^{n - 1},\varepsilon)\leq (1 + \frac{2}{\varepsilon})^n$  for every  $\varepsilon >0$

Lemma 8.3 (Computing the spectral norm on a net). Let  $\mathbf{B}$  be an  $p\times p$  matrix. Then

$$
\left\| \mathbf {B} \right\| := \max  _ {\left\| \mathbf {x} \right\| _ {2} = 1} \left\| \mathbf {B} x \right\| _ {2} = \sup  _ {x \in S ^ {p - 1}} | \langle \mathbf {B} x, x \rangle | \leq (1 - 2 \varepsilon) ^ {- 1} \sup  _ {x \in \mathcal {N} _ {\varepsilon}} | \langle \mathbf {B} x, x \rangle |.
$$

Lemma 8.3 shows that  $\left\| \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X} - \mathbf{I}_p\right\| \leq 2\max_{\boldsymbol {x}\in \mathcal{N}_{1 / 4}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\| \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol {x}\| _2^2 -1\right|$ . Indeed, note that  $\langle \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol {x} -$ $x,x\rangle = \langle \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}x,x\rangle -1 = \frac{1}{n}\| \mathbf{X}x\| _2^2 -1$ . By setting  $\varepsilon = 1 / 4$  in Lemma 8.3, we get

$$
\left\| n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \right\| \leq (1 - 2 \varepsilon) ^ {- 1} \sup  _ {x \in \mathcal {N} _ {\varepsilon}} | \langle n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} x - x, x \rangle | = 2 \sup  _ {x \in \mathcal {N} _ {1 / 4}} | n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} x \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 |. \tag {8.9}
$$

By (8.9), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left\{\left\| n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \right\| \geq 2 t \right\} \leq P \left\{2 \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {x} \in \mathcal {N} _ {1 / 4}} | n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 | \geq 2 t \right\} \leq \sum_ {x \in \mathcal {N} _ {1 / 4}} P \left\{| n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 | \geq t \right\} \\ \leq \mathcal {N} \left(S ^ {n - 1}, 1 / 4\right) P \left\{\left| n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} x \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 \right| \geq t \right\} \leq 9 ^ {n} P \left\{\left| n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} x \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 \right| \geq t \right\}, \forall x \in \mathcal {N} _ {1 / 4}, \tag {8.10} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality follows Lemma 8.2 with  $\varepsilon = 1 / 4$ .

Step2. It is sufficient to bound  $P\{\left|\frac{1}{n}\right|\| \mathbf{X}x\| _2^2 -1\geq t\}$ . Let  $Z_{i}\coloneqq |\langle X_{i},x\rangle |, \forall x\in S^{n - 1}$ . Observe that  $\| \mathbf{X}x\| _2^2 = \sum_{i = 1}^n |\langle X_i,x\rangle |^2 = : \sum_{i = 1}^n Z_i^2$ . Apply the sub-exponential concentration inequality in Corollary 4.2,  $P(n^{-1}||\mathbf{X}x||_2^2 -1||\geq t) = P(n^{-1}|\sum_{i = 1}^n (Z_i^2 -1)|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-\frac{n}{2} (\frac{t^2}{\theta^2}\wedge \frac{t}{\theta})}$ . Specially, let  $t = c\theta \max \left(\delta ,\delta^2\right) = c\theta \left[\delta \mathrm{I}_{\{\delta \leq 1\}} + \delta^2\mathrm{I}_{\{\delta >1\}}\right]$  with  $\delta \coloneqq 2c(p / n + t / \sqrt{n})$ . From (8.10),

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left\{\left\| n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \right\| \geq 2 t \right\} \leq 9 ^ {n} P \left\{| n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 | \geq c \theta \max  (\delta , \delta^ {2}) \right\} \\ \leq 2 \cdot 9 ^ {n} e ^ {\left[ \right. - \frac {c n}{2} \min  \left\{\delta^ {2} I _ {\left\{\delta \leq 1 \right\}} + \delta^ {4} I _ {\left\{\delta > 1 \right\}}, \delta^ {I} I _ {\left\{\delta \leq 1 \right\}} + \delta^ {2} I _ {\left\{\delta > 1 \right\}} \right\}} = 2 \cdot 9 ^ {n} e ^ {- \frac {c n}{2} \delta^ {2}} = e ^ {- \frac {c}{2} (\sqrt {p} + t) ^ {2}} \leq 2 \cdot 9 ^ {n} e ^ {- c (p + t ^ {2}) / 2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is obtained by using the inequality  $(a + b)^2 \geq a^2 + b^2$  for  $a, b \geq 0$ . For  $c \geq n \log 9 / p$ ,  $2 \cdot 9^n e^{-c(p + t^2)} \leq 2e^{-ct^2}$ , which proves (8.7).

Step3. To show (8.8), the  $\max_{||x||_2 = 1}||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{X}x||_2^2 - 1| = \max_{||x||_2 = 1}||\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X} - \mathbf{I}_p\right)x||_2^2 = \left\| \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X} - \mathbf{I}_p\right\|^2 \leq t^2$  implies that  $1 - t^2 \leq \lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{S}_n) \leq 1 + t^2$ . Similarly, for  $\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{S}_n)$ ,

$$
\min  _ {| | \boldsymbol {x} | | _ {2} = 1} | \| \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 1 | = \min  _ {| | \boldsymbol {x} | | _ {2} = 1} \left\| \left(\frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p}\right) \boldsymbol {x} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \max  _ {| | \boldsymbol {x} | | _ {2} = 1} \left\| \left(\frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p}\right) \boldsymbol {x} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq t ^ {2}.
$$

$$
\operatorname {S o} \lambda_ {\min } (\mathbf {S} _ {n}) \in [ 1 - t ^ {2}, 1 + t ^ {2} ] \text {a n d} \left\{\| \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \| ^ {2} \leq t ^ {2} \right\} \subset \left\{1 - t ^ {2} \leq \lambda_ {\min } (\mathbf {S} _ {n}) \leq \lambda_ {\max } (\mathbf {S} _ {n}) \leq 1 + t ^ {2} \right\}.
$$

$$
\text {T h e n} P \left\{1 - t ^ {2} \leq \lambda_ {\min } \left(\mathbf {S} _ {n}\right) \leq \lambda_ {\max } \left(\mathbf {S} _ {n}\right) \leq 1 + t ^ {2} \right\} \geq P \left\{\left\| \frac {1}{n} \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I} _ {p} \right\| ^ {2} \leq t ^ {2} \right\} \geq 1 - 2 e ^ {- c t ^ {2}}.
$$

# 8.3 Oracle inequalities for penalized linear models

This section introduces the proofs of the error bounds from the perspective of Lasso penalized linear models with the  $\ell_2$ -loss function. When  $p > n$ , the OLS estimator is no longer available as  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_i\mathbf{X}_i^T$  is of invertible. A common way for obtaining a plausible estimator for the true parameter  $\beta^*$  is by adding penalized function to the square loss function. For  $0 < q \leq \infty$ , we write  $\|\beta\|_q := (\sum_{i=1}^{p}|\beta_i|^q)^{1/q}$  as the  $\ell_q$ -norm for  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} \in \mathbb{R}^p$ . If  $q = \infty$ ,  $\|\beta\|_{\infty} := \max_{i=1,\dots,p}|\beta_i|$ ; if  $q = 0$ ,  $\|\beta\|_0 := \sum_{i=1}^{p}1(\beta_i \neq 0)$ . There are two types statistical guarantees of  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}$  as mentioned in Bartlett et al. (2012).

1. Persistence:  $\hat{\beta}$  performs well on a new sample  $X^{*} \stackrel{d}{=} X$  (equal in distribution), i.e.  $\operatorname{E}\{[X^{*}(\hat{\beta} - \beta^{*})]^{2}|X^{*}\} \to 0$ .  
2.  $\ell_q$ -consistency ( $q \geq 1$ ):  $\hat{\beta}$  approximates  $\beta^*$ , i.e. with high probability  $\| \hat{\beta} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^* \|_q \to 0$ .

The persistence and  $\ell_1$ -consistency are respectively obtained by error bounds:

$$
\| \hat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq O _ {p} (s \lambda_ {n}), \quad \mathrm {E} \{[ \pmb {X} ^ {*} (\hat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*}) ] ^ {2} | \pmb {X} ^ {*} \} \leq O _ {p} (s \lambda_ {n} ^ {2}), (\mathrm {s a y s o r a c l e i n e q u a l i t i e s})
$$

where  $\lambda_{n}\to 0$  is a tuning parameter and  $s\coloneqq \| \pmb{\beta}^{*}\|_{0}$ . In the following, we focus on the  $\ell_1$  estimation and prediction consistencies for the penalized linear models. Let  $\lambda >0$  be a tuning parameter, the Lasso estimator (Tibshirani, 1996) for Model (8.1) is

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} = \operatorname {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {Y} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / n + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1} \right\}. \tag {8.11}
$$

By sub-derivative techniques in convex optimizations, the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) condition of Lasso optimization function is

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{c} 2 [ \mathbf {X} ^ {T} (\mathbf {Y} - \mathbf {X} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L}) ] _ {j} / n = - \lambda \mathrm {s i g n} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L j}) \text {i f} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {j} \neq 0, \\ 2 | [ \mathbf {X} ^ {T} (\mathbf {Y} - \mathbf {X} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) _ {L} ] _ {j} | / n \leq \lambda \qquad \text {i f} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L j} = 0. \end{array} \right..
$$

which implies  $\| \frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}^T (\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\|_{\infty}\leq \frac{\lambda}{2}$ . Another approach to get the Lasso-like sparse estimator is attained by Dantzig selector (DS)

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {D S} = \operatorname {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1}: \| \boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} (\boldsymbol {Y} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\beta}) \| _ {\infty} / n \leq \lambda / 2 \right\}. \tag {8.12}
$$

see Candes and Tao (2007). Lasso and DS are capable of producing sparse estimates with only a few (hence sparse) nonzero coefficients among the  $p$  coefficients of the covariates. The idea of Lasso and DS was presented in a geophysics literature (Levy and Fullagar, 1981). By (8.12), we get  $\| \hat{\beta}_{DS} \|_1 \leq \| \hat{\beta}_L \|_1$ , which signifies that the DS may be more sparse than the Lasso.

It is well-known that  $\pmb{\Sigma} := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pmb{X}_{i}\pmb{X}_{i}^{T}$  is singular when  $p > n$ . To obtain oracle inequalities for the Lasso estimator with the minimax optimal rate (Ye and Zhang, 2010), the restricted eigenvalues proposed in Bickel et al. (2009) is usually needed. Let  $S(\pmb{\beta}^{*}) := \{j : \beta_{j}^{*} \neq 0, \pmb{\beta}^{*} = (\beta_{1}^{*}, \dots, \beta_{p}^{*})^{T}\}$  and  $s := |S(\pmb{\beta}^{*})|$ . For any vector  $\pmb{b} \in \mathbb{R}^p$  and any index set  $H \subset \{1,2,\dots,p\}$ , define the sub-vector indexed by  $H$  as  $\pmb{b}_{H} = (\dots, \tilde{b}_{j}, \dots)^{T} \in \mathbb{R}^{p}$  with  $\tilde{b}_{j} = b_{j}$  if  $j \in H$  and  $\tilde{b}_{j} = 0$  if  $j \notin H$ . Define the conic set for a sparse  $\pmb{\beta}^{*}$  with support  $S(\pmb{\beta}^{*})$ :

$$
C (\eta , S \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right)) = \left\{\boldsymbol {b} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}: \| \boldsymbol {b} _ {S \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \leq \eta \| \boldsymbol {b} _ {S \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right)} \| _ {1} \right\}, \eta > 0. \tag {8.13}
$$

Denote the restricted eigenvalue condition (RE) as  $RE(\eta, S(\pmb{\beta}^*), \pmb{\Sigma}) = \inf_{0 \neq \pmb{b} \in C(\eta, S(\pmb{\beta}^*))} \frac{(\pmb{b}^T \pmb{\Sigma} \pmb{b})^{1/2}}{\|\pmb{b}\|_2} > 0$  for any  $p \times p$  matrix  $\pmb{\Sigma}$ . In the following, we present a modified version of Theorem 7.2 in Bickel et al. (2009) from Lemma 2.5 of Li and Jia (2017) beyond Gaussian noise.

Proposition 8.2 (The rate of convergence of the Lasso). Suppose that  $\mathbf{X}$  is the fixed design matrix and the error sequence  $\{\varepsilon_i\}_{i=1}^n \stackrel{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim} N(0, \sigma^2)$  or  $\{\varepsilon_i / \sigma\}_{i=1}^n \stackrel{\mathrm{IID}}{\sim} 2-$  strongly log-concave distribution satisfying Lemma 3.2. Let  $\{\mathbf{X}_{(j)}\}_{j=1}^p \in \mathbb{R}^n$  be column vectors of  $\mathbf{X}$ . We assume that  $\frac{1}{n} \mathbf{X}_{(j)}^T \mathbf{X}_{(j)} = 1$ . If  $\lambda = A \sigma \sqrt{\log p / n}$  satisfies the KKT condition for  $\boldsymbol{\beta}^*$ ,

$$
\left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {T} \left(\mathbf {Y} - \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) / n \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \lambda / 2. \tag {8.14}
$$

(1) Then the estimated error  $\pmb{u} := \hat{\pmb{\beta}}_L - \pmb{\beta}^*$  satisfies  $\| \pmb{u}_{S(\pmb{\beta}^*)^c}\|_1 \leq 3\| \pmb{u}_{S(\pmb{\beta}^*)}\|_1$ , i.e.  $\pmb{u} \in C(3, S(\pmb{\beta}^*))$ .  
(2) Suppose that  $\mathbf{X}$  satisfies the RE condition  $\gamma \coloneqq \mathrm{RE}(3,S(\pmb{\beta}^{*}),\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\pmb{X}_{i}\pmb{X}_{i}^{T}) > 0$ . We have non-asymptotic oracle inequalities with probability greater than  $1 - 2p^{1 - \frac{A^2}{8}}$ :

$$
(a). \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq \frac {1 2 A \sigma}{\gamma} s \sqrt {\frac {\log p}{n}}; (b). \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {9 A \sigma^ {2}}{\gamma^ {2}} \frac {s \log p}{n}; \tag {8.15}
$$

$$
(c). \frac {1}{n} \| \mathbf {X} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {9 A \sigma}{\gamma} \frac {s \log p}{n}, A > 2 \sqrt {2}. \tag {8.16}
$$

Proof. The proof consists of 3 steps: 1. Checking  $\hat{\pmb{\beta}}_L - \pmb{\beta}^*$  be in cone set by using definition of Lasso and KKT conditions; 2. Verifying the high probability of the KKT condition; 3. Deriving the oracle inequalities from restricted eigenvalue condition with some elementary inequalities.

Step1: By the Lasso optimization (8.11),

$$
(2 n) ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {Y} - \mathbf {X} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} \| _ {1} \leq (2 n) ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {Y} - \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.17}
$$

From  $\frac{\|\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L\|_2^2}{2n} = \frac{1}{2n}\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^* + \varepsilon - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L\|_2^2 = \frac{1}{2n}\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^* - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L\|_2^2 + \frac{\|\varepsilon\|_2^2}{2n} - \frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^T\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L - \boldsymbol{\beta}^*)$  and  $\frac{1}{2n}\|\mathbf{Y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^*\|_2^2 = \frac{\|\varepsilon\|_2^2}{2n}$ , thus  $\frac{1}{2n}\|\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^* - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L\|_2^2 + \frac{\|\varepsilon\|_2^2}{2n} - \frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^T\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^*) + \lambda\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_L\|_1 \leq \frac{\|\varepsilon\|_2^2}{2n} + \lambda\|\boldsymbol{\beta}^*\|_1$ . Then,

$$
(2 n) ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {X} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} \| _ {1} \leq n ^ {- 1} \varepsilon^ {T} \mathbf {X} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.18}
$$

The (8.18) is usually called the basic inequality in the proof of Lasso oracle inequalities. The first term in the left side of inequality (8.18) is the empirical prediction error, while on the right side,  $\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^T\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^*)$  is random and  $\lambda ||\boldsymbol {\beta}^{*}||_{1}$  is still fixed and unknown. For  $\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^T\mathbf{X}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^*)$ , if we can get a sharper upper bound and it approaching 0 as  $n\to \infty$ , then we can achieve a sharper oracle inequality in below. By (8.14),

$$
\frac {\left\| \mathbf {X} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{2 n} + \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1} \leq \left\| \frac {1}{n} \varepsilon^ {T} \mathbf {X} \right\| _ {\infty} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq \frac {\lambda}{2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.19}
$$

Let  $S \coloneqq S(\pmb{\beta}^{*})$  and notes that  $||\hat{\pmb{\beta}}_S||_1 = ||\pmb{\beta}_S^* + (\hat{\pmb{\beta}}_S - \pmb{\beta}_S^*)||_1 \geq ||\pmb{\beta}_S^*||_1 - ||\hat{\pmb{\beta}}_S - \pmb{\beta}_S^*||_1$ , then

$$
\left| \left| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \right| \right| _ {1} = \left| \left| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S ^ {c}} \right| \right| _ {1} + \left| \left| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S} \right| \right| _ {1} \geq \left| \left| \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \right| \right| _ {1} - \left| \left| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S} - \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \right| \right| _ {1} + \left| \left| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S ^ {c}} \right| \right| _ {1}. \tag {8.20}
$$

From (8.19), we get  $\| u_{S^c}\| _1\leq 3\| u_S\| _1$  be checking

$$
\begin{array}{l} 0 \leq (2 n) ^ {- 1} | | \mathbf {X} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) | | _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \lambda | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1} / 2 + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1} \\ \leq \frac {\lambda}{2} \left\{\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S} - \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \right\} + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \lambda \left\{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S} - \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \right\} [ \text {B y (8 . 2 0)} ] \\ = \frac {3 \lambda}{2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S} - \boldsymbol {\beta} _ {S} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \frac {\lambda}{2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} =: \frac {3 \lambda}{2} \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S} \| _ {1} - \frac {\lambda}{2} \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.21} \\ \end{array}
$$

Step2: The Gaussian error vector  $\varepsilon$  enables us to get the Gaussian concentration around its mean, we can shows that (8.14) occurs with a high probability. So next we need to check the Lipschitz condition in Lemma 3.1. Use Lemma 3.1, it implies that

$$
P \left(n ^ {- 1} \left| \boldsymbol {X} _ {(j)} ^ {T} \left(\boldsymbol {Y} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right| \geq t\right) \leq 2 e ^ {- n t ^ {2} / 2 \sigma^ {2}}, \forall j. \tag {8.22}
$$

under the presupposition  $\| \mathbf{X}_{(j)}\| _2^2 = \mathbf{X}_{(j)}^T\mathbf{X}_{(j)} = n$ . The Lipschitz condition depends on the design matrix  $\mathbf{X}$ . The different types of CIs require different assumptions on the design matrix (the random design is allowed if we adopt empirical process theory). In Lemma 3.1, put  $f_{j}(\pmb {a}):= \frac{1}{n}\pmb{X}_{(j)}^{T}(\sigma \pmb {a} + \pmb {X}\pmb{\beta}^{*})$ . Then, Cauchy's inequality implies,

$$
f _ {j} (\boldsymbol {a}) - f _ {j} (\boldsymbol {b}) \leq \frac {\sigma}{n} \left| \boldsymbol {X} _ {(j)} ^ {T} (\boldsymbol {b} - \boldsymbol {a}) \right| \leq \frac {\sigma}{n} \| \boldsymbol {X} _ {(j)} \| _ {2} \cdot \| \boldsymbol {b} - \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2} = \frac {\sigma}{\sqrt {n}} \| \boldsymbol {b} - \boldsymbol {a} \| _ {2} \forall j.
$$

Hence,  $f_{j}(\pmb{a})$  is  $\sigma / \sqrt{n}$ -Lipschitz for all  $j$ . Recall  $\lambda = A\sigma \sqrt{\log p / n}$ . So (8.22) implies

$$
P (\| \frac {1}{n} \pmb {X} ^ {T} (\pmb {Y} - \pmb {X} \pmb {\beta} ^ {*}) \| _ {\infty} \geq \frac {\lambda}{2}) \leq \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} P (\frac {1}{n} | \pmb {X} _ {(j)} ^ {T} (\pmb {Y} - \pmb {X} \pmb {\beta} ^ {*}) | \geq \frac {1}{2} A \sigma \sqrt {\frac {\log p}{n}}) \leq 2 p ^ {1 - \frac {A ^ {2}}{8}}.
$$

By Lemma 3.2, (8.22) is also held for  $\{\varepsilon_i / \sigma \}_{i = 1}^n\sim 2$  -strongly log-concave distributions.

Step3: Next we can start on the proof based on cone set condition (8.13). Since the  $\mathbf{X}$  satisfies RE condition  $\gamma \coloneqq \mathrm{RE}(3,S,n^{-1}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{T}) > 0$ , by (8.13) we have

$$
\gamma \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{n} \| \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \stackrel {(8. 2 1)} {\leq} \lambda \left(3 \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S} \| _ {1} - \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \leq 3 \lambda \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S} \| _ {1} \leq 3 \lambda \sqrt {s} \| \boldsymbol {u} _ {S} \| _ {2} \leq 3 \lambda \sqrt {s} \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2},
$$

where the second last inequality is by Cauchy's inequality. Therefore,

$$
\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2} =: \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {9 \lambda^ {2} s}{\gamma^ {2}} = \frac {9 A ^ {2} \sigma^ {2}}{\gamma^ {2}} \frac {s \log p}{n}, \quad \frac {\| \mathbf {X} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} _ {L} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{n} =: \frac {\| \mathbf {X} \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{n} \leq \frac {9 \lambda^ {2} s}{\gamma} = \frac {9 A ^ {2} \sigma^ {2}}{\gamma} \frac {s \log p}{n}.
$$

Lastly, by Cauchy's inequality and  $\| \pmb{u}_{S^c}\| _1\leq 3\| \pmb{u}_S\| _1$

$$
\| \hat {\pmb {\beta}} _ {\lambda} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} =: \| \pmb {u} \| _ {1} = \| \pmb {u} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} + \| \pmb {u} _ {S} \| _ {1} \leq 4 \| \pmb {u} _ {S} \| _ {1} \leq 4 \sqrt {s} \| \pmb {u} \| _ {2} \leq \frac {1 2 \lambda s}{\gamma} = \frac {1 2 A \sigma}{\gamma} s \sqrt {\frac {\log p}{n}}.
$$

![](images/3129c242d827edc8bb62a773eeca6c983751b8c0a511fb45391462d999b36e6d.jpg)

According to (8.5), the OLS with diverging number of covariates has the convergence rate  $O(\sqrt{p / n})$  under the minimal eigenvalue condition  $\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{X}^T\mathbf{X}) = O(n)$ . In contrast, due to the sparse restriction and the RE condition in Proposition 8.2, the factor  $\sqrt{\log p}$  is much more small than the factor  $\sqrt{p}$  in the convergence rate (8.5). Under the RE condition,

Proposition 8.2 reveals that Lasso is  $\ell_2$ -consistent if  $\frac{s\log p}{n} \to 0$ , and  $s\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n}} \to 0$  guarantees  $\ell_1$ -consistency. Theorem 7.1 in Bickel et al. (2009) also gives oracle inequalities (8.15) and (8.16) for the DS estimator (8.12).

# 8.4 High-dimensional Poisson regressions with random design

The Poisson regression (McCullagh and Nelder, 1983) is a model for nonnegative integers response variables, i.e.  $Y_{i}^{\mathrm{IID}}$  Poisson  $(\lambda_{i})$ , where  $\log (\lambda_i) = X_i^T\beta$  for  $i = 1,\dots ,n$ . We presume that the  $\{X_{i}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  are IID r.vs on some space  $\mathcal{X}$ , and we observe  $n$  copies of  $\{(Y_{i},X_{i})\}_{i = 1}^{n}\sim (Y,X)\in \mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}^{p}$ . The average negative log-likelihood of Poisson regressions is  $\ell_n(\beta)\coloneqq -\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}[Y_iX_i^T\beta -e^{X_i^T\beta}]$  and the Lasso penalized estimator is

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} := \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} (\lambda) = \operatorname {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\ell_ {n} (\boldsymbol {\beta}) + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1} \right\} \text {w i t h a t u r n i n g p a r a m e t e r} \lambda > 0. \tag {8.23}
$$

Lemma 4.2 in Buhlmann and van de Geer (2011) shows the first-order conditions for the optimization in (8.23).

Lemma 8.4 (Necessary and sufficient condition). Let  $j \in \{1,2,\dots ,p\}$  and  $\lambda >0$ . Then, a necessary and sufficient condition for the Lasso estimates (8.23) is

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{c l} n ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} \left(Y _ {i} - e ^ {\mathbf {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \hat {\beta}}\right) = - \lambda \operatorname {s i g n} \left(\hat {\beta} _ {j}\right) & \text {i f} \hat {\beta} _ {j} \neq 0, \\ | n ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} \left(Y _ {i} - e ^ {\mathbf {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \hat {\beta}}\right) | \leq \lambda & \text {i f} \hat {\beta} _ {j} = 0. \end{array} \right. \tag {8.24}
$$

Let  $l(Y, X, \beta) = -YX^T\beta + e^{X^T}\beta$  be the Poisson loss function. The true coefficient  $\beta^*$  is the minimizer of the expected Poisson loss, i.e.

$$
\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} = \operatorname {a r g m i n} _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \operatorname {E l} (Y, X, \boldsymbol {\beta}). \tag {8.25}
$$

The KKT condition of the  $\ell_1$ -penalized likelihood is for the estimated parameter. But, here we use the true parameter version of the KKT conditions:  $|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)| \leq \lambda$ ,  $j = 1,\dots,p$  by replacing  $e^{X_i^T\hat{\beta}}$  by  $\mathrm{E}Y_i = e^{X_i^T\beta^*}$  to approximate the estimated version (8.24). To motivate the next two propositions concerning high-probability events, let us consider the following notations and the decomposition of empirical process.

The Poisson loss  $l(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X}, \pmb{Y}) = l_{1}(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X}, \pmb{Y}) + l_{2}(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X})$  is decomposed into two parts where  $l_{1}(\pmb{\beta}):=l_{1}(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X}, \pmb{Y}):= -\pmb{Y}\pmb{X}^{T}\pmb{\beta}$  and  $l_{2}(\pmb{\beta}):=l_{2}(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X}):=e^{\pmb{X}^{T}\pmb{\beta}}$  is free of response. Let  $\mathbb{P}l(\pmb{\beta}):= \operatorname{El}(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{X}, \pmb{Y})$  be the expected loss. We are interested in the centralized empirical loss  $(\mathbb{P}_{n} - \mathbb{P})l(\pmb{\beta})$  representing fluctuations between the expected and empirical losses. Note that

$$
\left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}\right) l (\boldsymbol {\beta}) = \left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}\right) l _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\beta}) + \left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}\right) l _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\beta}), \tag {8.26}
$$

which is crucial in attaining the convergence rate of  $\| \hat{\beta} -\beta^{*}\|_{1}$ . Motivated from rate of convergence theorem [Theorem 3.2.5 of van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)] for M-estimation with functional parameter in some metric space, we study the upper bounds (or the rate) for the first and second part of the difference of the centralized empirical process between  $\pmb{\beta}^{*}$  and  $\hat{\beta}$ :  $(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_m(\pmb {\beta}^*) - l_m(\hat{\pmb{\beta}}))$ , for  $m = 1,2$ .

Proposition 8.3 (Convergence rate of  $(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_1(\boldsymbol{\beta}^*) - l_1(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})))$ . Suppose that

$$
\sup  _ {1 \leq i \leq \infty} \| X _ {i} \| _ {\infty} \leq L <   \infty \text {a . s . a n d} \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq B. \tag {8.27}
$$

In the event of  $\mathcal{A} \coloneqq \bigcap_{j=1}^{p}\left\{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)\right| \leq \frac{\lambda}{4}\right\}$ , we have

$$
\left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}\right) \left(l _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) - l _ {1} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}})\right) \leq \frac {\lambda}{4} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.28}
$$

If  $\lambda \geq \max \left\{\frac{16A^2L\log(2p)}{3n},8ALe^{LB / 2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2p)}{n}}\right\}$  with  $A > 1$  we have  $P(\mathcal{A})\geq 1 - (2p)^{1 - A^2}$ .

Proof. Note that, on the event  $\mathcal{A}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}) (l _ {1} (\pmb {\beta} ^ {*}) - l _ {1} (\hat {\pmb {\beta}})) = \frac {- 1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}) \pmb {X} _ {i} ^ {T} (\pmb {\beta} ^ {*} - \hat {\pmb {\beta}}) = \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} (\hat {\beta} _ {j} - \beta_ {j} ^ {*}) \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} (Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}) \\ \leq \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {p} | \hat {\beta} _ {j} - \beta_ {j} ^ {*} | \cdot | \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} (Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i}) | \stackrel {\mathcal {A}} {\leq} \frac {\lambda}{4} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Next, we show that  $\mathcal{A}$  is a high probability event if the  $\lambda$  is well chosen. For  $j = 1,\dots,p$  and  $i = 1,\dots,n$ ,  $P(\mathcal{A}^c)\leq \sum_{j = 1}^{p}P\{| \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}X_{ij}(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)| > \frac{\lambda}{4}\}$ . Given  $\mathbf{X}$ ,  $\{S_{nj}(Y,X):= \frac{1}{n} X_{ij}(Y_i - \mathrm{E}Y_i)\}_{i = 1}^n$  are conditional independent for each  $j = 1,\ldots ,p$ . Thus Corollary 5.2 with  $w_{i} = X_{ij} / n$  gives

$$
P \left(\left| S _ {n j} (Y, X) \right| \geq t | \mathbf {X}\right) \leq 2 \exp \left\{- \frac {n t ^ {2} / 2}{\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} e ^ {\mathbf {X} _ {i} ^ {T}} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} X _ {i j} ^ {2} + \max  _ {1 \leq i \leq n} \frac {| X _ {i j} | t}{3}} \right\} \leq 2 \left(e ^ {\frac {- n t ^ {2}}{4 L ^ {2} e ^ {L B}}} \vee e ^ {\frac {- 3 n t}{4 L}}\right) \tag {8.29}
$$

where the last inequality is from  $\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{a}{b + c}} \leq \mathrm{e}^{\frac{-a}{2b}} \vee \mathrm{e}^{\frac{-a}{2c}}$  for any positive numbers  $a, b$  and  $c$ . Let  $t = \frac{\lambda}{4}$ . Assumptions (8.27) and (8.29) give for  $j = 1, \dots, p$

$$
P \big (| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} \big (Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i} \big) | \geq \frac {\lambda}{4} \big) = \mathrm {E} P \big (| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i j} \big (Y _ {i} - \mathrm {E} Y _ {i} \big) | \geq \frac {\lambda}{4} | \mathbf {X} \big) \leq 2 \max \{e ^ {\frac {- n \lambda^ {2}}{6 4 L ^ {2} e ^ {L B}}}, e ^ {\frac {- 3 n \lambda}{1 6 L}} \},
$$

which implies that  $P(\mathcal{A}^c) \leq 2p\max \left\{e^{\frac{-n\lambda^2}{64L^2e^{LB}}}, e^{-\frac{-3n\lambda}{16L}}\right\}$ .

Finally, if  $\lambda \geq \max \left\{\frac{16A^2L\log(2p)}{3n},8ALe^{LB / 2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2p)}{n}}\right\} (A > 1)$ , so  $P(\mathcal{A}^c) \leq (2p)^{1 - A^2}$ .

![](images/19bf11907acdbf596db7a26bb559869cfc8a9cd4142835adc59e279fc738f401.jpg)

Next, we provide a crucial lemma to bound  $(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta}))$ . Let  $\nu_n(\pmb{\beta},\pmb{\beta}^*) \coloneqq \frac{(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta}))}{\|\pmb{\beta} - \pmb{\beta}^*\|_1}$  the normalized empirical process indexed by  $\pmb{\beta}$ . Denote the  $\ell_1$ -ball by  $S_M(\pmb{\beta}^*) \coloneqq \{\pmb{\beta} \in \mathbb{R}^p : \| \pmb{\beta} - \pmb{\beta}^* \|_1 \leq M < \infty\}$ , we define the local stochastic Lipschitz constant:

[ Z_M(\pmb{\beta}^*) := \sup_{\pmb{\beta} \in S_M(\pmb{\beta}^*)} |\nu_n(\pmb{\beta}, \pmb{\beta}^*)| \text{ and a random event } \mathcal{B} := \{Z_M(\pmb{\beta}^*) \leq \lambda_1 / 4\}. ]

It is easy to see  $|\nu_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})|\leq \sup_{\mathcal{S}_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})}|\nu_{n}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})|\leq \frac{\lambda_{1}}{4}$ , which gives  $|(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_2(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) - l_2(\boldsymbol{\beta}^*))|\leq \frac{\lambda_1}{4}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^*\|_1$ , provided that  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in \mathcal{S}_M(\boldsymbol{\beta}^*)$ . Then we have follow result.

Proposition 8.4 (Convergence rate of  $(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\hat{\pmb{\beta}})))$ . Assume that there exists a large constant  $M$  such that  $\hat{\pmb{\beta}}$  is in the  $\ell_1$ -ball  $\mathcal{S}_M(\pmb{\beta}^*)$ . Under assumption (8.27), we have

$$
P \left(Z _ {M} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \geq 5 A L e ^ {L B} \sqrt {\frac {\log 2 p}{n}}\right) \leq (2 p) ^ {- A ^ {2}}. \tag {8.30}
$$

If  $\lambda \geq 20ALe^{LB}\sqrt{\frac{2\log 2p}{n}}$ , we get  $P\{|(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})(l_2(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) - l_2(\boldsymbol{\beta}^*))| \leq \frac{\lambda}{4} (\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^*\|_1)\} \geq 1 - (2p)^{-A^2}$ .

Proof. In the first step, we apply following McDiarmid's inequality to  $Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})$  by showing that  $Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})$  is fluctuated of no more than  $\frac{2e^{LB}}{n}$ . Let us check it. Put  $\mathbb{P}_n \coloneqq \frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^n 1_{X_j,Y_j}$  and  $\mathbb{P}_n' \coloneqq \frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^n 1_{X_j,Y_j} + 1_{X_i',Y_i'}$ , where  $(X_i',Y_i')$  is the independent copy of  $(X_j,Y_j)$ .

Let  $X_{i}^{T}\tilde{\beta}_{i}(X_{i}^{\prime T}\tilde{\beta}_{i})$  be an intermediate point between  $X_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol {\beta}(X_{i}^{\prime T}\boldsymbol {\beta})$  and  $X_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}(X_{i}^{\prime T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})$  from the Taylor's expansion of function  $F(x)\coloneqq e^{x}$ . It deduces

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {| (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}) (l _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) - l _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\beta})) |}{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1}} - \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {| (\mathbb {P} _ {n} ^ {\prime} - \mathbb {P}) (l _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) - l _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}})) |}{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1}} \\ \leq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {\left| l _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} , \boldsymbol {X} _ {i}\right) - l _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} , \boldsymbol {X} _ {i}\right) - l _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} , \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) + l _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} , \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {\prime}\right) \right|}{n \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1}} \\ \leq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {1}{n} e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} \cdot \frac {\left| \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} \right|}{\left\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \right\| _ {1}} + \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {1}{n} e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} \cdot \frac {\left| \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right|}{\left\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \right\| _ {1}} \leq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in S _ {M}} \frac {2 L e ^ {L B}}{n} \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \right\| _ {1}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \right\| _ {1}} = \frac {2 L e ^ {L B}}{n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first inequality stems from  $|f(x)| - \sup_{x} |g(x)| \leq |f(x) - g(x)|$  (and take suprema over  $x$  again).

Apply McDiarmid's inequality to  $Z_{M}(\pmb{\beta}^{*})$ , we have  $P(Z_{M}(\pmb{\beta}^{*}) - \mathrm{E}Z_{M}(\pmb{\beta}^{*}) \geq \lambda) \leq e^{-\frac{n\lambda^{2}}{2L^{2}e^{2LB}}}$ . Let  $(2p)^{-A^{2}} = \exp \left\{-\frac{n\lambda^{2}}{2L^{2}e^{2LB}}\right\}$ , we get  $\lambda \geq ALe^{LB}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2p)}{n}}$  for  $A > 0$ , therefore

$$
P \left(Z _ {M} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) - \mathrm {E Z} _ {M} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \geq \lambda\right) \leq (2 p) ^ {- A ^ {2}}. \tag {8.31}
$$

The next step is to estimate the sharper upper bounds of  $\mathrm{EZ}_M(\pmb{\beta}^*)$  by Lemma 7.3 with  $\Phi(t) = |t|$  and Lemma 7.4. Note that  $(\mathbb{P}_n - \mathbb{P})\{l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta})\} = \mathbb{P}_n\{l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta})\} - \mathrm{E}\{l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta})\}$ , by symmetrization theorem, the expected terms is canceled. To see construction theorem, for  $Z_M(\pmb{\beta}^*) = \sup_{\pmb{\beta} \in S_M}\left\{\frac{1}{n\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(e^{X_i^T\pmb{\beta}^*} - e^{X_i^T\pmb{\beta}}) - n\mathrm{E}[l_2(\pmb{\beta}^*) - l_2(\pmb{\beta})]\right|\right\}$ , it is required to check the Lipschitz property of  $g_i$  in Lemma 7.4 with  $\mathcal{F} = \mathbb{R}^p$ . Let  $f(x_i) = x_i^T\pmb{\beta}/\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1$ ,  $h(x_i) = x_i^T\pmb{\beta}^*/\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1$  and  $g_i(t) = \frac{e^{t\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1}}{n\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1}$  ( $|t| \leq LB/\|\pmb{\beta}^* - \pmb{\beta}\|_1$ ). Then the function  $g_i(t)$  here is  $\frac{e^{LB}}{n}$ -Lipschitz. In fact

$$
| g _ {i} (s) - g _ {i} (t) | = \frac {e ^ {\tilde {t}}}{n} \cdot | s - t | \leq \frac {e ^ {L B}}{n} | s - t |, t, s \in [ - L B / \| \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} - \pmb {\beta} \| _ {1}, L B / \| \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} - \pmb {\beta} \| _ {1} ]
$$

where  $\tilde{t} \in [-LB / \| \pmb{\beta}^{*} - \pmb{\beta}\|_{1}, LB / \| \pmb{\beta}^{*} - \pmb{\beta}\|_{1}]$  is an intermediate point between  $t$  and  $s$  given by applying Lagrange mean value theorem.

The symmetrization theorem and the contraction theorem imply

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {E} Z _ {M} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \leq \frac {4 e ^ {L B}}{n} \operatorname {E} \left(\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathcal {S} _ {M}} \left| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {\epsilon_ {i} \boldsymbol {X} _ {i} ^ {T} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} - \boldsymbol {\beta}\right)}{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}} \right|\right) \leq \frac {4 e ^ {L B}}{n} \operatorname {E} \left(\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} \in \mathcal {S} _ {M}} \max  _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i j} | \cdot \frac {\| \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}}{\| \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}}\right) \\ \leq \frac {4 e ^ {L B}}{n} \mathrm {E} (\max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i j} |) = \frac {4 e ^ {L B}}{n} \mathrm {E} (\mathrm {E} [ \max _ {1 \leq j \leq p} | \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \epsilon_ {i} X _ {i j} | | \mathbf {X} ]). \\ \end{array}
$$

From Corollary 7.5, with  $\mathrm{E}_{\epsilon}[\epsilon_{i}X_{ij}|\mathbf{X}] = 0$  we get  $\frac{4e^{LB}}{n}\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{E}[\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}X_{ij}||\mathbf{X}])\leq \frac{4e^{LB}}{n}\sqrt{2\log 2p}$

$\sqrt{nL^2} = 4e^{LB}L\sqrt{\frac{2\log 2p}{n}}$  . Thus, for  $A\geq 1$

$$
\operatorname {E Z} _ {M} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \leq 4 e ^ {L B} L \sqrt {\frac {2 \log 2 p}{n}} \leq 4 A L e ^ {L B} \sqrt {\frac {2 \log 2 p}{n}}. \tag {8.32}
$$

With  $\lambda \geq ALe^{LB}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2p)}{n}}$  and (8.32), we conclude from (8.31) that  $P(Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}) \geq 5ALe^{LB}\sqrt{\frac{\log 2p}{n}}) \leq P(Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}) \geq \lambda + \mathrm{E}Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})) \leq (2p)^{-A^{2}}$ . Finally, we complete the proof of Proposition 8.4 by letting  $\frac{\lambda}{4} \geq 5ALe^{LB}\sqrt{\frac{2\log 2p}{n}}$  and setting  $\boldsymbol{\beta} = \hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} \in Z_{M}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})$ .

Let  $S \coloneqq S(\beta^{*})$  for  $\beta^{*}$  defined in (8.25) and  $s \coloneqq |S|$ . To obtain sharp oracle inequalities for Lasso penalized Poisson regression, we consider the following regularity conditions:

(H.1): The covariate  $X$  is almost surely bounded  $\| X\|_{\infty}\leq L$  a.s.for  $L > 0$  
(H.2): There exists a constant  $B > 0$  such that  $\| \pmb{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq B$  
(H.3): (Stabil Condition) For  $\Sigma \coloneqq \operatorname{E}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^T)$ , there exist a  $k\in (0,1)$  such that

$$
\delta^ {T} \Sigma \delta \geqslant k \sum_ {j \in S} \delta_ {j} ^ {2} \text {f o r a n y} \delta \in C (c _ {0}, S) := \left\{\delta \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}: \sum_ {j \in S ^ {c}} | \delta_ {j} | \leq c _ {0} \sum_ {j \in S} | \delta_ {j} | \right\}.
$$

The Stabil Condition (H.3) is denoted as  $S(c_0, S, k, \Sigma)$  which is a similar version of the RE condition in the Lasso linear models proposed in Bunea (2008). Due to the random variance, Poisson regression is more complex than the linear model with the constant variance assumption. Thus, (H.1) and (H.2) are stronger than those assumed for the linear models. Based on the high-probability event  $\mathcal{A}$  and  $\mathcal{B}$ , we have the oracle inequalities for estimation and prediction for Lasso estimator  $\hat{\beta}$  in (8.23) for the Poisson regressions.

Theorem 8.1. Assume conditions (H.1)-(H.3) hold. Let  $\lambda$  be chosen such that

$$
\lambda \geq \max  \left\{\frac {1 6 A ^ {2} L \log (2 p)}{3 n}, 8 A L e ^ {L B / 2} \sqrt {\frac {\log (2 p)}{n}}, 2 0 A L e ^ {L B} \sqrt {\frac {2 \log 2 p}{n}} \right\} f o r A > \sqrt {2}. \tag {8.33}
$$

Suppose that we have a new covariate vector  $\mathbf{X}^*$  (as the test data) which is an independent copy of  $X$  (as the training data), and  $\mathrm{E}^*$  represents the expectation w.r.t.  $\mathbf{X}^*$  only, then

$$
P (\mathrm {E} ^ {*} [ \pmb {X} ^ {*} (\hat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*}) ] ^ {2} \leq \frac {1 2 e ^ {1 0 L B}}{k} s \lambda^ {2}) a n d P (\| \hat {\pmb {\beta}} - \pmb {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq \frac {4 e ^ {5 L B}}{k} s \lambda) \geq 1 - (2 p) ^ {1 - A ^ {2}} - (2 p) ^ {- A ^ {2} / 2}.
$$

The Theorem 8.1 leads to the persistence and  $\ell_1$ -consistency if  $\max \{s\lambda, s\lambda^2\} \to 0$ .

Proof. The proof consists of three steps. The techniques are adapted from Zhang and Jia (2022), Huang et al. (2021) and references therein.

Step1: Check  $\hat{\beta} - \beta^{*} \in C(3, S)$ . From the definition of the Lasso estimates  $\hat{\beta}$  (see (8.23)),

$$
\mathbb {P} _ {n} l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) + \lambda | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} | | _ {1} \leq \mathbb {P} _ {n} l \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) + \lambda | | \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1}. \tag {8.34}
$$

By adding  $\mathbb{P}(l(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) - l(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})) + \frac{\lambda}{2} ||\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}||_{1}$  to both sides of (8.34), we have

$$
\mathbb {P} (l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) - l (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*})) + \frac {\lambda}{2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \leq (\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}) (l (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) - l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}})) + \frac {\lambda}{2} \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda (| | \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1} - | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} | | _ {1}),
$$

which leads

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} (l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) - l (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*})) + \frac {\lambda}{2} | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1} \leq \left(\mathbb {P} _ {n} - \mathbb {P}\right) (l (\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) - l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}})) + \frac {\lambda}{2} | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1} + \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1}\right) \\ \leq \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1}\right). \tag {8.35} \\ \end{array}
$$

By the definition of  $\beta^{*}$ ,  $\mathbb{P}(l(\hat{\pmb{\beta}}) - l(\pmb{\beta}^{*}))\geq 0$ . The above inequality and the fact:  $|\hat{\beta}_j - \beta_j^* | + |\beta_j^* | - |\hat{\beta}_j| = 0$  for  $j\notin S$  and  $|\hat{\beta}_j| - |\beta_j^* |\leq |\hat{\beta}_j - \beta_j^* |$  for  $j\in S$  lead to

$$
\lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} / 2 \leq \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1}\right) \leq 2 \lambda \| \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} \| _ {1}. \tag {8.36}
$$

Thus,  $\frac{\lambda}{2} ||(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})_{S^{c}}||_{1}\leq 1.5\lambda ||(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})_{S}||_{1}$  and then  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\in C(3,S)$ .

Step2: Choosing  $\lambda$ . Since  $\mathbb{P}(l(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}) - l(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})) \geq 0$ , (8.35) implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} / 2 \leq \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1}\right) \\ \begin{array}{l} \leq \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1} + \lambda (\| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1} - \| \boldsymbol {\beta} \| _ {1}) \\ \leq \lambda \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1} + \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} + \lambda (\| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} - \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} \| _ {1}) = 2 \lambda \| \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}. \end{array} \tag {8.37} \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus (H.2) implies  $||\hat{\beta} - \beta^{*}||_{1} \leq 4B$ . After having shown Propositions 8.3 and 8.4, we need the result on the high probability of the event  $\mathcal{A} \cap \mathcal{B}$ , whose proof is skipped.

Proposition 8.5. Under the event  $\mathcal{A} \cap \mathcal{B}$  with (H.1)-(H.3), we have  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} \in S_{4B}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^*)$ . And if  $\lambda$  are chosen as (8.33), then  $P(\mathcal{A} \cap \mathcal{B}) \geq 1 - (2p)^{1 - A^2} - (2p)^{-A^2 / 2}$ .

Step3: Error bounds from Stabil Condition. As  $X^{*}$  is an independent copy of  $X$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. \mathbb {P} \left\{l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) - l \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right\} = \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left[ \mathrm {E} \left\{l \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}\right) - l \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \mid \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} \right\} \right] := \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left\{\mathrm {E} \left[ - Y \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) + e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta}} - e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}} \right] \mid \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} \right\} \right| _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} \\ = \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left\{\mathrm {E} [ - Y | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} ] \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) + (e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} - e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}}) ] | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} \right\} | _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} ^ {\prime}} \left(\mathrm {E} ^ {*} [ Y | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} ] = e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}}\right) \\ = \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left\{- e ^ {X ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}} + e ^ {X ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}} + 2 ^ {- 1} e ^ {X ^ {* T} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} [ \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ] ^ {2} \right\} | _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} = 2 ^ {- 1} \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left\{e ^ {X ^ {* T} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}}} [ \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} (\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ] ^ {2} \right\} | _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb{X}^{*T}\tilde{\pmb{\beta}} = (1 - t)\pmb{X}^{*T}\pmb{\beta}^{*} + t\pmb{X}^{*T}\hat{\pmb{\beta}}$  is an intermediate point of  $\pmb{X}^{*T}$  and  $\pmb{X}^{*T}\hat{\pmb{\beta}}$  with  $t\in [0,1]$ .

Note that  $\| \pmb{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq B$  by (H.1) and  $||\hat{\pmb{\beta}} -\pmb{\beta}^{*}||_{1}\leq 4B,$  (H.2) yields

$$
| \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\beta}} | \leq t | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | + | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | \leq | | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {*} | | _ {\infty} \cdot | | \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | | _ {1} + | \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} | \leq 4 L B + L B = 5 L B,
$$

which implies for  $c \coloneqq e^{-5LB} / 2$

$$
\left. \mathbb {P} \left\{l (\hat {\beta}) - l \left(\beta^ {*}\right) \right\} \geq \inf  _ {| t | \leq 5 L B} 2 ^ {- 1} \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left\{e ^ {\boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \tilde {\beta}} \left[ \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right] ^ {2} \right\} \right| _ {\boldsymbol {\beta} = \hat {\beta}} =: c \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left[ \boldsymbol {X} ^ {* T} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right] ^ {2}. \tag {8.38}
$$

As  $\mathrm{E}^{*}(\mathbf{X}^{*}\mathbf{X}^{*T}) = \pmb{\Sigma},\mathrm{E}^{*}[\mathbf{X}^{*}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})]^{2} = (\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\pmb {\Sigma}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}).$

Having checked the cone condition  $C(3,S)$ , we apply the Stabil Condition

$$
c \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \geq c k | | \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} | | _ {2} ^ {2}. \tag {8.39}
$$

From (8.35), (8.36) and (8.38), we get

$$
c \mathrm {E} ^ {*} \left[ X ^ {*} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) \right] ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{2} \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {1} \leq \mathbb {P} \left(l (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}}) - l \left(\boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right)\right) + \frac {\lambda}{2} \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {1} \leq 2 \lambda \left\| \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} \right\| _ {1}, \tag {8.40}
$$

which gives  $ck||(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})_{S}||_{2}^{2} + \frac{\lambda}{2} ||\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}||_{1} \leq 2\lambda ||(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})_{S}||_{1}$  by plugging (8.39) into (8.40). Then, employing Cauchy's inequality, we have

$$
2 c k \left\| \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \lambda \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {1} \leq 4 \lambda \left(s \cdot \left\| \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \leq 4 t \lambda^ {2} s + \frac {1}{t} \left\| \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}\right) _ {S} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {8.41}
$$

where the last inequality is from the elementary inequality  $2xy \leq tx^2 + y^2 / t$  for all  $t > 0$ . Let us set  $t = (2ck)^{-1}$  in (8.41), thus  $\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} - \boldsymbol{\beta}^*\|_1 \leq 4t\lambda s = \frac{2\lambda s}{ck} = \frac{4e^{5LB}}{k} s\lambda$ .

To derive the oracle inequality of prediction error, from (8.40), we obtain

$$
c \mathrm {E} ^ {*} [ X ^ {*} (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) ] ^ {2} \leq 1. 5 \lambda | | (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) _ {S} | | _ {1} \leq 1. 5 \lambda | | (\hat {\boldsymbol {\beta}} - \boldsymbol {\beta} ^ {*}) | | _ {1}
$$

which implies  $\mathrm{E}^{*}[X^{*}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})]^{2}\leq 1.5\lambda ||(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}} -\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})||_{1} / c\leq \frac{3s\lambda^{2}}{c^{2}k} = \frac{12e^{10LB}}{k}s\lambda^{2}$ , where the last inequality is from  $\| \hat{\pmb{\beta}} -\pmb{\beta}^{*}\|_{1}\leq \frac{4e^{5LB}}{k}s\lambda$

For general losses beyond linear models, the crucial techniques in the non-asymptotical analysis of increasing-dimensional and high-dimensional regressions, which are Bahadur representation's for the M-estimator (Kuchibhotla, 2018; Pan and Zhou, 2020) and concentration for Lipschitz loss functions (Buhlmann and van de Geer, 2011; Zhang and Jia, 2022), respectively. In large-dimensional regressions with  $p / n \to c$ , the theory of random matrix (Yao et al., 2015), leave-one-out analysis (El Karoui, 2013; Lei et al., 2018) and approximate message passing (Donoho and Montanari, 2016; El Karoui, 2013, 2018) play important roles for obtaining asymptotical results.

# 9 Extensions

The review has been focused on the sum of independent r.vs in the Euclidean space. However, independence structure may not be suitable for some applications, for instance, econometrics, survival analysis, and graphical models. At the same time, the Euclidean valued r.vs may not be appropriate for functional data and image data. In the following we point out results in settings not covered to broaden this review.

By CIs for the martingales, oracle inequalities have been proposed for Lasso penalized Cox models, see Huang et al. (2013). Some statistical models, such as the Ising model involving Markov's chains. Miaojobedow and Rejchel (2018) applied Hoeffding's inequality for Markov's chains to deal with this difficulty, see Fan et al. (2021) for a review. In time series analysis, Xie and Xiao (2018) studies the square-root Lasso method for HD linear models with  $\alpha$ ,  $\rho$ ,  $\phi$ -mixing or  $m$ -dependent errors. The Hoeffding's and Bernstein's CIs for weakly dependent summations can be found in Bosq (1998). Via sub-Weibull concentrations under  $\beta$ -mixing, non-asymptotic inequalities for estimation errors, and the prediction errors are obtained by Wong et al. (2020) for the Lasso-regularized sparse VAR model with sub-Weibull innovations. U-Statistic is another dependent sum, and Example 2.4 provides a concentration result by McDiarmid's inequality. Borovskikh (1996) introduces the concentration for the Banach-valued U-statistics.

In non-parametric regressions, the corresponding score functions may be r.vs in Banach (or Hilbert) space; see the monographs Ledoux and Talagrand (1991), Yurinsky (1995) for introductions. Exponential tail bounds for Banach- or Hilbert-valued r.vs are indispensable for deriving sharp oracle inequalities of the error bounds, see Zhang (2005), Zhang and Lei (2023). Recently, Banach-valued CIs are applied to conceive non-asymptotic hypothesis testing for non-parametric regressions, see Yang et al. (2020). To extend the empirical covariance matrices from finite to infinite dimension, the sample covariance operator is treated as a random element in Banach spaces. The concentrations of empirical covariance operator also have been raised attention in kernel principal components analysis, and functional data analysis, see Rosasco et al. (2010), Bunea and Xiao (2015).

Testing hypotheses on the regression coefficients are a necessity in measuring the effects of covariates on the certain response variables. Scientists are interested in testing the significance of a large number of covariates simultaneously. From this backgrounds, Zhong and Chen (2011) proposed simultaneous tests for coefficients in HD linear models under the "large  $p$ , small  $n$ " situations by U-statistics motivated by Chen and Qin (2010). However, their HD tests are asymptotical without a non-asymptotic guarantee. Motivated by Arlot et al. (2010), Zhu and Bradic (2018) invents a new methodology for testing the linearity hypothesis in HD linear models, and the test they proposed does not impose any restriction of model sparsity. Based on the concentration of Lipschitz functions of Gaussian distributions or strongly log-concave distribution, Zhu (2018) developed a new concentration-based test in HD regressions. Recently, Wang et al. (2021) studied non-asymptotical two-sample testing using Projected Wasserstein Distance, via McDiarmid's inequality.

In future, it would be essential and practical to study the estimator for the subexponential, sub-Gaussian, sub-Weibull and GBO norms as the unknown parameters when constructing non-asymptotical and data-driven confidence intervals; see Wang et al. (2020); Zhang et al. (2023); Zhou et al. (2025).
